The Commentary on the King of Tantras, the Glorious Catuṣpīṭha, called Smṛtinibandha (Śrīcatuṣpīṭhatantrarājasya Smṛtinibandha nāma Ṭīkā)

The Commentary on the King of Tantras, the Glorious Catuṣpīṭha, called Smṛtinibandha, or The Cause of Recollection, known in Sanskrit as Śrīcatuṣpīṭhatantrarājasya Smṛtinibandha nāma Ṭīkā and in Tibetan as Gyü kyi Gyalpo Pal Den Shipé Drelpa Drenpé Gyutsen, is a detailed Indian commentary on the Catuṣpīṭha Tantra, a yoginī tantra whose name means "the Four Seats." Composed by the great master Bhavabhadra, it opens with homage to Avalokiteśvara and verses praising the tantra as a grove of ripened fruit and a source of many nectars, and then works through the root text passage by passage, explaining its seed syllables, visualizations of deities and maṇḍalas, practices with the winds and the drops within the body, mantra recitation, rites, and the yoga of emptiness. Its closing sections treat the Secret Seat, the visualization of the body as a stūpa, the herbal diet of the practitioner in retreat, and the secrecy with which the tantra is to be guarded. It is Toh 1607 in the Tengyur, where it belongs among the tantra commentaries, and it was translated into Tibetan by the Indian paṇḍita Gayadhara together with the Tibetan translator Gö Lhetsé.

In the language of India: śrī caturapīṭha tantrarājasya smṛtinibandha nāma ṭīkā. In the language of Tibet: The Commentary on the King of Tantras, the Glorious Catuṣpīṭha, called The Cause of Recollection.

Homage to the Great Compassionate One, the noble Avalokiteśvara.

Where everywhere flowers and fruits of every form ripen all at once,
where fruit trees of pure splendor, most fragrant and full of flavor, grow,
like a forest grove made lovely by delightful shade, is the Catuṣpīṭha:
to what is understood through devotion to the Sage's well-arranged words, I bow.

Although here and there its qualities are not spelled out,
elsewhere its qualities are spelled out in full on every side,
so the Catuṣpīṭha Tantra, source of many nectars,
is like the lotus stalk and the lotus root.
Those with the power to taste the nectar of the gods
who dwell upon the golden mountain
have the ability, should they wish, to make its meaning clear;
but it is not the sphere of one like me, who seem so unlike them.
Just as a deer has no power to win by force
the jewel of an elephant it longs to seize,
so I, amid the dust of those afflictions,
am like one who wanders cropping grass.
And yet, just as iron, by the power of an elixir,
becomes soft enough for people to eat,
so, relying on the words of my guru,
I have the power to analyze, and here I set the meaning straight.
Since the minds of beings are various and discordant,
no one at all is able to please them all.
I have made a cause of recollection
of meanings that accord with the instruction;
as for words of prosody, gender, case and the like,
for people who delight in outward conventions,
may the intelligent, who would adorn the meanings of this precious tantra,
complete these for themselves.

Here, this tantra was extracted from the Glorious Catuṣpīṭha Tantra in twelve thousand verses, which is the essence of the tantra in eighteen hundred thousand verses. That is why the compiler did not set down at the beginning of this tantra words such as "Thus have I heard," for they are stated in that larger tantra itself. Therefore the words beginning "of the yoginīs" are to be construed as follows:

The Saṃvara of the net of yoginīs,
the Lord of wisdom, possessor of emptiness,
the Omniscient One, spoke thus.

Here "thus" is the word that takes in the whole tantra, in its nature as what is expressed and what expresses it, up to the end, "they praised what the Blessed One had spoken." "Spoke" means that he spoke the tantra without error, that is, he taught it and made it clear just as it is. With this the tantra that expresses is indicated. With "the characteristics of teaching the secret" and so on, what is expressed is taught; and by these it should be seen that the relation between what expresses and what is expressed is also taught. Here the purpose is to become manifest as the deity of bliss, and its intent, it should be understood, is to take beings into one's care.

If one asks how he taught without error, the text says "the Omniscient One" and so on. Because he knows all things just as they are, in their manner of being impermanent and so forth, he is omniscient, that is, the Blessed One. This states the excellence of the teacher. His distinction is stated in "of the yoginīs" and so on. Yoga is the gnosis in which method and wisdom are not two. Here wisdom is the absence of intrinsic nature, and method is perfectly pure compassion. As it is said:

The union of method and wisdom
is what is called yoga.
What is nonentity is wisdom;
the characteristic of entity is method.

Those who possess that yoga are the yoginīs. The net is their perfectly pure emanations. In "saṃvara," saṃ is all the sentient beings of saṃsāra, and vara is so called because the yoginīs restrain them and protect them completely.

In "the Lord of wisdom, possessor of emptiness," wisdom is the knowing of what is to be known. The possessors of emptiness are the buddhas. Since the great Vajradhara, whose essential nature is great merit, gives birth to all the buddhas, he is their lord. As it is also said:

The glorious one, the self of all the buddhas,
the state of the great Vajradhara,
is the one who brings forth all the buddhas;
it is the very nature of reality of all phenomena.

So he is the progenitor of all the buddhas.

By way of showing the distinction of the Blessed One, the excellence of the place is stated: "while dwelling in the pure abode." Here the pure ones are the gods. Because it guards and protects them, it is their abode, Mount Meru. "Dwelling" means that he resided there; the sense is that he dwelt within a multistoried pavilion upon Meru.

Here, "the Omniscient One" states the excellence of his own aim. "The Lord of wisdom, possessor of emptiness" states the excellence of his mindstream. "While dwelling in the pure abode" states the excellence of the aim of others. "The Saṃvara of the net of yoginīs" states the excellence of the tantra. By these it is established that he taught without error, for the excellences of what is to be taught, the teaching, the teacher and the place are stated in order.

To show also the excellence of the retinue there, the text says "the one with trained qualities" and so on. "The one with trained qualities" is Maitreya. "The unlovely one" is Vajrapāṇi. "The pendant one" is Samantabhadra. "The three-necked one" is Ākāśagarbha. "Meru" is Sarvanivaraṇaviṣkambhin. "The Meru-crested one" is Kṣitigarbha. "Lotus" is Lokeśvara. "Broad lotus" is Mañjuśrī. These eight are named because they are the chief bodhisattvas. The "eighty koṭis of lords of yoga" are the bodhisattvas headed by these chiefs. Among so many, he looked at Vajrapāṇi and smiled. A smile is a slight laugh accompanied by the light of the teeth, and its subject, "the Omniscient One," is carried over from before.

Why did he do so? Because he knew the minds of others, that the Blessed One's retinue wished to hear the Glorious Catuṣpīṭha, and he understood that Vajrapāṇi was able to compile it. Thinking, "I shall have him make the request of me," he urged him on by looking at him, and by smiling he showed that he was well pleased.

To show that Vajrapāṇi then set about making the request, the text says "as soon as he had smiled." "As soon as" means without interval. He is Vajrapāṇi because he holds a vajra in his hand. "From his seat" means from the moon maṇḍala. "One shoulder" is said of what is one side of the body. "Upper robe draped" means that his upper garment was draped. "On the ground" means without a cushion. "Palms joined" means that the palms of his two hands were placed evenly together and pressed face to face. By these, the proper manner is shown to those who will make requests in the future.

Then the request he made is stated, in the words beginning "secret." "Secret" means hidden, that is, the gnosis of the mudrā, the pledges and so forth. "Union" means the rites of pacifying, increasing and so forth, and the practices with water and the like. The "characteristics" of the secret and of union are their intrinsic nature. In "lord of wisdom," wisdom is the mirrorlike wisdom and the other wisdoms, and their lord is the teacher, Vajradhara. Or else, the wisdoms are Vairocana and the others, since they are the nature of the mirrorlike wisdom and the rest, and the lord is their master, since he is their progenitor. With this the Blessed One is addressed, and this is to be connected with what follows.

"Earth" is the ground. "Space" is what gives room. Putting a short vowel in place of a long one shows that there is no concern for what is or is not proper speech, because these are mere designations. "Water" and the rest are easy to understand. In "out-flowing" and the rest, out-flowing is going outward and in-flowing is coming inward; these are taught in the terminology of the Dharma. "Breath" is the wind of the nostrils.

"How should the meditation be grasped?" means, "What is the nature of the meditation on the wind that goes and comes?"

"The protectors of the land, the first gods,
and their maṇḍalas: how are they?"

Here "the land" is the body. Those who protect it are the protectors of the land, the tathāgatas Vairocana and the others. They are "the first gods" because they make the welfare of beings clear and roam about, accomplishing and emanating various forms such as Vajraḍākinī. The "maṇḍala" is their accomplishment: the welfare of beings is the essence, the maṇḍa, and because they take up and hold that fruit, it is so called. Or else, the protectors of the land are the field protectors. They themselves are the first gods, and their maṇḍala is they together with the members of their maṇḍalas. Or else, the protectors of the land are the aggregates, elements and sense sources. Because these are the nature of Vairocana and the others, they are first, that is, gods.

"Wisdom, consciousness, of the body:
how are action and the rest applied?"

Here wisdom is the stable and the unstable, the possible and the impossible, and so forth. Consciousness is life, and "of the body" means of a body endowed with these. "Action" is acting well and so forth, and "applied" means applied to that.

The words beginning "liberating from saṃsāra" give the answer. "Liberating from saṃsāra" means carrying across to the far shore of saṃsāra, that is, to awakening. Because this is what signifies it, "the very thing" is the reality of the wind. "As it is" means without error. "Vajra" is Vajrapāṇi. The construction is: "O Vajrapāṇi, listen to the reality of the wind, just as it is, by which one is carried to the far shore of saṃsāra."

That very thing is stated:

"All the actions of breath and the rest
are applied in the action of all who have wisdom."

This is connected with "listen." "Action" is the activity of the breath; it is reality. "And the rest" takes in everything up to "the supreme" and "the chief." "All who have wisdom" are all living creatures. "Applied" means that it goes into application. If one asks what that is: "in the action," that is, by the action. Here the third case is not made explicit, and the meaning is "by the action of the application of Mahendra and the others."

The action of the breath is stated:

"It should be counted as twenty thousand,
one thousand and six hundred;
by the breaths of sixty nāḍikās
it is known as a day and night."

Here "twenty thousand" is two ten-thousands. "One thousand" is a single thousand. "Six hundred" is six hundreds. "Counted" means that they are reckoned. If one asks where, it says "sixty nāḍikās." A nāḍikā is a daṇḍa. "Sixty" is six tens, and they are reckoned in these. "The breaths" are the twenty-one thousand together with the six hundred just mentioned. "It is known as a day and night" means that the whole number of breaths in a day and night passes by that count.

"The out-flowing and in-flowing breaths
should be counted by the number of the out-flowing."

Here the out-flowing breath is the wind going outward, and the in-flowing is its reverse. "The breaths" means of the two, the out-flowing and the in-flowing breaths. In "should be counted by the number of the out-flowing," "number" is the counting and "counted" means marked: one should mark them by taking the number of the winds going outward.

If one asks how this is to be done:

"The intelligent one should make the two breaths,
the in-flowing, dissolve into the out-flowing."

Here "the intelligent one" means the one endowed with understanding, and it addresses Vajrapāṇi. "The two breaths" are the out-flowing and the in-flowing. "The in-flowing dissolving into the out-flowing" means that the in-flowing dissolves into the out-flowing; the meaning is that they become one.

"Likewise, on twelve places
the outer marks should be set down."

Here the outer marks are lines other than the action of the breath; a mark is a line. "On twelve places" means on twelve squares.

That very thing is stated:

"According to the lunar day and its mark,
set them down in the twelve squares."

The meaning is that in those twelve squares one writes the name of each lunar day, from the first day on which the breaths enter up to the twelfth day, together with its mark.

"With great effort, in the twelve in sequence,
set down, from the east, the middle spring month and the rest."

Here "with great effort" means by the yogin with supreme devotion. "In sequence" means following the sequence of the months, that is, in the order of the middle spring month, the last spring month and so forth. "East" is the eastern section of squares; drawing them counterclockwise in the squares of the east, the northeast and so on is the "setting down." By the word "effort," it should be understood that the zodiac signs, Aries and the rest, are also meant.

The drawing itself is stated with the words beginning "Rohita." The lunar days beginning with the first, the individual months beginning with the middle spring month, and the zodiac signs beginning with Aries are called, in order, by the names Rohita and the rest. "The twelve lords of places": the places are the twelve places, that is, the squares, and their lords are Rohita and the others. Within a single day and night these are to become twelve, beginning with the rising of Rohita. Among them, Makara is like Rohita, of three units. Mohita and Kurma are of four. Bhadrā and Vṛṣabha are of five. Raṇḍa and Kama are also of five. Vidrika and Vyākuli are of six. Mikira and Svapna are of seven. Thus the three nāḍikās of dawn are Rohita. After that, four are Mohita. After that, five are Bhadrā. After that, the same number are Vṛṣabha. After that, four are Kurma. After that, three are Makara. After that, five are Raṇḍa. After that, seven are Mikira. After that, six are Vidrika. After that, the same number are Vyākuli. After that, seven are Svapna. After that, five are Kama. These are the sixty nāḍikās of one day and night. Moreover, when the nāḍikās of the lunar day, the month and the zodiac sign coincide, it is as just stated; otherwise it is otherwise.

A summary of the arrangement is stated:

"These are to be set out in order
in the squares beginning with the east."

The meaning is that by these, Rohita and the rest, the marks are to be set out in order, as stated before. However, it should be understood that in the months beginning with the last spring month, the first lunar day and the rest revolve through their separate names. The first lunar day of the last spring month is Mohita, and in the same way, up to the twelfth, one applies them so that they end with Rohita. The first lunar day of the first summer month is Bhadrā, and in the same way the twelfth is Mohita; and so on, applying them separately up to the middle spring month.

In the passage beginning "Rohita," "fortunate" means looked upon with joy by people.

"Of malicious mind, and sickly":

Here "malicious mind" means thinking to harm, not to help. "Sickly" means having weak digestive heat.

"Always a leader in the borderlands":

This means that in a good borderland country he becomes a king or the like.

"Always delighting in sexual union":

This means that, for the sake of pleasure, he engages without examining what is a proper place and what is not.

"Everywhere speaking pleasantly":

Here "everywhere" means always, and "speaking pleasantly" means without harsh words.

"In Rohita, worshipping the gods":

This means that the mind of one born in Rohita turns to worshipping the gods at the very time of Rohita. This linking of time and birth should be seen in what follows as well.

"Likewise, he lives seventy years":

This means that the end of his life is fixed at seventy.

"If wealth is given out in Rohita,
all one's friends will turn away":

Here "wealth given out" means lent out at interest and so forth. "Friends turning away" means falling out over that very debt.

"If the body is wounded in Rohita":

This means a wound cut by a weapon.

"One will meet with glory everywhere":

This means drawing in the good fortune of others.

"If one settles in a place in Rohita,
one will flourish with friends and wealth":

This means that in the place one settles at the time of Rohita, wealth, servants and the like increase.

"If one sets out on a journey in Rohita,
all one's wishes will be fulfilled":

The meaning is that, since the aim for which one set out on the road will be accomplished, one should therefore go.

"Taking a bride, rites of fire,
and rites of pacifying should be performed":

Here "rites of fire" are rites such as the fire offering, and "rites of pacifying" means bearing fruit without obstacles. Some say that, in the work of transmuting substances, cooking, burning and the like are the rites of fire.

"The concerns of Mohita
should be known by the reckoning itself":

Here "concerns" means the collection of matters. "Reckoning" is the calculation by means of the twelve squares drawn; by that it is to be known.

"Of small mind" means characterized by a petty mind.

"Always delighting in sexual union" means always pursuing the means of union.

"Quarrelsome" means much given to fighting and dispute.

"Wrathful, proud, without courage,
doing evil, of bad disposition":

Here "wrathful" means fierce. "Proud" means conceited. "Doing evil" means committing killing and the like. "Bad disposition" means short-tempered, or else unpleasing in bodily appearance.

"Idle in speech and harsh in speech":

This is said of those who talk much to no purpose and who speak roughly.

"He will surely die by a weapon":

This is easy to understand. Some texts read, "he is always thinking of dying," meaning that he thinks of killing himself with any weapon or other means, and dies by it.

"Or else a life of eighty
and four is well known":

This means that, or else, his life lasts long, to eighty-four rather than sixty-five.

"He abandons suitable places
and exerts himself in unsuitable places":

Here a suitable place is a proper place, that is, one fit for the deed.

An unsuitable place is an improper place, that is, one not fit for the deed. The rest is easy to understand.

"If a limb of the body is wounded,
he will meet with the Lord of Death":

This is the explanation of "he will surely die by a weapon."

"If he gives out even a little wealth,
what he gives out will not come back":

This means that the loans and the like that this person gives out will not be recovered.

In the passage beginning "Bhadrā":

"Eating good food":

This means living by fields and the like.

"He will do good works":

This is because he is devoted to rites of pacifying and the like.

"He will live a hundred years":

This is easy to understand.

"Poor, and practicing the Dharma":

Here "poor" means having little wealth. The Dharma is the cause of the higher realms and of liberation, that is, the Dharma of merit.

"His body content with one wife,
he will be a leader who speaks well":

Here "content with one wife" means that one wife is enough for him. "Good" means fine, that is, fine in body and speech. "Leader" means that people do as he says.

"Knowing all the crafts and the times":

Here the crafts are drawing pictures and the like, and the word "all" shows that they are many. "The times" means the sciences, and science means knowledge. If one asks what those sciences are, they are what is spoken of as follows:

The four Vedas and Mīmāṃsā,
the six treatises that expand on method,
law, scripture and the ancient histories,
which are spoken of in three kinds,
the science of healing and the science of archery,
music, and the three kinds,
and the common treatise on wealth;
training, ritual, grammar,
etymology, prosody and the science of the stars,
which are called the six limbs;
training is the taming of elephants and the like,
and ritual is the treatises of tradition and the like.
These, gathered together in brief,
are the eighteen fields of knowledge.

"Always fond of his own kin":

This means that he takes special delight in his own relatives.

"Of the kind that metes out punishment":

This means that he draws in wealth by meting out punishments; or else, that he lives by the great dignity of his family.

"Likewise, his body will die in water":

This is easy to understand.

In the passage beginning "Vṛṣabha":

"Diligent and brave against enemies":

This means that by diligence he overpowers his enemies. The rest is easy to understand.

"Always having many relatives":

This means having many ties of family, kin and in-laws.

"He destroys his father's life":

This means that as soon as he is born he destroys his father's life, that is, the father dies. The rest is easy to understand.

"Lazy and speaking idly":

This means talking a great deal and being in the grip of laziness.

"Malicious, of bad deeds and disposition":

The meaning of this has already been explained.

"He will die by a weapon,
or else by a venomous creature":

This means that his life is destroyed by weapons, or by the venom of snakes and the like.

In the passage beginning "Kurma":

"He always lives full of worries":

This means that he always lives thinking about the affairs of others. Some texts read, "he lives without thought," in which case it is the opposite of the former.

"Angered by little and pleased by little," and so on, is easy to understand. "Very violent" means fierce. "Exceedingly angry" means angry where there is no cause.

"Rich, and delighting in giving everything":

This means that he possesses goods and gives away what he possesses.

"His children and wife will die":

This means that his children and wife do not live long. The rest is easy to understand.

"Likewise, he will die in his own land":

This means that he dies while thinking well.

In the passage beginning "Makara":

"His life is nine, three and sixty":

Here nine, three and sixty make seventy-two. Some texts read "it is ninety and six," that is, ninety-six.

"Perfect in moral discipline and endowed with merit":

Here moral discipline is threefold: engaging in virtue, turning away from nonvirtue, and working for the benefit of others. Merit is giving, making offerings and the like.

"He keeps discipline and good conduct":

Here discipline means restraint of the senses, and conduct means the customs of people. The rest is easy to understand.

"He has one wife, most illustrious":

This is said of one who has a single, especially excellent wife.

"His mind is always tender":

Here "tender" means pained: when he sees the suffering of others, he is brought low as though bound himself.

"Among his relatives, he alone is chief":

This means that he shares his wealth in common with his relatives. The rest is easy to understand.

"A life of fifty-five years,
or else sixty or thirty":

Here "sixty or thirty" is the wording of scripture; according to the oral instruction, with the word for ten placed after, it is to be understood as sixty-three. By these, the two kinds of his shortest lifespan are stated.

In the passage beginning "Raṇḍa":

"Always of evil mind":

This means having a mind set on committing the deeds of immediate retribution and the like.

"Always poor, and a flatterer":

Here a flatterer is one who speaks smooth words. The rest is easy to understand.

"He does not trust others":

This means that he does not rely on them.

"Knowing all arts of artifice and crafts":

Here the arts of artifice are making imitation pearls, setting gems and the like. "And so forth" includes riding elephants and the like. The rest has already been explained.

In the passage beginning "Mikira":

"By Mikira" is to be understood in the second case, as "in Mikira," for it is said: "Time, road and close connection are expressed with the case of the object."

"Knowing the Dharma, and his family is high":

Here "high" means praised: it is said of one who advances his own family higher and higher. "Treatises on conduct" are treatises on royal law and the like. "Crafts" are dancing and the like. "Sciences" are archery and the like. The rest is easy to understand.

"All his tasks will be swiftly accomplished":

This means that he accomplishes what is needed without difficulty and without delay. The rest is easy to understand.

On "Vidrika" and the rest:

"Likewise he is chief in his own household":

He is chief because he has no father or mother.

"His work and worries are small":

Here "small" means low: he does the work of a low family. Some texts read, "Some have work of their own mind," meaning that they do their tasks just as they themselves intend.

"He is the very chief of troops":

This means he becomes a general. The rest is easy to understand.

"He is endowed with kindness toward the miserable and the destitute":

Here "miserable" means those who suffer, and "destitute" means those who are low in family and the like.

On "Vyākuli" and the rest: "Small-minded" means of inferior intelligence. "Speaking badly" means speaking out of place, or delighting in proclaiming faults. "Weak in wisdom" means foolish. The rest has already been explained.

"Very rich in wealth and cattle":

This means he has much wealth and many cattle.

"In his household, three wives and more":

This means there are no fewer than three wives in a single household. The rest is easy to understand.

"He dies together with it, through the cause of trade":

Here "together" means together with his wealth: he will perish along with his wealth. "Dies through the cause of trade" means that he dies in the very marketplace, or dies through the very cause of pursuing wealth.

"The signs for undertaking an application
should be counted by one possessed of wisdom":

Here "application" means the time periods of Rohita and the rest. The "signs" are what is drawn just as it was taught. If one asks where they are to be known, the text says "should be counted": in the place where the twelve squares are drawn.

On "Svabna" and the rest:

"He is the support of the protectorless":

This means that for those who have no father, mother, and so on, he does what they would do. The rest is easy to understand.

"And thinking oneself a lion":

This means having the arrogance that thinks, "There is no one at all better than me; I alone am excellent." The rest is easy to understand.

"And living by depending on others":

This means living by work such as serving in a king's retinue. The rest is easy to understand.

On "Kama" and the rest:

"Endowed with wealth, grain, and villages,
livestock, and mounts such as horses":

This is said of one who enjoys wealth such as gold and heaps of grain such as rice, and who, having been appointed chief over the townspeople, keeps many livestock and the like. The rest is easy to understand.

Now the summary of the arrangement in the twelve squares is taught:

"The signs of the applications that have been taught:
there is no doubt in what I have explained."

Here "application" means Rohita and the rest. "Sign" means what is to be indicated; it is to be known by each of them in its order. "There is no doubt in what I have explained" is the command of Vajradhara: "Do not be in two minds about the words just as I have spoken them."

Now, so that unclear matters such as the stars may be known by way of their characteristics, "Rohita" and the rest is taught. "Rohita" means a person born in Rohita. The word "indeed" is restrictive, applying to worshipping the gods. By this it shows that a person born in Rohita will certainly worship the gods from childhood onward. "In Mohita" means a person born in Mohita. "Greedy for wealth" means that he craves wealth and will obtain wealth.

"Bhadra eats good food":

This means that it is the nature of a person born from Bhadra certainly to obtain good food.

"In Vṛṣabha, much anger":

This means that the definite nature of one born in Vṛṣabha is to be exceedingly angry. "Kurma" and the rest are easy to understand. "Speaks at length" means he talks a great deal.

"In Raṇḍa, one of sinful mind;
Mikira relies on sexual union":

This too is easy to understand.

"Vidrika sleeps":

This means that the nature of a person born in Vidrika is to sleep a great deal.

"Vyākuli talks deliriously":

This means that the nature of a person born from Vyākuli is to talk deliriously as soon as he falls asleep.

"In Svabna, one thinks of oneself":

This means one whose nature delights in reflection.

On "Kama" and the rest: the nature of a person born in Kama is to delight in music such as song.

Here, in the three water hours of Aries are Aśvinī, Bharaṇī, and one quarter of Kṛttikā, making nine quarters. After that, in the four water hours of Taurus are three quarters of Kṛttikā, Rohiṇī, and half of Mṛgaśiras. After that, in the five water hours of Gemini are half of Mṛgaśiras, Ārdrā, and three quarters of Punarvasu. After that, in the same number of water hours of Cancer are one quarter of Punarvasu, Puṣya, and Āśleṣā. After that, in the four water hours of Leo are Maghā, Pūrvaphalgunī, and one quarter of Uttaraphalgunī. After that, in the three water hours of Virgo are three quarters of Uttaraphalgunī, Hasta, and half of Citrā. After that, in the five water hours of Libra are half of Citrā, Viśākhā, and three quarters of Svātī. After that, in the seven water hours of Scorpio are one quarter of Viśākhā, Anurādhā, and Jyeṣṭhā. After that, in the six water hours of Sagittarius are Mūla, Pūrvāṣāḍhā, and one quarter of Uttarāṣāḍhā. After that, in the same number of water hours of Capricorn are three quarters of Uttarāṣāḍhā, Śravaṇa, and half of Śatabhiṣā. After that, in the seven water hours of Aquarius are half of Śatabhiṣā, Dhaniṣṭhā, and three parts of Pūrvabhādrapadā. Then, in the five water hours of Pisces are one part of Pūrvabhādrapadā, Uttarabhādrapadā, and Revatī. Thus the suns of Aries and the rest should be seen as occupying three lunar mansions each, not one each.

To teach that, in the applications of three water hours and so on designated by the words Rohita and the rest, one should perform the rites of pacifying and the others in their corresponding order, the text says, "In Rohita, the rite of pacifying," and so on. This is easy to understand.

"The bull of the goats is accomplished:
the application of nectar will arise":

Here "goats" means the horned ones. "Bull" means chief: these are Rohita, Makara, Raṇḍa, and Mikira. They are bulls in relation to the horned ones, Bhadrā and the rest. "Accomplished" means that nothing remains to be done: it is like one who has already died. To show that very fact that nothing remains to be done, the text says, "the application of nectar will arise." Or else these are words of reproach.

To teach the rules concerning the time for drawing the wheel and so on, the text says "East" and the rest. Rohita is placed in the eastern compartment, and "and the rest" means that Mohita and the others are to be placed in the compartment of Īśāna and the others. "In the morning" means at the time of dawn. "Calculate" means one should count.

To explain the teaching "If wealth is given out in Rohita," the text says "In Rohita" and the rest. The meaning is that if wealth given out in Rohita comes back, riches and plenty will come back with it.

To explain the teaching about Mohita, "If one gives out even a little wealth," and the rest, the text says "Mohita" and the rest. Wealth gathered in Mohita cannot be stopped by others; that is, they are unable to harm it.

"If one undertakes a house in Bhadrā,
one will meet with glory":

This means that if one builds a house in the application of Bhadrā, one will be endowed with glory.

"Through the yoga of the deity in Vṛṣa,
mantras will be accomplished in every way":

This means that if, in the application of Vṛṣa, one undertakes a deity, that is, an image of the Teacher and the like, and undertakes mantra, that is, the recitation of the Teacher's mantra, it will be accomplished in every way, that is, perfectly from every side.

"By the application of Kurma, take a bride;
they will never be parted":

This means that if one takes a bride in the application, that is, the time period, of Kurma, they will be joyful and affectionate at all times and will not be parted.

"If one sows a field in Makara,
without doubt the crops will increase":

This means that if one begins sowing seed in the application of Makara, the crops will increase and ripen.

"If one goes to a woman in Raṇḍa,
even when he dies she will not let him go":

This means that if one engages in passion with a woman in the application of Raṇḍa, that woman will be his in another life as well.

"If one trades in Mikira,
it seems like taking back what was deposited":

This means that wealth gained by trading in the application of Mikira will seem like taking back wealth deposited in a former life or the like.

"Whatever should always be firmly established,
and the like, should be taken up in Vidrika":

The meaning is that whatever is taken up in the application of Vidrika will remain for a long time.

"If a guest sets out in Vyākuli,
he reaches his destination without harm":

This means that if, in the application of Vyākuli, one sets out as a traveler on the road to a border land, one reaches one's destination there without harm from illness, robbers, and the like.

"Through Svapna, everything, in every way,
will certainly always come back":

This means that whoever sets out in the application of Svapna will come back again.

"If wealth is given out in Kama,
what is given out will not come back":

This is easy to understand.

To teach that nāgas too are designated by the words Rohita and the rest, the text says "Rohita is the son of Vāsuki" and the rest. This is easy to understand.

"These, in the order of the applications of poison,
should be known in their particulars":

Here "application" means Rohita and the rest. The "order of poison" is just what "these" refers to: Vāsuki and the rest. "Should be known in their particulars" means that one should apply them and know each separately, or else know the distinction between those that can be cured and those that cannot. It is for that very reason that the text says "the bull of the horned ones" and so on, which has already been explained.

"One who has been seized by fangs":

This means a being seized by the fangs of some nāga. It connects with the earlier words, "should be known in the order of the applications of poison."

"Has met with the Lord of Death":

The meaning is that one should determine, in the way that is explained below, whether or not the time has come; and if, when the time has come, the person has been seized by four fangs, then one turns back.

"The applications for drawing out poison:
one should apply the poison to another":

Here "one should apply the poison to another" refers to the poisons of applications whose time has not come. "The applications for drawing out poison" connects with "should be done." The meaning is that by the application explained below, one removes the poison from someone seized by the eight fangs whose time of death has not come: having determined from the messenger, the place of the bite, the lunar date, the lunar mansion, and the hour whether or not the time has come, one applies it.

Now, to teach the fixed number of water hours of the day and night in each month, the text says "the signs of time" and the rest.

"Just as the signs of time are,
so one should do it in sequence, in order":

The meaning is that in the twelve squares, one draws the sign of each month, beginning with the middle month of spring, in whichever square it falls, going counterclockwise, and calculates.

"In order, beginning with the middle month of spring":

This means that when the sun goes north, one should begin from the new moon of the middle month of spring. "At the time of going" means the time of its going. "North": of the two, going south and going north, going north is taught as the principal one, because it is the beginning of the year.

"Just as the time is, so the signs
should be applied step by step":

This means that for the days of each month, one should know them in the twelve squares according to the order counted in worldly calculation.

"For the water hours of the day, each,
the wise should take ten twice, and four;
for the night, take six, and ten thrice:
so the wise should set out the water hours."

When the sun goes north, beginning from the new moon, for each day of the middle month of spring one multiplies ten by two, which is twenty, and adds four more, giving twenty-four water hours. "The wise" connects with "should set out." "Night" here also connects with "each." "Ten thrice" means multiplying by three tens, which is thirty. "Six" means another six, so that there are thirty-six water hours. In this way one should apply it to all of them and complete it. The condensed meaning is this: the days of the middle month of spring have twenty-four water hours, and the nights have thirty-six water hours.

Having taught about the month that has reached the first square, in order to teach also about the month of the second square, the text says "In the second square" and the rest. "In the second square" refers to the last month of spring, which has reached the second square.

"Day" refers to the daytime portion. "Six and two tens multiplied" means twenty-six. "Night" refers to the night portion. "Four and ten multiplied by three" means thirty-four.

To teach about the first month of summer, the text says the passage beginning "Eight and." In it, "Eight and ten multiplied by two" means twenty-eight. "Day" is to be joined with "water hours." "Two and ten multiplied by three" means thirty-two.

In the passage
"Exactly thirty water hours:
in the middle, at the viṣu, apply them to both,"
"the middle" is the middle of the six months. "Both" refers to both the portions of day and night. This equal assignment of thirty water hours each to both the day and the night portions is to be connected with the word "viṣu" (the equinox); it is that of the middle month of summer.

For the last month of summer the text says:
"Two at the end, ten made threefold,
are called the water hours of the day;
eight and ten made twofold
should be made the mark of its night."
Here "two at the end" means that two itself is the end. Of what? "Ten made threefold," that is, exactly thirty. "Eight and ten made twofold" means twenty-eight; the sense is "the water hours of the night."

To teach about the first month of autumn, the text says the passage beginning "Four and." In it, four and ten made threefold is thirty-four; these are the day's water hours of that month. "Six and ten multiplied by two" means twenty-six.

For the middle month of autumn the text says the passage beginning "Six and." In it, "Six and ten multiplied by three" means thirty-six. "Four and ten multiplied by two" means twenty-four. The rest is easy to understand.

In the passage
"Having explained the northern course,
the other period is likewise so called,"
the sense is this: having taught that the sun travels north during the six months, each made a month of thirty days, counted from the new moon of the middle month of spring, one should now understand that during the six months beginning from the new moon of the middle month of autumn the sun travels south.

For the last month of autumn the text says the passage beginning "Four and ten multiplied by three." In it, "three" is what supplements "ten." By it ten is multiplied into thirty, and joined with the other four it becomes thirty-four. "Day" refers to the daytime portion of that month. "Six and ten multiplied by two" means twenty-six. "Of the night" refers to the night portion.

For the first month of winter the text says the passage beginning "Two and ten multiplied by three." In it, two and ten multiplied threefold is thirty-two. In "Eight and ten multiplied by two," eight and ten multiplied twofold is twenty-eight.

For the middle month of winter the text says the passage beginning "Ten multiplied by three." "Viṣu" is to be understood as for the middle month of summer. "Of the day" is the daytime portion; "night" is the night portion. "Ten is to be multiplied by three" means exactly thirty; one should understand that both portions have thirty each.

For the last month of winter the text says the passage beginning "Eight and." In it, "Eight and ten multiplied by two" means twenty-eight. "Two and ten multiplied by three" means thirty-two.

For the first month of spring the text says the passage beginning "Six and." In it, "Six and ten multiplied by two" means twenty-six. "Four and ten multiplied by three" means thirty-four. The rest is easy to understand.

In order to sum up the water hours of a day and night of the middle month of spring, the text says the passage beginning
"With three hundred, fifty, and five twice
of breaths, the periods of a water hour pass."
"Three hundred" is three hundreds. "Fifty" is the number fifty. "Five twice" is ten, and ten with fifty becomes sixty. Breath is the movement of wind through the nostrils. Thus, with three hundred and sixty movements of breath, "the periods of a water hour pass," meaning that it amounts to one water hour.
"By the number of sixty water hours
one full day and night is declared"
is easy to understand. Some texts read "of three hundred and sixty"; there too the meaning is the same.

In the passage
"According to the sequence of the months,
make the marks in that same order,"
the sense is that, according to the sequence of the months, one should make the marks in the squares in order, three water hours and so on, and examine them.

In the passage
"When the water hours, breaths and the rest are complete,
except at the viṣu, there is gradual increase and decrease,"
the sense is that, from the water hours in which the breaths of the twelve months are fully complete, one should understand by the reasoning explained above also the subtle increases and decreases in the water hours of day and night of the remaining months, apart from the two months of the viṣu.

In the passage
"According to the distinction of action and non-action,
the breath should be taken in sequence,"
"the distinction of action and non-action" is the division into virtuous actions, such as pacifying, and non-virtuous ones, such as the fierce rites (abhicāra). "The breath should be taken in sequence" means that, just as wind and the others are applied, each should be taken separately in the sequence of the breaths. Here one should know that wind moves at six finger-widths, fire at four finger-widths, water at twelve finger-widths, and Mahendra at sixteen finger-widths.

In order to explain the brief indication "twenty thousand and" and so on, the text says the passage beginning "Kha and ra and so on." The words "and so on" are to be joined to both. It thus becomes "kha and so on": the twenty-four letters beginning with kha and the six letters beginning with ra are "kha and ra and so on." By this it teaches that one makes the marks of twenty-four and of six. "Seasons" means that one should take the marks of the six seasons; with twenty-four and two sets of six it becomes thirty-six. "It should be indicated by the two courses" means that dividing those same thirty-six marks into two equal parts gives eighteen each, so one should make the marks of two sets of eighteen.

"Both are multiplied by ten" means that each of those two is multiplied by ten and counted; this gives a hundred each, together with two sets of tens. Then the two hundreds are combined into one and the mark of two hundred is made; from the two sets of eight tens, the mark of ten and the mark of six are made separately, and so the mark of sixteen is made. By placing two zeros beside it, it becomes twenty-one thousand six hundred, and this becomes the mark of the number of water hours in a year.

"With that, possessed of the mark,
reckon the breaths of a day and night"
means that by that very mark of the number of the water hours of the year, the mark of the breaths of a day and night is also taught: it comes to twenty-one thousand six hundred.

Now, in order to teach the reckoning of the water day, the text says the passage beginning "Having taken all the lord of speech." Here "the lord of speech" is the letters of the questioner's words. "All" means counting through to the end. For example, if "ma" is spoken, one makes the mark of twenty-five, counting from ka through to it. In the same way it is to be applied to the other consonants. In order to teach that the vowel letters too are separated from the consonants and that such a mark is made of them, the text says, "Set down the mark of the vowels"; this is easy to understand.

Alternatively, "the lord of speech" is the questioner. What is "taken" is a flower: one writes, as before, the mark of however many letters there are in the name of the flower. If one asks what is to be done after that, it is to be connected with what comes below, "Having set it down sixfold," and so on. And what is to be done with that? The text says the passage beginning
"Having discarded the two neuters,
set down all the lords of the ground."
The two neuters are ṛ and ṝ, and ḷ and ḹ. "Discarded" means left out. "The ground" is the twelve squares. Its lords are the twelve vowel letters beginning with a. "Set down all" means set down in order. After that one should reckon according to the rite, and for that very reason the text says the passage beginning "If it falls on the turn of a," which is easy to understand.

"Just as by the first a,
so the last should be taken"
means that, just as the result of the letter a is understood, so too the result of the letter a should be understood.

In order to teach the continuation of "Having taken all the lord of speech," which was left off above, the text says the passage beginning "sixfold." "Having set it down sixfold" means that the mark of however many letters there were before is multiplied sixfold. "Make that former one threefold" means this: the number of letters of the words, however many there are, is what is called "the former one," and it is that which is to be multiplied threefold after that, not however much was obtained by multiplying it sixfold. "Having added three hundred and sixty" means that after that a further three hundred and sixty is added. If one asks what is to be done next, the text says, "Having divided it according to the ground." "The ground" is the twelve grounds. "Divided" means cut up, that is, it is reckoned by cutting it into twelves. In the passage beginning "the letter," the letter is a and the others. "The remainder" is what is left over of the number. The sense is that by the remainder, through the division of the number, one takes that letter, and virtue and non-virtue are indicated; one should examine them as stated before.

In order to teach divination, the text says the passage beginning "Alternatively":
"Alternatively, draw a crooked line,
and count from the first of the vowels."
The sense is this: with a fragment of skull empowered by the mantra of the wisdom ḍākinī, having recited the mantra once, one draws a line like the trail of an ox's urine, drawing it out as far as the mind wishes. Then, starting from the end of the line and going as far as its beginning, one counts its bends, and one should examine which of the letters beginning with a the last number falls on, for that is the result.

"Or else, having scattered flowers" means: or else, with an undistracted mind, one scatters flowers on the place drawn before, and
"In the same way the ground is to be indicated,"
that is, virtue and wrongdoing are to be indicated by whichever square it is.

"In the early morning, at the time of Rohita,
all truths should be examined"
means that if those calculations and divinations are examined at the conjunction of Rohita, all that is virtuous and non-virtuous will be true, that is, certain; so one should examine them then.

In "praised in the treatises of yoga," the treatises of yoga are the way of mantra. "Praised" means that those rites for examining virtue and wrongdoing are extolled by the buddhas.

In "from the section on the seat of the self," "self" is one's own body. Since it has become the abode of the reality of wind and the rest, it is the seat, that is, the basis. As for "section," since in this text the reality of wind and the rest are set out and made clear, this is a section.

From the Commentary on the Glorious Catuṣpīṭha called Smṛtinibandha, the first chapter, on the seat of the self.

Now, in order to teach the chapter on knowing the signs of death and then cheating it, the text says the passage beginning "That very wisdom." "That very wisdom," when particularized, is the removal of poison and so on.
"What are the signs of the body?"
means the signs that are omens of death appearing on the body.
"On what is that reality to be relied?"
means on what support the reality of mantra turns death back.
The reply is given: the text says the passage beginning "Those that show the signs of the body." "Those that show the signs of the body" are the things appearing on the body, such as the agitation of the breath, which show death; this is to be connected with "Listen!"
"It remains, having reached the time of death"
means it stays, having certainly reached the time of death; that is, it has come to that.
In "great vajra king," the vajra is Akṣobhya. He himself is the great king, because he is adorned with emanation bodies. The one to whom he belongs is also called by that name; it is a form of address. "Listen!" is an instruction to listen to what comes below.

The signs are taught in the passage beginning "If the breath is merely agitated." The agitation of the breath is its becoming other than its ordinary state, that is, its increase and decrease.
"The embodied one dies within six months"
is easy to understand. Alternatively, "agitation" means that the places of its entering, emerging and so on have become reversed.

If one asks by what kind of agitation, the text says the passage beginning
"If no fear at all has been seen."
"Some fear seen" means being made afraid, that is, frightened, by seeing in some place an ugly form. "Seen" is given as an example: being frightened by hearing a hostile sound and the like is also to be included. If, apart from such fear, the breath becomes agitated, then it shows with certainty that one will die; but it is not so with agitation of the breath produced otherwise, by fear and the like.

"If the border of the cheek splits open"
means: if suddenly the flesh at the border of the cheek is gone and it becomes a sunken line, then it shows that one will die in that very way. Here too it is to be connected with "if no fear at all has been seen."
"Then one has reached the time"
means that one has without doubt touched the domain of death.
"The breath breaks off and breaks off again"
is an illustration of agitation, that is, its becoming other than ordinary. If one asks when it is to be examined, the text says, "In the early morning, at the time of Rohita." The sense is that in the three water hours of dawn all the signs appearing on the body that are not produced by fear and the like are to be taken as certain.

"If the flesh between the nostrils is cut off"
means the flesh of the bridge of the nose is cut off: when the channel located in the nose is severed, then, because the flesh of the bridge of the nose is cut off, the nose becomes crooked.
"In seven days, without doubt"
means that in seven days one will die, without doubt.

In "If the water of the eyes is cut off," the water of the eyes is tears, and "cut off" means exhausted. When the channel located in the eyes is severed, the tears of the eyes become exhausted.
In "Likewise in five days one is cut off," "cut off" means that the life is exhausted and stops; that is, one dies after five days.

"Of the sign on the side of the cheek"
is the sign that appears on the side of the cheek: when the channel located on the side of the cheek is severed, then a long sunken line appears on the side of the cheek.
"One does not live beyond one day"
is easy to understand. If on that same side of the cheek a sunken line appears like the opening of a well, one will die in six months; this is to be connected with the preceding.

In "By the folding of the rim of the ear," the rim of the ear needs no explanation. "Folding" means breaking: when the channel located in the two ears is severed, the circle of the ear becomes broken.
"In five water hours, without doubt"
means that within five water hours one will die without doubt.

"Here, if there is a black spot on the tongue"
means: when the channel located in the tongue is severed, then here, that is, on the body, there is a black spot on the tongue; the tip of the tongue and so on appear black.
"The embodied one will die in two days"
is easy to understand.

In "When the upper and lower teeth are clenched," the clenching of the upper and lower teeth means that the two rows of teeth have no gap between them: when the channel located at the junction of the teeth is severed, the sign of clenched teeth appears.
"In three days one will meet the Lord of Death"
is easy to understand.

In "The channel on the outside of the side of the neck," when the channel located on the two sides of the neck deteriorates, then on the two sides of the neck there come to be high swellings; that is the sign.
"In half a month, without doubt"
means that within half a month one will die; it is said in order to show that there is no doubt.

In
"If the net of the chest collapses,
for half a month the physician cannot cure it,"
the collapse of the net of the chest means that a hollow appears in the chest: when the channel located in the net of the chest is severed, then the net of the chest has collapsed. "Half a month" means one dies in half a month. In order to show that there is no doubt, the text says, "the physician cannot cure it," which is easy to understand.

By
"Through the absence of blood in the nails,
the embodied one dies in eight days,"
the absence of blood in the nails means that they suddenly become white: when the channel located in the nails has been severed, then the nails suddenly appear white.
"The embodied one dies in eight days"
is easy to understand.

In "With the eight signs of death, together with," "eight" is the number eight. "Together with" means together with that one, making nine. Thus it shows that there are nine signs that are omens of death.
In "The arrival of the time of death should be recognized," "the arrival of the time" means remaining at the arrival of the time. Since the nine signs taught above appear on the body, it should be recognized that embodied beings will certainly die without doubt.

Having thus taught the signs of death, now, in order to teach the cheating of death, the text says the passage beginning "When the flesh between the nostrils is cut off." "When the flesh between the nostrils is cut off" refers to filling the severed channel of the nose.
In "One should press it with the wisdom seed," the wisdom seed is the seed of the wisdom ḍākinī, that is, hūṃ. "One should press it" means that by hūṃ, with a terrifying stream of nectar, the place is to be satisfied. Alternatively, one visualizes in that place the wisdom ḍākinī, arising from the transformation of hūṃ, with the form described below, and fills it. Thus one dies through the severing of a channel, and from filling it that is reversed. Therefore it teaches that in every case one should satisfy the place with the seed, or meditate on it in deity yoga.

In
"When the water of the eyes is cut off,
take the seed of the Vajra Lady,"
the cutting off of the water of the eyes means the time when the channel of the eyes, which it illustrates, is severed.
"Take the seed of the Vajra Lady"
means that in that place one should satisfy the body with the seed of Vajraḍākinī, the syllable suṃ, or with the body of Vajraḍākinī arising from its transformation, possessed of a dripping stream of nectar.

"On the sign on the side of the cheek,
one should press it with the seed of the Fierce Lady"
means that at the severed channel on the side of the cheek one should fill it with the seed of the Fierce Ḍākinī, the syllable kṣuṃ, or with the nectar of the body of the Fierce Ḍākinī arising from the transformation of the seed.

"When the rim of the ear collapses,
fill it with the seed of Vetālī"
means that, at the severed channel of the ear, one should fill that place with the seed of Vetālī, the syllable yuṃ, or with the nectar of Vetālī arising from the complete transformation of the seed.

"For this black spot on the tongue,
one should contemplate the seed of Caṇḍālī"
means that in that place where the channel of the tongue is severed one meditates on Caṇḍālī's syllable hūṃ, or on Caṇḍālī, together with nectar, arising from its complete transformation.

"When tooth and tooth are clenched,
strike with the seed of Siṃhinī"
means that, at the severed channel of the teeth, one strikes with Siṃhinī's seed smraṃ, or with the form of Siṃhinī, together with nectar, arising from its complete transformation; that is, one sets it there and meditates.

"The channel on the outside of the side of the neck:
one should strike with the seed of Vyāghrī"
means that in that place where the two channels on the sides of the neck are severed one meditates on Vyāghrī's seed hmraṃ, or on Vyāghrī, together with nectar, arising from its complete transformation.

"When the net of the chest collapses,
one should take the seed of Jambukī"
means that when the channel of the net of the chest is severed, one meditates there on Jambukī's seed, the syllable ymraṃ, or on the goddess Jambukī, together with nectar, arising from its complete transformation.

"When the nails of the hands are without blood,
take the seed of Ulūkī"
means that, at the severed channel located in the nails of the hands, one meditates there on Ulūkī's seed, the syllable yu kṣmra, or on the goddess Ulūkī, together with nectar, arising from its complete transformation.

In order to teach the summary, the text says:
"The limbs and signs that are omens of death
are guarded by setting down those seeds."
The sense is that, if the signs that are omens of death explained above appear on someone's body, one should guard against them by placing in those places, in the order explained above, the seeds, hūṃ and the rest, of "those," namely the wisdom ḍākinī and the others.

Now, in order to teach the protection of the outer place, the text says the passage beginning "From outside":
"From outside, the wisdom wheel:
by the rite of drawing it, guard the consciousness."
Here "from outside" means "other." The wisdom wheel is the wheel described below. Consciousness is the life of the one to be accomplished. "Guard" is easy to understand. In another text the reading is "I shall explain the protection in another way." There "other" means by another method. "Protection" means the life remaining; the rest is as before.

In order to teach the rite of protection itself, the text says the passage beginning "Unfired." The two unfired clay bowls are two clay bowls not baked in fire.
"With saffron and gorocana"
means with either saffron or gorocana, or else with the two mixed. "Draw" is to be connected with the earlier word "wheel."
"The lower part has nine squares"
means that on the base of the lower bowl four lines are drawn, dividing it into nine squares.
"Likewise make twelve on the upper"
means that on the base of the upper bowl too four lines are drawn as before, dividing it into nine, and then again four lines are drawn on the four corner squares, dividing it into twelve squares.
"Having written the name in the center"
means that in the center of the wheel, that is, in the heart square, one writes the name of the one to be accomplished.
"Likewise surround it with the seeds"
means surrounding it with the seed syllables of the mantra; the sense is that one writes the name interspersed with the syllables of the mantra.

In order to teach that interspersing, the text says the passage beginning "Homage." "The sound of homage" is the syllable oṃ, which is the very nature of the word "homage." "First" means at the beginning. Of what? This is to be read with "by the name of the one to be accomplished." And where does the name of the one to be accomplished go? The text says, "It is placed before the words of protection": one writes the name before rakṣa rakṣa and at the end writes the words rakṣa.

In
"Endowed with the five buddhas and the rest,
the seed stands at the end,"
"at the end" is at the end of the word rakṣa; there one writes the seed of the five buddhas, that is, hūṃ.
In "The syllables of the burner at its end," "its end" is the end of the syllable hūṃ. The syllables of the burner are svāhā, and that is written. The condensed meaning is that it becomes oṃ devadatta rakṣa rakṣa hūṃ svāhā. The two words rakṣa are to be understood from the oral instructions.

In "Place the vowel letters on the top and the rest, in the rim," the word "top" refers to the outer words. The rim is the border, that is, between the two outer lines of the nine squares. "Vowel letters" are the sixteen vowel letters. One should understand that they are to be written in the same way on the upper bowl too. Alternatively, "top" is the upper bowl and "rim" is the lower bowl; the rest is as before.

Another arrangement is taught. In the passage beginning "kha pha," "kha" is hūṃ. "Pha" is yuṃ. "Doubled" is kṣuṃ. "The goddess Vajra Lady" is the seed of the goddess Vajra Lady, the syllable suṃ. "The seed of the five buddhas" is the seed in which the five buddhas are joined with one another, that is, hūṃ.
In "All, homage, the burner at the end," "homage" is in the substance of all of them, that is, oṃ; and at the end of all, the burner, svāhā, is joined. Their meaning is this:
oṃ devadattaṃ rakṣa rakṣa hūṃ hūṃ svāhā,
oṃ devadattaṃ rakṣa rakṣa yuṃ hūṃ svāhā,
oṃ devadattaṃ rakṣa rakṣa kṣuṃ hūṃ svāhā,
oṃ devadattaṃ rakṣa rakṣa suṃ hūṃ svāhā.
This is the extraction of the mantras of the yoginīs.

In order to teach that the parts of the syllable hūṃ are the five buddhas, the text says the passage beginning "The staff is Lokeśvara, the Protector." "The staff" is the stroke of the lower part of the letter ha. "Lokeśvara, the Protector" is Amitābha. "The hook" is the subscribed vowel sign u. "Vajrapāṇi" is Akṣobhya. "The body" is the upper part of the letter ha. "The Gentle Supreme One" is Ratnasambhava. "The vase" is the half-moon. "Maitreya" is Amoghasiddhi.
In
"The mother letter blazing above
liberates from all of saṃsāra,"
"the mother letter" is the drop above, and "blazing" is Vairocana.

"Liberates from all of saṃsāra" means that what is thus gathered from the limbs of the five buddhas is hūṃ.

"There, dwelling in the eastern direction,
the goddess Vajraḍākinī"
shows the places for setting down the extracted mantras of the ḍākinīs taught above, and which goddess each one embodies. "Dwelling in the eastern direction" means that it is written in the eastern square of the nine squares. If one asks what is written there, it is "the goddess Vajraḍākinī," that is, the mantra that is the nature of Vajraḍākinī: oṃ devadattaṃ rakṣa rakṣa suṃ hūṃ svāhā.

"In the north, the seed of the one called the Fierce Lady is to be applied" means that likewise, going round counterclockwise, in the northern square one applies, that is, writes, the mantra that is the seed having the nature of the Fierce Lady: oṃ devadattaṃ rakṣa rakṣa kṣuṃ hūṃ svāhā.

"Dwelling in the western direction": dwelling in the western square is the seed of Vetālī, that is, the mantra that is the nature of Vetālī: oṃ devadattaṃ rakṣa rakṣa yuṃ hūṃ svāhā. "Applied" means written.

"The southern direction" means in the southern square. "The seed of Caṇḍālī" is the mantra oṃ devadattaṃ rakṣa rakṣa hūṃ hūṃ. "Taught completely" means written in full.

In the passage beginning "In Īśāna, Siṃhinī," one should write in sequence: in the corner of Īśāna, the mantra of Siṃhinī, smraṃ; in the corner of Agni, the mantra of Vyāghrī, hmryuṃ; in the corner of Nairṛti, the mantra of Jambukī, ymraṃ; and in the corner of Vāyu, the mantra of Ulūkī, kṣmaṃ. "Making hūṃ" means making terror by proclaiming the sound hūṃ. "Ulūkī" means the one possessed of darkness. These four mantras are to be understood individually from the oral instructions.

"The seeds dwelling in the corners,
the four, are the substance of six letters"
means that each of those four seeds of Siṃhinī and the others, dwelling in the corners, is by nature a stack of six letters.

In order to teach the summary of setting down the mantras, the text says the passage beginning "Directions and." The sense is that one should set down each of those seeds in the squares of the directions and the rest, in the manner stated.

"In the twelve places above
one should place the seeds of the vowels"
means that one should write twelve vowel letters in the twelve squares of the upper bowl.

In order to teach that, just like the mantra written in the center of the lower bowl, one writes in the center of the upper one as well, the text says, "With homage and the burner at the end." "Homage" is oṃ, "the burner" is svāhā, and "with" is hūṃ. By this it teaches that in the central square of the upper bowl one writes the mantra oṃ devadattaṃ rakṣa rakṣa hūṃ svāhā. In the square east of it, ṛ; in the north, ṝ; in the west, ḷ; and in the south, ḹ. That is what is said in "In the directions one should place the neuters": in the order taught, the four neuter letters are written in the directions. In the corners, according to the oral instructions, it is taught that eight vowel letters are written: going round clockwise, in the corner of Īśāna one writes i ī, in the corner of Agni u ū, in those of Nairṛti e ai, and in those of Vāyu o au. That is the condensed meaning.

The four neuter seeds are by nature the Blazing Lady and the others. To teach this, the text says the passage beginning "the Blazing Lady," which is easy to understand.

"With homage, the burner placed at the end"
means that, just as in the center of the nine squares the syllable hūṃ is interspersed with homage and svāhā, so here too the letters in the twelve squares are to be interspersed, as in oṃ devadattaṃ rakṣa rakṣa hūṃ svāhā.

In order to teach which seed belongs to whom, and the fixed direction of each, the text says the passage beginning "One should place them according to their places."
"The east is the place of the ḍākinī"
means that the seed ṛ in the eastern square is the very nature of the goddess Ḍākinī.
"Dwelling in the north is the Blazing Lady"
means that the seed in the northern square is the goddess the Blazing Lady, that is, ṝ.
"In the west she is declared the Sucking Lady"
means that the seed in the western square is the goddess the Sucking Lady, that is, ḷ.
"In the south the Ravisher is to be placed"
means that the seed in the southern square is the goddess the Ravisher, that is, ḹ.

In order to explain what was taught before, in "The name is to be written in the center," "in the center" is in the center of the wheel. "The name" is the name of the one to be accomplished, written interspersed with the mantra.
"Below likewise, and above likewise"
means that, just as the name is written in the central square below, so it is written in the square above as well. Alternatively, "the center" means that the name of the one to be accomplished is written between homage and the words rakṣa rakṣa. In "Below likewise, and above likewise," "above" is the upper bowl, and "below" is the center; the meaning here is that one writes here too just as on the earlier bowl.

In the passage beginning "Both," "both" is the two bowls. "Joined mouth to mouth" is easy to understand. "Wound with red thread" means bound all round with red thread. The passage beginning "All round" is easy to understand.

The offering is taught. In the passage beginning "Various," the torma of enjoyments is a collection of meat, fish, garlic, onion, pastries and the like.
"Having placed the yantra in the center"
means placing the yantra on top of the maṇḍala explained before; it is connected with "Having imagined a vajra, one should touch it." If one asks what kind of yantra, the text says the passage beginning "The five ḍākinīs": it is the joined pair of bowls on which one has meditated the wisdom ḍākinī, Vajraḍākinī, the Fierce Lady, Vetālī and Caṇḍālī.
"Through the yoga of oneself as the vajra holder"
means meditating on oneself as the wisdom ḍākinī, or as Vajraḍākinī together with her retinue.

"One says, 'I am Vajrasattva'"
means that after that one should take on the pride of Vajrasattva, or else meditate on it as lacking intrinsic nature.
"At the tip of the vajra hand"
means at the tips of the fingers held in the mudrā of the three-pronged vajra. "Having imagined a vajra" is easy to understand. "One should touch" means touching the bowls. Or else it means holding a vajra in the hand and touching them with it.
"The seed shown in the center"
means whatever mantra is written in the center of the bowls, namely oṃ debadattaṃ rakṣa rakṣa hūṃ svāhā.
"One should recite it eight hundred times"
is easy to understand.
The benefits are stated in the passage beginning:
"The sins of former lives and so on
Should be charged to the gods and the rest."
The sins accumulated in former lives and so on are charged to the gods; that is, it is the gods who do away with them. Otherwise, the meaning is, the body of the one on whose behalf the rite is performed would not be protected by this much of the rite alone.
"Affliction of all the limbs
And the arrival of the time of death will not occur"
means that illness and untimely death will not occur in the body of the one for whom it is accomplished.
"Having placed it at the sick person's side and applied it"
means if one places the yantra at the side of the sick person and applies the mantra recitation.
"At all times illness will not arise"
means being free of illness. Alternatively, "at all times" means that for as long as the yantra over which the mantra has been recited eight hundred times remains, for that long untimely death and illnesses will not occur; and by reciting the mantra eight hundred times over that very yantra, even if one has been seized by illness, illness and untimely death are turned back.
Then:
"Even an embodied being whose life is exhausted
Will remain for six months"
is connected with "having placed it at the sick person's side and applied it." The meaning is that if one places the yantra at the side of the sick person and recites the mantra, even those whose life is exhausted will have their life extended by six months.
"Having placed it in another place,
One should always be diligent in worship"
means that even if one places it somewhere other than at the side, when one recites the mantra preceded by offerings, it will turn out as stated before.
For that very reason it says:
"Through the application of the vajra seal,
Consciousness will not go anywhere."
The meaning is that just as a person does not come out when the door of the house is guarded by a seal, so consciousness does not depart from a body protected by this rite.
Now, in order to teach the curing of poison, the text says the passage beginning "Furthermore," which is to be construed with "Furthermore, I shall explain the rite."
"Furthermore, one's own breath
Should be carefully examined there."
"One's own breath" is the movement of one's own breath. "There" means in curing poison. "Should be carefully examined" means that one should strive at investigation.
For that very reason it says:
"The place of the breath being the same"
This means that if the messenger speaks from the same side as the nostril through which one's own breath is flowing, then the breath and the position are the same.
"Everything is declared to succeed"
means that those actions, such as curing his poison, will succeed.
To make this clear, it says the passage beginning "On the right." "On the right, standing on the right" means that when one's own breath flows through the right nostril, the messenger also stands on the right: the breath and the position are the same. "On the left, standing on the left" is likewise.
"Speaking while standing there,
Everything will indeed succeed"
is easy to understand.
To teach what is to be avoided, it says:
"When the breath and the questioning do not match,
Accomplishment should be declared far off."
"Questioning" means words such as, "Devadatta has been bitten by a fang; what should be done for him?" "Not matching the breath" means that the position is not the same: when one's own breath flows through the right nostril the messenger stands on the left, or when it flows on the left he stands on the right; the meaning is that he is on the side of the nostril empty of breath. "Accomplishment should be declared far off" is easy to understand. Having thus examined the breath for success and failure, one should either go or not go to the one bitten by the fang.
Another kind of examination is stated in the passage beginning "Moment." "Moment" means the breath entering inward. "Life" means its going outward. "Two applications" means the two applications of the breath just described: asking as the breath enters inward, and asking as it goes outward. "Should be known" means that, of those two applications, the application of the moment is to be adopted, and the other should be known as to be abandoned.
For that very reason, in the passage beginning "Moment," "all actions" means all purposes. If one asks what that is, it says:
"It is renowned as bringing increase."
That is, if everything, such as increase, is accomplished, what need is there to mention curing poison alone? Or else "bringing increase" means bringing completion: all actions are accomplished.
"Whatever action is done on the life breath,
Accomplishment is far off, there is no doubt"
means that actions of questioning made while the application of the life breath prevails are far off; that is, they will not succeed.
"Having considered fangs and the rest, the poison
Should be cured there."
By the word "rest," other illnesses too should be included. "Having considered" means having examined, in the manner stated before, whether it is fit or not. "Should be cured there" is easy to understand. If one asks what should be examined: "having the characteristic of mantra, such as the application," that is, the characteristic of the application stated in the earlier division into stages. "By the one who knows all" means by the yogin. "Commanding the nāgas" means that, knowing how to issue commands, one cures illness caused by nāgas.
To teach the characteristic mentioned before, it says:
"With the four applications, one bitten by teeth
Will die, there is no doubt."
"Four applications" means the four horned ones. "Bitten by teeth" is illustrative, standing for all illnesses. Or else "bitten by teeth" means fang poison alone.

"Four applications" means, according to the guru's oral instructions, Mikiri and the rest. In the application of Mikira, it is being bitten by Kulika; in that of Bitika, by the Hundred-Headed One; in that of Svapna, by a sting; and in that of Kama, by the fang of Time. The meaning is that those seized during these four applications will die without any doubt. The rest are to be treated, and for that very reason it says:
"By the application that draws in the poison,
All the others should be cured."
The meaning is that all the other eight should be cured by the application for removing poison that is given below.
To teach that very application for removal, it says the passage beginning "On the bitten body":
"One should place them by placing the seeds."
This means that one places the eight seed syllables on the limbs of the body of the person being treated; that is, one imagines them there. For that very reason it says, in the passage beginning "Of eight syllables":
"The mantras of eight syllables
Should be placed on the eight limbs."
The eight syllables themselves are the mantra. By placing and imagining them on the eight limbs, that is, on the limbs beginning with the head, one should protect him, as follows. On the head is kṣuṃ. On the nose and the two ears is yuṃ. In the two eyes is hūṃ. In the mouth is suṃ. At the throat is smryuṃ. At the palate is hmraṃ. At the heart is ymraṃ. At the navel is kṣmaṃ.
"Imagined as white in color"
means that although those seeds are of various colors, in the context of removing poison they are all imagined as white.
"With the seeds, remove the mind poison."
"Mind poison" is said because poisons have the nature of one's own conceptual thought. "Remove with the seeds" means removing the power of the poison with the seeds mentioned before, according to the rite that comes below.
"Abiding in the center of the maṇḍala"
Here the maṇḍala is the body, and its center is the heart. The wisdom abiding there is the syllable hūṃ; with it consciousness is to be bound, that is, restrained.
"Having performed the application that protects the limbs,
After that one should begin the outer."
The meaning is that first, in the sequence stated before, one protects the body seized by poison, and after that begins the outer action.
The outer action is stated in the passage beginning:
"With water mixed with milk
One should fill an earthen vessel."
The earthen vessel is a vase made of clay or the like. Filling it with water mixed with milk is easy to understand. The meaning of the passage beginning "the moon disk" is this meditation: inside the vase is an eight-petaled lotus; at its center is a moon disk; and on that, arisen from the complete transformation of suṃ, is Vajraḍākinī, adorned with a white parasol, white in color, holding a khaṭvāṅga and a skull cup in her two hands, adorned with ornaments of snakes, seated in the posture of a sattva, and pressing down the heads of the eight nāgas, which are gathered beneath her moon-disk seat.
Now, to show the nāgas who dwell on the lotus petals, it says the passage beginning "In the east." On the eastern petal is Vāsuki, white. In the north is Takṣaka, yellow. In the west is Karkoṭa, red. In the south is Padma, blue. In Īśāna's quarter, the northeast, is Mahāpadma, whitish yellow. In Agni's quarter, the southeast, is Śaṅkhapāla, whitish blue. In Nairṛti's quarter, the southwest, is Kulika, bluish red. In Vāyu's quarter, the northwest, is the Coiled Faller, reddish yellow. These are the eight nāgas.
To show the summary, it says:
"Having imagined the eight nāgas,
Meditate that their heads are pressed down."
This has already been explained.
