To teach the offerings, it says the passage beginning "With all substances." All the substances are flowers, incense, scents, lamps and food offerings.
"Give torma with flowers, incense and the rest"
means giving torma to the nāgas.
"Oneself, in the yoga of the deity,
Should be imagined as Vajraḍākinī"
means that at this time of removing poison one performs the deity yoga of oneself as Vajraḍākinī.
"With a bunch of peacock feathers,
By the application of water, one should draw it out"
means drawing it out and transferring it into the water. If one asks from where it is drawn, it says:
"Having drawn it all the way to the feet."
The whole body, beginning with the head, is swept with the bunch of peacock feathers, drawing the poison down as far as the soles of the feet.
"From the feet, dissolve it into the water"
means that one transfers it into the water of the vase and offers it to the deity.
The mantra for removing poison is given: oṃ hrīḥ ṭaṭṭaṇṭa ra hūṃ phaṭ svāhā. Here, according to the oral instructions, it should be read with three hūṃ and three phaṭ.
To show that it does not succeed unless the approach has first been performed, it says the passage beginning "In Vajrasattva." First, through the yoga of Vajrasattva, one performs the approach by reciting all the mantras, of Vāsuki and the rest, a hundred thousand times each. Those mantras are also given, beginning oṃ vāsuki, and they are easy to understand.
To show how the mantras are interwoven at the time of removing poison, it says the passage beginning oṃ hrīḥ ṭaṭṭaṇṭar. In it, āmukasya is the sixth case used in the sense of the second.
"Having drawn it all the way to the feet"
is illustrated by the passage beginning "By drawing it into the middle of the milk." Reciting the mantra oṃ vāsuki hrīḥ ṭaṭṭaṇṭa ra devadattan ni ri ba śyaṃ ru hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ phaṭ phaṭ svāhā and all the other mantras as well, one sweeps from the head down to the feet, draws the poison out, and transfers it into the water. Merely by that transfer, the milk at once turns black.
To show the application to be performed after the poison has been removed and the milk has turned black, it says the passage beginning "White mustard seed." The word "white" is to be applied to every item. "Reciting the secret mantra" means reciting all the mantras mentioned before while striking. Merely by the striking, the heart of the person being treated begins to throb and so on. If one asks what is to be done then, it says the passage beginning "The heart":
"Restraining the wisdom, one should hold it."
This means that by meditating on the wisdom, the syllable hūṃ, at his heart, his consciousness is bound and held. By that alone the breath begins to stir.

If one asks what is to be done next, it says the passage beginning "When the breath stirs":
"One should press him with one's own breath."
The meaning is that, with the wind of one's own nostrils, one should make it dissolve into his body through the nostrils of the one bitten by the fang, and fill him with it. By that alone his feet, hands and so on begin to tremble.
If one asks what is to be done next, it says the passage beginning "When all the limbs tremble":
"One should oneself take up the melody."
This means that the mantra practitioner himself should sing a song. If one asks what song, it says "the song of hūṃ," that is, the one beginning oṃ hūṃ trena. To show that it is sung preceded by three finger snaps, it says:
"One should sing it with three finger snaps."
The meaning is that when the song is sung together with three finger snaps, the one bitten by the fang will rise up.
Even after the sick person has risen, one should protect him by meditating on his body as Vajraḍākinī. To teach this, it says:
"Having raised up the one bitten by the fang,
One should protect his limbs."
This is easy to understand.
After that, to show that the vase of milk is carried to a place blessed by nāgas, it says the passage beginning "Having gathered."
Now, to teach that the approach must be performed beforehand to the chief of the poison mantras, it says the passage beginning "The moon disk." The meaning of these lines is this: one meditates on oneself as Vajrasattva, generated from hūṃ on a moon disk upon a lotus, white in color, with two arms, adorned with all ornaments, seated in vajra posture, and holding a vajra and a bell in the right and left hands; and if one recites the mantra beginning oṃ hrīḥ three hundred thousand times, the prior approach has been performed. Moreover, having first performed the approach by reciting it three hundred thousand times, afterwards too one should without fail recite it one hundred thousand times.
"Then, if the practitioner draws in the poison,
It will succeed; of this there is no doubt."
This states the benefit of the approach.
To show the places of a bite that are not to be treated, it says the passage beginning "The mouth." "The tilaka" means the middle of the eyebrows. "Above" means the palate. "The tip of the nose" means the two nostrils. "The heart" means the sexual organ. "The secret place" means the anus. "The big toes of the feet" is illustrative, so the thumbs of the hands are also included. The rest is as the words say.
"One seized there should not be treated"
means that such a one is not fit to be cured and should be given up. For that very reason it says:
"This is declared to be a cause of certain death."
That is, the words "being seized at those places" are to be supplied.
Now, since it arises incidentally in connection with the nāgas, to teach the rite of bringing down rain, it says the passage beginning "Listen!" "Great Vajra King" is an address to Vajrapāṇi.
"The supreme rite of bringing down rain":
supreme means chief, that is, sublime. "Close to a riverbank" means on the bank of an ordinary stream.
"At a level spot, in a pleasing place"
means on the shore of a lake blessed by nāgas; in the northwest of such places one should begin the maṇḍala. The meaning of the passage beginning "Having drawn canopies" is easy to understand.
"Square, with four gates"
means with four gates that have no archways.
"Adorned all around by eight nāgas"
means that the corners and the gates are adorned by the eight nāgas beginning with Jaya, resembling archways and the like. For that very reason it says the passage beginning "The heads": at the gates their hoods are raised upward and crossed with one another, and their hearts are pressed against one another.
"With two tails, knotted at the corners"
means that the four corners are adorned with the knots of the eight tails. If one asks who they are, it says the passage beginning "In the east, Jaya and Bhadra," which is easy to understand.
"The eight nāgas are the fence"
means that the eight nāgas just described encircle the maṇḍala like a fence. Since this is taught in connection with the words "Place the eight families in the directions and the rest," it shows that the eight fence nāgas described before resemble in color the eight families that come below. By "the rest," the intermediate directions are included. If one asks who these are, it says the passage beginning "Vāsuki." One draws an eight-petaled lotus in the center of the maṇḍala and places Vāsuki and the others on its eastern petal and the rest, in the order stated. Their colors too should be regarded as explained earlier in the context of removing poison.
"The eight kings, lords of the clouds,
Should be summoned from the eight directions."
The meaning is that one draws the eight clouds, beginning with the Thunderer, in the eight directions and intermediate directions outside the maṇḍala, summons them from their spontaneously present abodes, and merges them with these. If one asks who they are, it says the passage beginning "The Thunderer." The Thunderer is in the east, the Murky One in the southeast, the Fierce One in the south, the Coiling One in the southwest, the Dense One in the west, the Violent One in the northwest, the Rain-Bringer in the north, and the Brimming One in the northeast. These are called the eight, and the meaning is "place them in the directions beginning with the east."
"Imagine them in the colors of their directions"
means imagining them the same color as the inner nāgas. These have the form of men, adorned with a single hood.
"In the center, Vajraḍākinī
Is perfectly seated on a lotus seat."
The meaning is that one places Vajraḍākinī on the center of the lotus in the middle of that maṇḍala. If one asks what she is like, it says the passage beginning "A khaṭvāṅga and a yoga vessel," which is easy to understand.
"Having encircled the lotus with snakes,
One should arrange a seat of snakes."
This means that, as stated before, the lotus petals are encircled by the eight nāgas, and one draws the eight heads of the nāgas pressed down by the goddess's seat.
To teach the offerings, it says the passage beginning "Vast offerings": one makes offerings with eight bowls of milk and so on.
"The knower offers with various lamps."
"Various" means various offerings. "The knower" means one who possesses knowledge, that is, the yogin. The rest is easy to understand.

If one asks with what kind of yoga one is endowed, it says:
"The yogin possessing wisdom,
Who has meditated on himself as the wisdom ḍākinī."
The ḍākinī whose nature is wisdom is the wisdom ḍākinī; that is, one meditates on oneself as Vajraḍākinī. Meditation is illustrative, so mantra recitation is also included. That itself is wisdom, and one possesses it. This teaches that one first draws the maṇḍala, and after that meditates on oneself as Vajraḍākinī and recites the mantra. "The mantras of the ancillary rites" are the mantras for purification, torma, touching and so on. The passage beginning oṃ vāsuki gives the mantras of the eight nāgas. In the passage beginning oṃ ghili ghili, the three letters ṭha here are to be changed into three phaṭ, so that it becomes phaṭ phaṭ phaṭ.
"Having circled the hands all around,
One should show them to the eight directions."
This means that by circling the two hands into a lotus and showing them to the clouds in the eight directions, the clouds are summoned, since it was said before, "They should be summoned from the eight directions."
The mudrās in the passage beginning "Having clenched the left hand into a fist" are easy to understand. "The mudrā of the eight nāga kings" is the samaya mudrā of the eight nāgas; it should be shown joined with the preceding mantras. Likewise, the mantras and mudrās of the clouds should be joined inseparably and shown one by one.
oṃ vajraḍākinī varṣa varṣa paya hūṃ phuḥ svāhā is the root mantra of Vajraḍākinī for bringing down rain. Although it is also the mantra by which the nāgas and the clouds bring down rain, it is to be used as her root mantra because Vajraḍākinī is the principal deity.
"Having first performed the approach
Three hundred thousand times, one should recite"
means that one first performs an approach of three hundred thousand recitations with that very mantra. In the passage beginning "By the yogin," "these" is connected with "the prior approach of these mantras," and the words to be supplied are: having performed the approach, the "actions," that is, drawing the maṇḍala and the other actions for bringing down rain, will certainly succeed.
In the passage beginning "Furthermore, of virtue and sin," "furthermore" means other signs: the signs of the messenger, which are different from the signs of the breath mentioned before. The passage beginning "Squinting eyes" is easy to understand. "Frightful clothes" means black clothes. "Carrying a staff" is self-explanatory.
"Comes in the company of women"
means that he comes accompanied by women. "Smoke" means giving off smoke. "Loose hair" means hair hanging down in disheveled strands. "And so on" means crying out words such as "gone" and "lost."
"Naked, and speaking forcefully"
"Naked" means without clothes. "Speaking forcefully" means speaking of being dragged off by force and the like.
"Through the four applications, it indicates death"
means that messengers who come during the four applications of Mikira, Bitika, Svapna and Kama indicate that the sick person will certainly die.
"As with the messenger, so with oneself:
Having examined, one should make it twofold."
The meaning is that, just as with the messenger, one should also examine the signs of one's own breath as described before, and then, according to whether it will succeed or not, decide on the two options of going or not going. For that very reason it says:
"If at that time the mantra practitioner wishes to go,
The accomplishment will be ruined, there is no doubt."
The meaning is that even if one wishes to go in spite of bad signs, the accomplishment is ruined: it will not succeed.
In "The Chapter of the Self Seat," the Self Seat has already been explained. It is called a chapter because this text, through knowing the omens of death, clarifies how to cheat death and so on.
This is the second division into stages, on the Self Seat, from the commentary on the Glorious Catuṣpīṭha called Smṛtinibandha.

To teach meditation on oneself as the deity, preceded by the complete relinquishing of the ordinary aggregates and so on, the division into stages on emptiness is taught. In the passage beginning "Yoga":
"The particulars of undertaking yoga"
means the particulars of the rite for undertaking deity yoga.
"Of which should the mantras be sought?"
means, "How should the mantras be sought?"
"How is wisdom to be grasped?"
refers to grasping the wisdom that is the sign of accomplishments such as pacifying.
"How should the mind be moistened?"
is to be construed with "I wish to hear from the Blessed One."
The answer is given in the passage beginning "Listen." "Reality" means what is unerring. "The nature of the deity" means the nature of meditating on oneself as the deity; or else, it is said for the sake of making oneself the deity. "Listen" is an exhortation to listen to what comes below. What is to be listened to is stated in the passage beginning "First." "First" means first in meditating on oneself as the deity. The meaning is that at that first time one meditates on the body as emptiness, and after that one meditates on it as the deity.
"The embodied one should wash away the stains."
The stains are fixation on the grasped and the grasper and so on. The meaning is that, having washed away those stains with emptiness, one meditates on oneself as the deity. To explain this, it says the passage beginning:
"First, form and the rest should be made empty."
It is called form because it is set up and imagined; that is, the five aggregates. "Empty" refers to the very nature of that form. By the word "rest," signlessness and wishlessness are to be included. The meaning is that because the essential nature, the signs and the conceptual thoughts of that form lack intrinsic nature, one meditates on them as the three doors of liberation. To show that it is not only the five aggregates that are purified by the three doors of liberation, but that other things too are included, it says:
"As far as the eighteen, mind itself."
"As far as" indicates inclusion.

If one asks what these are: they are the six faculty elements called the eye, the ear, the nose, the tongue, the body and the mind; the six objects called form, sound, smell, taste, the tangible and phenomena; and the six elements of consciousness that arise in dependence on them. The meaning is that these eighteen too are purified by the three doors of liberation, as before. "Mind itself" means that when these are closely examined, what proves to be valid is one's own mind alone. Even granting that objects exist externally, the positing of them depends on mind alone, because mind is luminous by its own nature and is simply the experiencing of everything.
Now, to teach the particular divisions of emptiness, it says the passage beginning "The form element." "Form" means things that are objects of the eye, such as white, yellow, long and short; it is so called because they are visibly manifest. Because it holds its own characteristic it is an element; and since it is both form and element, it is the form element. "Empty" means its essential nature is free of grasped and grasper. That is to say, since it is an appearance of consciousness, it is empty of a grasped and a grasper distinct from consciousness, but it is not empty of the appearance of consciousness, like the moon in water. The moon in water is empty of the essential nature of the moon, yet the moon in water itself is not nonexistent. If one asks whether the elements of form and the rest exist as something distinct from appearance, they do not, because they cannot withstand analysis. Because, like the moon in water, it is free of the conventions of true and false, it is spoken of in that way.
"Sound too should be treated in that same way"
means that the element of sound too is likewise empty of grasped and the rest. This should be applied to the others in the same way.
"Form too should be treated in that same way."
Here it is called form because it is known and experienced; it is the object whose nature is the element of phenomena, having the essential nature of bliss.
To show, after the objects, that the faculties such as the eye are empty, it says the passage beginning "In the emptiness of the nature of the eye." When the eye and the rest are examined, they too lack the intrinsic nature of a grasper and so forth; therefore their essential nature is that of the three doors of liberation. To show that when these do not exist, how could the consciousnesses that arise in dependence on them exist, so that they too are emptiness and the rest, it says the passage beginning "In the middle."
"How should one examine the middle?"
means: how should the nature of the consciousness in the middle be understood? The answer runs up to the words, "All these are merely imputations of the mind."
To enter into that, the samādhi of non-appearance is taught in the passage beginning "The mind." "The mind" means cognition. "Having dissolved into space" means that everything is burned by the blazing fire of the syllable hūṃ and is made so that not even ashes remain, and thus it dissolves into non-appearance. For that very reason it says:
"One should imagine everything as empty."
This is the summary. To explain that teaching, it says the passage beginning "The continents." By the word "and the rest," everything up to the oceans and the wind maṇḍala is included. "Above" means those who move in the sky. "Below" means those who move beneath the earth. "Between" means those who move on the earth. These three worlds are the three realms. "All" refers to the twofold division of the container and its contents. The summary is given in the passage beginning "The ten directions," which is easy to understand.
Having thus dismantled all things one by one, in order to make oneself too into the essential nature of space, it says:
"One should exhaust one's own entity."
The meaning is that finally one burns and dismantles the conceptions of the form and so forth of one's own body, and makes them non-appearing.
"One should focus on things as mingled"
means that one mixes all objects that are things into the single taste of the essential nature of space and focuses on that; and then one should meditate on oneself as the deity. "By the yogin" means by one who possesses yoga. "By the wise" means by one whose understanding is that the grasped and the rest are empty.
To answer the question, "How is wisdom to be grasped?" it says the passage beginning "Sameness." The meaning is that, having contemplated emptiness dissolved into space in the way just described, and "having looked at its non-appearing nature" in its center, one should grasp wisdom.
"Then its signs should be examined"
means that in that wisdom of emptiness one should examine, that is, investigate, the signs or omens of accomplishments such as pacifying. Or else the sign itself is wisdom, because through it one comes to know the accomplishments such as pacifying. "There" means at that time.
This is stated in the passage beginning "A lamp": when a light like a lamp appears, one should know that the rite of pacifying will succeed.
"Red, through its radiance, brings increase."
Radiance means light. Increase means that things will increase. The passage beginning "Of the sun" follows. "The rite of passion" means bringing under control; by the word "and the rest," summoning, suppressing and the like are included.
"If at the end one sees black smoke"
means if at the end of the samādhi one sees something black like smoke.
"It is renowned for fierce rites"
is easy to understand.
"Turbulent like the color of wind"
means bluish and churning, billowing in turmoil.
"At that very moment there will be expulsion"
is also easy to understand.
"Like the white rays of the moon"
means a light that is extremely white. "In all" means for a long time.
"One should know that the yoga is accomplished"
is easy to understand. If one asks what this is, it says:
"The intelligent one truly abides in beings."
The meaning is that through this one comes to know the past and future states of beings and so forth.
Now, to teach the meditation on the deity after emptiness, it says the passage beginning "After that." "After that" means at the end of meditating on emptiness. "Arisen from yoga" means generated in deity yoga.

If one asks of what, then:

"One should contemplate it as marked by the sprout and so forth."

This means that in the field of the body, purified by emptiness, one should generate the deity through the sequence of the deity's seed and the sprout of the divine form. This very point will be taught again and again; here only a partial indication has been given. The key instruction is this: if the yogin remains in all objects, all things, as emptiness for two instants or so, then through the sequence of the deity's seed and so forth, the fruit, such as knowledge of the past and the future, will ripen.

If one asks what fault there would be in deity yoga alone, apart from emptiness, it says:

"Whoever meditates, having known 'empty,'
will swiftly, O Sattva, be liberated."

"Whoever" means the yogin. "Empty" means having meditated on emptiness. "Meditates" means having meditated on oneself as the deity. This is to be construed as: that one will swiftly be liberated, and not otherwise. "Sattva" means the mind; or else it is an address to Vajrapāṇi.

If one asks what that is like, it says:

"With wisdom one should view the knowable;
with the knowable one should examine the going."

"With wisdom" means with the wisdom of emptiness. The knowable is the aggregates and so forth, and "viewing" means making them into its nature. In "with the knowable one should examine the going," "the knowable" is the deity, and "the going" is its intrinsic nature. "Examine" means analyze: if one analyzes the intrinsic nature of the deity too, to the very end, with nondual wisdom, then "that one will swiftly be liberated"; this connects with what came before.

In order to explain "The embodied one should wash away the stains," it says the passage beginning "The father and." "The father" is delusion, whose nature is grasping at "I" and "mine." Just as a father is the begetter of beings, so delusion too begets objects. "The mother" is craving. Just as a mother does not abandon her children even when they are dying, so craving too does not let persons give up objects; the two are alike in this respect. "Killing" means destroying them.

"Having seized the two pure brahmins"

means attachment and aversion, taught in the code language of the Dharma. "Seizing" means restraining their movement.

"Having destroyed the king's realm along with its retinue"

Here "the king" is pride, because it is exceedingly conceited and because it emanates everything. As it is said: "Everything arises from pride itself." "The realm" is the body, because it is the abode of all the afflictions and so forth. "Along with" means what moves in accompaniment: the aggregates, the elements and so forth, because they move together with the body. "Having destroyed" means having made them free of appearance through the meditative absorption of emptiness. This is connected with "the embodied one becomes pure of stains." Although this teaching is also found among the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas), it should not be rejected, because it is in no way contradictory.

This is the summarized meaning of this chapter. The section beginning "the Self" is called a chapter (prakaraṇa) because it fully clarifies the wisdom of emptiness together with the signs of pacifying and the other rites.

This concludes the third division, on the Self Seat, from the commentary on the Glorious Catuṣpīṭha called Smṛtinibandha.

Now, in order to teach in brief the deity yoga that follows emptiness, the fourth division is spoken, beginning "Listen." "Vajra" means Vajrapāṇi. "Listen" is connected with "nondual." "As the path" means because it is trodden like a path. "Abandoning the two" means being free of the grasped and so forth; because of that very fact it is nondual. By these words it shows that nondual deity yoga is itself the path by which one travels to great awakening.

If one asks how, it says the passage beginning "Saṃsāra." Saṃsāra itself is an ocean, because it is hard for the childish to cross. It is terrifying because its suffering is exceedingly hard to bear.

"Filled with the supreme enemies of the waters"

The enemies of the waters are sea monsters and the like. Here it means filled with supreme, that is, unbearable, enemies: the sea monsters and the like of attachment and so forth.

"The embodied ones who sink in its midst"

means the embodied beings who have sunk within that ocean of saṃsāra. If one asks what there is for them, it says:

"It is declared to have the characteristic of rescuing."

"Characteristic" means what is to be characterized, that is, deity yoga. "Rescuing" means bringing across: it is the path that brings embodied beings across the ocean of saṃsāra. "Declared" means that it has been taught by all the countless sages.

In order to teach the characteristics of nondual wisdom, it says the passage beginning "The characterized." The characterized is deity yoga. The characteristic is the act of examining it. That by which it is characterized is the characterizer, the agent, that is, the yogin.

"Consciousness and wisdom"

Here consciousness is the knowable, and wisdom is cognizance; these are the characteristics of grasped and grasper belonging to the characterized and the rest.

"With wisdom one should view the knowable"

Here wisdom is the wisdom of emptiness. The knowable is the aggregates and so forth, and this means making them into that.

"One should examine the going of the knowable"

Here the knowable is the characterized and the rest, and the going is their intrinsic nature. "Examine" means analyze: their intrinsic nature is wisdom whose nature is free of grasped and grasper, and that itself is nonduality.

"With a mind that has examined the going"

means by the yogin who has understood the intrinsic nature of the knowable. If one asks what comes of this, it says the passage beginning "One proceeds just as one wishes." The meaning is that by this path of nondual deity yoga one will travel, just as one wishes, to perfect awakening.

In order to teach another benefit of the path of knowing emptiness, it says the passage beginning "By the noose." "Like a rutting elephant that is bound" means this: a rutting elephant bound by a noose, even though it has been bound by the noose, obtains special enjoyments. In the same way, the yogin bound by the noose of saṃsāra is like that.

"Bewildered about the vital point by the noose of saṃsāra"

Here the vital point is the wisdom that fully knows intrinsic nature, and "bewildered" means having entered into it. If one asks who, it is the yogin, who is like the elephant. "One thousand" is merely illustrative; if one asks of what, it is of countless former lives. "From destiny to destiny" means this: although one has been bound by that saṃsāra in which the destinies of former lives are taken up moment after moment, by meditating on deity yoga that follows upon the realization of emptiness, even if perfect awakening has not been accomplished,

"when the time of death arrives,
one goes to a special destiny."

That is the meaning. For that very reason it is said, in the passage beginning "Previously," "As the characteristics were taught previously"; this refers to what was said in the passage beginning "The mind having dissolved into space."

If one asks what is to be done after meditating on emptiness, it says the passage beginning "In the field of emptiness." The meaning is that in the field of the body, purified by emptiness, the one endowed with intelligence, that is, endowed with intelligence through the wisdom that discriminates phenomena, sows and plants the seed, the seeds of the deities, and then the fruit will ripen.

If one asks how it can be fitting to sow the seeds of the Tathāgatas in the body made of the aggregates and so forth, then in order to teach this by way of an example, it says the passage beginning "For example." The house of an outcaste (caṇḍāla) is the house of one of low birth. "Burned by fire" means burned through contact with fire.

"It is proclaimed to be blessed by the gods"

Here "the gods" means the deities, and "blessed" means that relics, paintings and the like are placed there. And not only that; it says:

"Those of noble birth, the virtuous, and the learned always dwell there."

Those of noble birth are themselves the virtuous: kings, brahmins and so forth. The learned are those trained in the systems of reasoning, those who practice the yoga of reality, and so forth. They dwell there.

"Likewise, having demolished all through meditative absorption"

Here the meditative absorption is the meditative absorption of emptiness. "All" means the house of the body. "Demolished" means made free of appearance by the flames of emptiness.

"One should fully set it in place by placing the seeds"

This means that by placing the seeds of the deities one should make it into a place like a temple, the fragrant chamber of a deity, and so forth. It is construed with:

"The mantras of eight syllables
should be envisioned in place after place."

In order to explain this and to give the answer to "Whose mantras should be known?", it says the passage beginning "Above." After completing the deity yoga and the rest, this is construed with "One should fully set it in place by placing the seeds." "Above" means on the palate. "The seed of fire" means the seed whose nature is fire, the letter kṣuṃ.

"The seed of earth, in two forms"

means the seed whose nature is purified earth, hūṃ, in two forms. "Joining" means placing.

"The seed of wind, emptiness and so on"

means the seed of wind, which is purified emptiness: yuṃ. "On the nose and ears" is to be construed as "on the two nostrils and on the two ears"; to contemplate in those places is to meditate.

"The seed of nectar, of water"

The seed of nectar is suṃ, and that is itself the purified water element.

"The faculty of the tongue is the lord, the chief."

The faculty of the tongue is itself the lord, the lord who enjoys nectar; for that very reason it is the chief.

"Of which, stacked in order, they should be held"

Here "of which" means which seed belongs to which place, and it is connected with "should be applied in four places." "Stacked" means placing in order the letters, from ma and the rest up to the bindu.

To teach this very point, it says the passage beginning "At the throat." "The seed of delusion" means the seed of purified delusion, smraṃ.

"Likewise, at the shoulders, affliction"

means at the two shoulders the seed of purified aversion, hūṃ.

"In the region of the heart, the moving one"

Here the moving one is wind, and the meaning is that one meditates on its purified seed, ymraṃ, at the heart.

"At the root of the navel is the beast."

Here the beast is the owl, and this is construed as: its seed, kṣmryuṃ, is placed at the root of the navel.

"The seeds in the eight limbs:
by setting out the eight, one should protect."

The meaning is that the eight seeds, set out in the manner explained before, are like armor; with them one should fasten it on all around and protect oneself.

"Having made oneself the wisdom ḍākinī,
one should endow the body with the seeds."

This sums up the text. The meaning is that first one makes oneself the goddess, the wisdom ḍākinī, and after that one places the seeds. The wisdom ḍākinī is given as an illustration.

In order to teach the form of the wisdom ḍākinī, it says the passage beginning "The disc of the sun"; this is easy to understand.

"Above, she has a vajra crown."

Here "above" means the top of the crown; she who has a crown marked there with a vajra is described in these words.

"Bound with a beautiful silk headband"

means that she has a silk crown-band tied on, made beautiful by streaming out all around. "Three eyes" means that each of the three faces in turn has three eyes. In "wrathful and laughing," the laughing one is the left face and the wrathful one is the right face; the root face here is to be seen as amorous.

"She wears exceedingly beautiful garments"

means she is dressed in garments of an exceedingly white color.

"Sword, axe and vajra"

The words "in the right hands" are to be supplied here, and it is connected with "she holds the emblems as hand implements." The passage beginning "In the left" is easy to understand.

"Clouds of buddhas" means the host of Vairocana and the rest. The remainder is easy to understand.

"One should meditate with the breath unmoving."

Here "with the breath unmoving" means a state in which there is no movement of the breath. "One should meditate" means meditating as the wisdom ḍākinī; one meditates until the breath has dissolved into the body and has mingled with the mind. This teaches one-pointed focus.

Now the subtle yoga is taught, in the passage beginning "Mind." "Mind" means the letter hūṃ. "Wisdom, consciousness" are synonyms for it.

"It should be contemplated as like crystal."

This means that one meditates on that letter hūṃ as white in color.

"By fully joining the vowels and consonants (āli-kāli),
its seed is to be taken up."

Here "its" means that seed whose nature is the mind and so forth. "Taken up as a dhāraṇī-mantra" means extracted. If one asks what it is, it says the passage beginning "By fully joining the vowels and consonants." "The vowel" here means the long letter ū, and "the consonant" means the letter hā. The bindu goes with both, because it is the sound that follows after.

If one asks where it is to be meditated on, it says:

"In the places of the drop between the brows"

Here "between the brows" is easy to understand. "The place of the drop" means the place of the bindu. "In that place, meditate on the seed that possesses wisdom": this means that one meditates on the seed, the letter hūṃ, facing downward. Here too it is connected with "One should meditate with the breath unmoving." The meaning is this: first, after one has become familiar with the meditative absorption of the deity, one meditates on the absorption in which the seed, in the form of a bindu, rests on a moon disc facing downward, with a stream of nectar dripping from it.

In order to teach the recitation of the mantra at the end of that, it says the passage beginning "Adorned by the five buddhas." "Adorned by the five buddhas" has already been explained. If one asks what that is, it is the seed letter, the seed of the wisdom ḍākinī.

In order to teach the extraction of that very mantra, it says the passage beginning "For the meaning of the seventh of wind and fire." Here wind is the letter ya, and its seventh is the letter sa. Fire is the letter ra, and its seventh is ha. These are the meaning of the words, since they are what the words "the seventh of wind and fire" mean. It is construed with "should be taken up." The singular word is used for the class.

"Urge on the seed of the Vajra Lady."

The seed of the Vajra Lady is made from the letter sa, the seventh of wind. "Upon the form" means joining it as the crescent moon of ha, the seed that is the seventh of fire. If one asks why, the meaning is this: because the crescent moon and the seed of the Vajra Lady are alike in both having the nature of dripping nectar, the sa too is to be joined as the crescent moon.

If one asks what else it is like, it says:

"Pressed down firmly by the nāda and the bindu."

Here the nāda is the long ū, which is to be joined as the seat of the letter ha, the seventh of fire. The bindu is the drop that presses down on top of the crescent moon. Put together, it becomes hūṃ.

"It is called the stream of rain."

That very seed, the letter hūṃ, is the stream of rain because it drips a rain of nectar. This is construed with "it should be recited."

If one asks how that seed is to be recited, it says the passage beginning "At the beginning." "At the beginning" means before hūṃ. "The supreme letter" means the letter oṃ.

"Recite the burning letter at the end."

The burning letter is svāhā. "At the end" means at the end of hūṃ. Put together, it becomes oṃ hūṃ svāhā.

"The intelligent one endowed with meditative absorption
should recite it three hundred thousand times."

This means that the mantra practitioner who has attained the meditative absorption of the wisdom ḍākinī should recite the mantra just given three hundred thousand times. By this the preliminary approach is accomplished.

"The yogin endowed with recitation
will accomplish all his aims."

This means that whatever yogin is endowed with meditation and recitation in that sequence will accomplish all his aims. If one asks what all these aims are, it says:

"The accomplishments of the eight actions and the rest"

Here the eight actions are pacifying, increasing, subjugating, summoning, fierce rites, expelling, dividing and binding. By "and the rest" the eight great accomplishments and so forth are to be included. "Beforehand" means before awakening. The meaning is that after one has manifestly accomplished the accomplishments of the eight actions and the rest, later awakening too will be accomplished.

If one asks how it is accomplished, it says the passage beginning "Approach." Approach has already been explained.

"If after that the actions are undertaken"

The meaning is that if later, at the time of accomplishment, one undertakes and accomplishes the actions of pacifying and the rest with the mantra combined with the appropriate words, they will be accomplished without any doubt.

Now, in order to teach the mantra of the great Vajradhara, it says the passage beginning "Listen"; this is easy to understand.

"First utter the word vajra."

This means pronouncing the word vajra at the beginning of the mantra.

"Take the first of the first group."

The first group is the ka group, and its first letter is ka itself.

"Likewise, having taken
the first letter of the fourth group"

The fourth group is the ta group, and its first letter is ta itself. "Doubled" means that ta is to be stacked twice. "Place the repha above" means placing the letter ra on top of the doubled ta.

"Likewise join the i and the repha."

This means placing the letter ra after that, and joining the letter i on top of it.

"That which is the second letter
of the second group, with a bindu"

means the second letter of the second group, the ca group, which is cha, together with a bindu placed beside it.

"It should be pierced with the hook."

Here the hook is the short letter i, which is joined on top of cha.

"Together with the third of the fourth"

means together with the third letter of the fourth group and with what stands beside it.

"It should be recited twice."

This means saying it twice, so that it becomes chinda chinda.

"And adorned by the five buddhas"

Here the five buddhas are the letter hūṃ, and "adorned" means that by pronouncing the letter hūṃ after it, the mantra is made beautiful.

"At the beginning is the letter of homage."

This means that at the beginning of the whole mantra, that is, first, the letter of homage, oṃ, is to be pronounced.

"Join the fierce letter at the end."

Here the fierce letter is the letter phaṭ. "Joining it at the end" means reciting it at the end of the whole mantra. Put together, it becomes oṃ vajra kartari chinda chinda hūṃ phaṭ.

In order to teach the benefits of this mantra, it says the passage beginning "The yogin who recites this seed." "Binding" is merely an illustration; the meaning is therefore that if one recites this mantra, all actions will be accomplished.

In order to teach the mudrā, it says the passage beginning "The two hands."

"Having interlaced the fingers of the two hands"

What remains to be supplied is this: first the hands are to be bound in the vajra binding, and after that they are to be made into two vajra fists.

"Join it as though eating."

The meaning is that after that the left fist is to be shown toward the mouth as though eating. If one asks what is to be done with the right fist, it says the passage beginning "The wrathful one and."

"The wrathful one and the middle one are extended"

means that the index finger and the middle finger are extended. The passage beginning "The two should rub against each other" is easy to understand. The defining mark of the fist is that the thumb is placed inside and pressed down by the remaining fingers.

"The enemy's throat and so forth"

By the words "and so forth" the ears, the nose and the like are to be included; for that very reason it says "all things." "Mind" means that all of these are performed by the mind; the meaning is that cutting off the head and the rest are to be enacted and completed by the mind.

In the passage beginning "Then," "all of water and the rest" means waves moving on water, rain falling and so forth. "Binding" means suppressing.

"And whatever third letter
of the last group is to be taken"

Here the last group is the pa group, because it is the last of the five groups beginning with ka. Its third letter is ba.

"Likewise with the very third letter
of the third group"

means the letter ḍa.

"For the third seed of that"

By the words "the third seed," the letter va is to be taken. It says "of that" because ba and va are alike.

"Join the e on top."

This is easy to understand. By these it becomes ba ḍa ve.

"For the first of the fifth group"

means the letter pa.

"It should be pierced with the hook."

The word "hook" is to be taken as the short letter ī, and "piercing" means joining it on top of that letter.

"Making the letter of the strong dot the middle"

Here the letter of the strong dot is va, and "making it the middle" means pronouncing it after that.

"At the end, join the one with the vajra."

Here the one with the vajra is the letter ha.

"The repha, with the hook at its side"

This means joining both: ra is attached below, and at its side the hook, that is, the long ī, is joined.

"And at the end the fierce letter"

means joining the letter phaṭ at the end.

"At the beginning, place the homage."

This is easy to understand. Put together, it becomes oṃ ba ḍa ve pi va hrī phaṭ.

"It is renowned as the mantra of binding."

The meaning is that this is the mantra for binding rain and the like.

The mudrā is taught in the passage beginning "The two hands."

"Having joined the two hands palm to palm"

is easy to understand.

"The two ring fingers are twined around each other."

This means inserting the two ring fingers as far as their roots.

"They should be pressed down by the two wrathful ones."

This means hooking the two ring fingers from their tips with the two index fingers.

"The two thumbs are joined in the middle."

This means pressing the tips of the two thumbs against the roots of the two middle fingers. The remainder is easy to understand.

"It burns up the hosts of cloud gods."

This means that the cloud gods and the rest become bound. The remainder too is easy to understand.

As for the passage beginning "the Self Seat": what is taught in the four divisions, whose subject matter is deity yoga and the rest, is the chapter called the Self Seat. "Complete" means brought to its end.

This concludes the fourth division of the Self Seat, from the commentary on the Glorious Catuṣpīṭha called Smṛtinibandha.

Now, in the Seat of the Other, in order to teach the fire offerings of pacifying and the rest, the giving of torma, and the characteristics of a disciple, the first division is spoken, in the passage beginning "Of the deity."

"What is the form of the deity like?"

This means: in the rite of the fire offering for pacifying and the rest, what is the form of the deity, that is, the deity yoga, like? The words to be supplied are "What should it be seen to be like?"

"And the fire offerings of pacifying and the rest"

means the rite of the fire offering for pacifying and the rest, and it is connected with "How is it done?" In "joined with torma," the torma is what forms a limb of the fire offering, and "joined" means applied: the deity yoga at the time of giving torma. "How is it done?" is connected with the earlier words, "I wish to hear from the Blessed One." Alternatively it may be construed as: "How is the rite of the fire offering for pacifying and the rest performed? What is the application of the deity's form like? How are the tormas to be given?" By the words "and the rest," "How are the characteristics of a disciple to be seen?" is included.

In order to give the answer, it says the passage beginning "Form and the rest." "Form" means the application of the deity's form. "Contemplating" means meditating. By "the rest," torma and so forth are to be included. "One-pointed" means with undistracted mind, and it is connected with "Listen."

"The mantras and mudrās of the fire offering"

Here the mantras and mudrās are the collection of mantras and mudrās that appear below. If one asks what they belong to, it says "of the fire offering": they are those connected with the rite of the fire offering.

"The recitations and the meditations of dhyāna"

Here recitation is the explanation of "mantra," and the meditation of dhyāna is the explanation of "form." It is construed as: "Listen to the meditative concentration on the deity, the recitation of mantra and the binding of mudrās at the time of the fire offering."

"Beforehand, for the fire action and the rest,
the intelligent one should know the recitation."

Here the fire action is the action of the fire offering. "The intelligent one" means one endowed with intelligence. This is construed both ways, each as the beginning and end of the other: the intelligent mantra practitioner should know that recitation comes before the fire action, and should also know that the fire action is to be performed before the action of recitation.

"Immediately after the ground has been purified,
one should diligently make the characteristics of the platform."

First, for the sake of the fire offering, the ground is purified by the mudrās and other means that appear below. Then, as for "the characteristics of the platform," together with "One should diligently make the hearth in its proper nature," the meaning is that it should be dug by one who is trained in the characteristics of the hearth.

"Beginning with eight finger-widths and so forth,
take it as far as a single cubit."

This means taking it from eight finger-widths up to one cubit. Or else it is construed in two ways: beginning from eight finger-widths, "as far as" is taken up to one cubit; and beginning from one cubit, "as far as" is taken up to a thousand cubits.

By the passage beginning "The eighth destroys the enemy," the different measures of the hearth are matched with the particular actions. Here "the eighth" is the measure of eight finger-widths. "Destroying the enemy" means performing the fire offering of the fierce rite; the meaning is that it is to be performed in that hearth.

"Likewise, with the tenth there is increase."

This means that for the fire offering of increasing, a hearth of ten finger-widths is to be made.

"With the twelfth one brings under control."

This means that for the fire offering of subjugating, a fire hearth of twelve finger-widths is to be made.

"Likewise, with the fourteenth, pacifying itself."

This means that for the fire offering that removes illness and the like, a fire hearth of fourteen finger-widths is to be made.

"By a hearth measuring sixteen finger-widths
one makes the lineage flourish."

This means that a fire hearth of sixteen finger-widths is to be made in order to bring about the birth of a lineage of sons, grandsons and so forth that has not yet been born, and to make one already born endure.

"By a place of eighteen finger-widths
herds of cattle and the rest flourish."

Here "place" means the hearth. By the words "and the rest" in "herds of cattle and the rest," horses, elephants and so forth are included. The remainder is easy to understand.

"Of the width divided into three parts,
two parts are to be dug."

The meaning is that the measure of the width given before is divided into three parts, one part is set aside, and two parts are dug in depth. This is the characteristic common to all hearths.

"With an eighth part of what has been dug"

means with an eighth part of what has been dug in depth. If one asks what is to be done, it says:

"The rim of the pit should be made."

That is, it is made at the mouth of the hearth.

"With half the rim
an outer border should be made."

This means that outside the rim it is surrounded by a border measuring half of that.

"On the border, beyond eight parts,
the lotus petals, eight parts of that"

The meaning is that the border is divided into eight parts, the area within one part is made the hub, and what remains of the eight parts is made into lotus petals. Alternatively, "On the border, beyond eight parts" means that the border of the hearth is divided into nine parts, and with the outer, ninth part the border of the lotus is made.

"The lotus petals, eight parts of that"

means that the petals of that lotus are made eight in number. If one asks where, it says: "Outside the lotus." If one asks how large, it says the passage beginning "Of one finger-width." "A fence is to be made" means that the lotus petals themselves are a fence, since they resemble a fence.

"Within the hearth the vajras
are to be placed upon the lotus."

This means that the emblem of Vajrasattva, the vajra, and the emblems of the ḍākinīs are drawn upon the lotus inside the hearth.

In order to teach the different shapes according to the different actions, it says the passage beginning "For the fierce rite." The meaning is that for the action of the fierce rite the shape is to be made triangular. The remainder is easy to understand. "Likewise for increasing" is joined with "entirely round." "Half-moon" means like the shape of the moon on the eighth day. "Eight-cornered" means having eight facets like a jewel.

"They should be applied in due order:
pacifying, increasing, subjugating and the rest."

This sums up the text; the meaning is that the actions of pacifying and the rest are to be applied in hearths that are square and so forth, in the order just stated.

In order to explain the passage beginning "Within the hearth," it says:

"The emblems of the five buddhas
are to be set out in their proper directions."

"Buddhas" means Vajrasattva and the ḍākinīs, or else the wisdom ḍākinī and the rest. As for "are to be set out in their proper directions": in the center is the vajra, and on the four petals of the directions are the khaṭvāṅgas.

"In the four places of the rim"

means in the four directional parts of the rim, east and the rest.

"It should be adorned with the emblems of the buddhas."

This means that the wheel, the jewel, the lotus and the sword are drawn there in order.

"Most beautiful with half-moon and vajra"

means the jewel and the vajra resting upon a half-moon.

"They should be placed all around, in corner after corner."

This means they are drawn outside, in the four corners of the outer edge.

"Likewise at the place of the seat,
at twelve finger-widths,
endowed with half a cubit in height,
a beautiful Kailāsa is to be placed."

Here "the place of the seat" means toward the left edge of the master's seat. "Kailāsa" is merely the name by which it is known. "In height" is illustrative: in height, and in length and breadth. "Endowed with half a cubit" is the measure: a half cubit endowed with a half. By this it comes to two and a half spans. "At twelve finger-widths" means that in front of it an earthen step of twelve finger-widths is to be made.

"In the places of the eight petals
the goddesses are to be set out in order."

Here the eight petals are the petals of the outer border. "The goddesses" means the emblems of the goddesses, and "setting them out" means drawing them in order. On the eastern petal is the emblem of Ḍākinī, the two hands put into the mouth. On the north is the emblem of the Blazing Lady, the palms joined at the crown of the head.

On the west is the emblem of the Sucking Lady, drinking blood from the cupped palms. On the south is the emblem of the Ravisher, the fingers made in the manner of a pestle. In the four intermediate directions, beginning with Īśāna, the emblems of Siṃhinī, Vyāghrī, Jambukī and Ulūkī are to be drawn simply as hooks.

To teach the colors of the emblems, it says:

"The yogin should apply their measure
with distinction."

"Their measure" means the emblems, because they are measured and set up in the respective hearths; this is the distinction of the rites of pacifying and so on. "Applying" means making them in that color.

In the passage beginning oṃ hili, what is called "the articles of use" means the mantras for purifying and sprinkling the substances of the burnt offering and so on.

To teach that, for the rites beginning with the purification of the ground, one meditates on oneself as the wisdom ḍākinī, it says "wisdom" and so on. "One's own body" means the body of the master.

"By meditating as the wisdom ḍākinī"

is to be connected with

"The words should be emanated as deities."

To teach the rite, it says:

"In my eight limbs,
by setting them all out, protection is to be made."

This is the summary. To explain it, it says "on the tongue also" and so on. In "on the tongue, this very water," "this very" means the nectar, and it is called "water" because it is of the nature of water. Its purifying seed, suṃ, is also called by that name. In

"Space, on the ears and nose,"

the reality of space is the seed yuṃ. "Earth" is hūṃ, the seed of purified earth. "Fire" is hūṃ, the seed of purified fire. "Brahmā" is the upper abode of Brahmā, from the palate up to the crown of the head. These are to be known, in order, as the seeds whose nature is Vajraḍākinī, Vetālī, Caṇḍālī and the Fierce Lady.

In the passage beginning "throat," "confusion" means delusion, and its purifying seed is smryuṃ. Since that is the nature of the goddess Siṃhinī, the leader, it is to be applied at the nape of the neck. "Moving" means wind, and its purifying seed is ymryuṃ. "Affliction" means hatred, and its purifying seed is hmryuṃ. "Thongka" means the upper arm. "Livestock" means the owl, and its seed is kṣmryuṃ. These, in order, are the seeds whose nature is Siṃhinī, Vyāghrī, Jambukī and Ulūkī.

For that very reason it says:

"This protection of the eight limbs,
having understood it as the deities,"

which means that one should regard the seeds of the armor as having the nature of the goddesses. How so? It says "three banners" and so on. The mudrā of the three banners is made by pressing the nail of the little finger with the thumb and extending the remaining three fingers.

"Touching the eight limbs, protect."

This means touching with that mudrā the eight places, beginning with the crown of the head, and setting there the seeds mentioned before.

Now, to arouse recollection of the wisdom ḍākinī mentioned above, and to teach that one also meditates on the wisdom ḍākinī at the time of purifying the ground, it says "wisdom" and so on.

"As having the nature of the wisdom ḍākinī"

is connected with

"One should meditate on one's own body."

How so? It says:

"Having emanated my body,"

that is, by transforming my body. The passage beginning kunda is easy to understand. In

"In the hands, khaṭvāṅga and vajra,"

the vajra is in the right hand and the khaṭvāṅga in the left. "In meditative equipoise" means with one's back turned to the world and to its phenomena.

"One should meditate with breath unmoving"

has already been explained. In

"Then, having purified the ground,"

"then" means sequence: first, having meditated on one's own body as the deity, one purifies the ground, and after that one should dig the hearth. That is the meaning.

In the passage beginning oṃ:

"Stretching out the right hand,
visualizing a vajra, strike the ground."

This means: endowed with the yoga of oneself as the deity, one visualizes in the center of the fully outstretched right hand a five-pronged vajra arisen from the syllable hūṃ, and with that hand holding the vajra one strikes the places of the five ḍākinīs while reciting this mantra, and meditates on the ground as having the nature of vajra. That is the meaning.

To teach the purification of the substances, it says:

"Joining the two fists face to face,"

and so on. Its meaning is this: joining the fists of the two hands face to face, one extends the two middle fingers and touches their tips together, displays it, and holds it.

In "This mudrā is the purifier," "this" means the one just described. "The purifier" means the common mudrā that purifies all the substances, such as the butter, that appear below. The mantras, however, are to be applied to each one separately. The mantras stated there, from "oṃ śriṃ svāhā is the mantra for purifying the butter" up to "the mantra for purifying the kindling sticks," are easy to understand.

To teach the rite for generating the fire god, it says:

"Having imagined a lotus in the center of the fire,"

and so on. There, "having imagined a lotus in the center of the fire" is the statement, and the passage beginning "rosary" is the explanation.

"The god of fire is to be summoned"

teaches the rite of the second being.

The passage beginning "the left fist" is the explanation. In the center of the hearth one kindles a fire in the customary way. In the center of the fire is a lotus, and in the center of the lotus, from the transformation of raṃ, is the fire god seated on a sun, with a topknot of tawny matted locks, reddish-yellow in color like molasses, holding a rosary in his right hand and a ladle in his left, dwelling amid tumultuous blazing flames. Having imagined him thus, then, as to

"Having bound the left fist,"

one places the fist of the left hand on the layer of kuśa grass outside the hearth, extends the index and middle fingers, bends the tip of the index finger and sets it against the third joint of the middle finger; and with this beckoning mudrā and with this mantra, oṃ āgaccha āgaccha agne jaḥ svāhā, one summons the wisdom fire god and offers the argha and the rest. Then, as it says,

"Seated upon a lotus seat,
he is to be visualized in the center of the hearth,"

he dissolves into the pledge fire god, and one should meditate on the two as nondual.

Then, to teach the offering, it says "there" and so on. "There" is to be connected as follows: having summoned him by the sequence stated before, offered the argha, the water for the feet and so on, and dissolved him so that he is nondual with the pledge fire god, one worships him with the five things, from flowers and the like, as available, up to the food offering, and then makes the burnt offerings three times each. Where? It says,

"In the center of the fire, on the tongue of the fire god,"

which is easy to understand. From what? "From all," that is, from all the substances of the burnt offering.

In the passage beginning "the two hands,"

"The middle ones are to be fully extended"

means that the two middle fingers are stretched well out and raised. "Slightly parted" means that the remaining fingers are loosely joined together. "In the manner of a lotus" means that, showing this mudrā of the opened lotus to the fire god, one is to please him with the mantra oṃ agne juhū juhū svāhā.

What is to be done after that? It says, "Then, with effort, snapping the fingers three times, meditate on his dissolving into the blaze." The meaning is that one snaps the fingers three times and meditates that the invited fire god dissolves into the form of the blazing fire and becomes of one taste with it.

"In the center of the fire, through the all-knowing one,
then the purification is to be begun."

This means that, having visualized one's chosen deity in the center of the fire, at the time of performing the fire offering one begins the purification by sprinkling and cleansing all the substances of the burnt offering.

In the passage beginning oṃ, which states this, oṃ hūṃ ṭa ṭi phaṭ is the mantra. As for

"With the hand that bears the vajra,
with water with flowers and mustard seed,
having sprinkled in all directions,
one should pour it into the fire,"

"with the hand that bears the vajra" means with the hand that holds the vajra. "With water with flowers and mustard seed" means with water containing flowers and white mustard seed. "Having sprinkled in all directions" means sprinkling it over all the substances and sprinkling it toward the directions and intermediate directions of the fire. "One should pour it into the fire" is easy to understand. The purification is the purification of the fire and the substances. The mantra is this very mantra. The mudrā is the mudrā that purifies the substances, given before in the passage beginning "joining the two fists face to face." The passage beginning oṃ vajra prākāra is easy to understand.

"Stretching out the two hands,
make their tips touch one another."

This means forming the mudrā of the cupped palms above the head, with the fingertips just touching. The rest is also easy to understand.

"Having clenched the left fist,
the thumb is to be extended downward."

This means that one clenches the left hand into a fist, turns it over, and plants the tip of the thumb on the ground. The rest is easy to understand.

To teach that, having made the fire offering to the fire god, one makes the fire offering to the chosen deity, it says "of the sun" and so on. "The color red and so on" means that, according to the kind of rite, the colors white and the rest are also to be adopted. "The seat of union" means sitting in the cross-legged posture of the sattva. "One should meditate in the color of the rite" is the explanation of "the color red and so on." The rest is easy to understand.

The meaning of these is as follows. In the center of the fire, upon a lotus, is a sun disk, and upon it, from the transformation of hūṃ, a five-pronged vajra marked at its hub with the seed hūṃ. From the complete transformation of all that, one meditates on one's chosen deity, the wisdom ḍākinī or another, red or of another color, clad in ornaments and garments, holding a vajra and a khaṭvāṅga in the right and left hands, seated in the cross-legged posture of the sattva. This is the meditation.

To teach the accomplishment of the wisdom being, it says:

"Having generated the form through the vajra,
the one arisen from my own breath,
into whatever is my body,
the wisdom ḍākinī is to be dissolved."

"Arisen from my breath" means the wisdom being, in female form, emanated from my breath. "Whatever is my body" means the pledge being, in female form, generated within the fire, called "my body" because she is inseparable from my own nature. The rest is easy to understand.

In oṃ ṭha ṭha, hūṃ is to be pronounced in place of ṭha; thus it becomes oṃ hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ svāhā. "The stick" means the pouring ladle. "The vessel" means the filling ladle. The mantra stated for purifying the substances is oṃ hūṃ svāhā. To show that this is the root mantra, it says, "the root mantra and the mudrā." By "the mudrā" is meant that very mudrā which purifies the substances, given in the passage beginning

"Joining the two fists face to face."

Just as, with this mudrā joined to the respective mantras, one purifies the substances for the fire offering to the fire god, so too, with this root mantra joined to it, one should purify the substances for the fire offering to the chosen deity.

To teach the mantras and mudrās of the various offerings, it says "the right hand" and so on, which is easy to understand. "Extended from that same" means clenching the fist and extending the three fingers as if releasing them. For that very reason it says, "Below, from that same fist." "From the flower mudrā" means clenching the fist and raising the middle finger. The rest is easy to understand. Extending both hands, setting the two thumbs at the base of the middle fingers, and "moving" is the torma mudrā; these too are easy to understand.

"Within the mouth of the fire,
reciting once, giving once."

This means that, reciting once the full-ladle mantra, oṃ hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ svāhā, one should offer the full oblation into the mouth of the fire god, one time each. As for

"In the center the portion is to be burned,"

"in the center" means into the mouth of one's own chosen deity. "The portion" is a synonym for the full oblation.

"Taking all that exceeds the seven"

means that first one gives the seven full oblations, and then the remainder, namely the five full-ladle offerings of the concluding fire offering, are to be taken up.

"With effort, at the end of their completion,
the remaining things are to be burned."

The meaning is this: first, with effort, one offers a full ladle once into the mouth of the fire god, and after that one offers the burnt oblations of the remaining things, that is, of the other substances; and likewise to one's chosen deity too, one first offers a full ladle and, at the end of that, offers the burnt oblations of the remainder.

What these lines say is as follows. First one gives one full ladle into the mouth of the fire, then gives the burnt oblations of all the substances, and after that again gives one full ladle. Then, again within the fire, one makes one fire offering into the mouth of one's chosen deity, offers the burnt oblations of the remainder, and at the end of that again offers one full ladle and asks forbearance. Once more one visualizes the god of fire, gives one full ladle into his mouth, gives the burnt oblations of the remainder as before, and after that again gives one full ladle. At the end of all this, one has the patron give one full ladle into the mouth of the fire with his own hand. With that, the seven kinds of full-ladle offering are complete. This is what is found in the Four Seats.

"When the burning is finished, one should meditate
that the deities dissolve into one's own body."

This means that, when the fire offering is complete, one withdraws one's chosen deity and meditates that it dissolves into one's own body.

"In the completion of all the full ladles,
the mantra and mudrā are to be completed."

The meaning is that the mantra and the mudrā are to be joined to every one of the seven full ladles.

Now, to teach the concluding fire offering with its five full ladles, it says "the vessel" and so on. The meaning is this: when the fire offering with its seven full ladles is complete, one again visualizes one's chosen deity in the center of the fire, joins mouth to mouth the large and small ladles filled with butter, and offers one full ladle; then one offers the burnt oblations, and at the end one again offers a full ladle. Some say that after that one also gives two full ladles to the fire.

"Having drawn it forth, become the substance of the breath,
it is to be dissolved into one's own body.
Then, as one's very own body,
the wise one should meditate upon it."

This is the request for one's chosen deity to depart, and it is simply understood from the words.

"In the center, the god of fire himself
the wise one, having imagined, also,"

This means that, after one's chosen deity has been withdrawn into one's own center, one performs the cleansing sprinkling and so on for the fire, and visualizes the fire god in the center in the manner taught before. In

"Having burned the remainder three times,"

one gives three full oblations.

"Offerings and torma are to be given"

means that one gives the offerings, the torma and so on, and asks forbearance.

"Having sung the song of auspiciousness,
there the mudrā is to be formed."

This means forming the mudrā accompanied by the song of auspiciousness. The mudrā here, too, is the lotus mudrā, described in the passage beginning

"Having joined the two palms together."

So first there are the seven fire offerings of full ladles, and after that five more; one should understand that taken together these come to twelve.

"The other fire offerings, divided by rite,
are explained in brief, not at length."

The meaning is that the substances, divided according to the kinds of rite, such as pacifying, are to be explained in brief.

The passage beginning sālu is merely to be understood. To teach the kindling sticks and firewood, it says, "From the five trees with milky sap." The five, amra, bata, nyagrodha, barkata and aśvattha, are the five trees with milky sap.

"Of a length of fourteen finger-widths"

teaches the particular kindling sticks.

"When the time for pacifying has come"

up to "is the chief," means that whenever one performs pacifying, burning the substances just mentioned is the supreme form of the rite, that is, the principal one.

To teach the substances for increasing, it says "black sesame" and so on, which is merely to be understood. "Kindling sticks" stands for the whole class: firewood too is to be taken from those same trees. The passage up to

"The practitioner should burn the special ones"

refers to the burnt-offering substances that are not shared, being divided by the kinds of rite.

To teach the substances for the rite of subjugation, it says

"Sesame, red and black,"

and so on: that is, black sesame, and red, where "red" applies to red flowers, not to sesame. The passage beginning nāgagesar is easy to understand. In the passage beginning "pine," "nāga tree" means the nāgagesar tree. "The rite of attachment" means the rites of bringing under control and of summoning.

To teach the substances for fierce rites, it says "black sesame" and so on.

"balataga, and the fruit of the great black one"

is to be construed as two things: the fruit of the balataki and the fruit called "great black." "Blood" means any ordinary blood. In the passage beginning "lungtang," "the wood of the crooked one" means the tree known as the one with crooked thorns. The rest is merely to be understood.

To teach the substances for the rite of expulsion, it says "white mustard seed" and so on. "Golden tree" means dadura. Here too the length of the kindling sticks is not stated, because it depends on the length given for fierce rites. The rest, too, is merely to be understood.

To teach the substances for the rite of binding, it says "fish" and so on, which is merely to be understood. Here too the length of the kindling sticks is as before.

Accordingly, pine, nyagrodha, the nāga tree, baśaka, lungtang, the crooked one, balataka, the golden tree, twigs from a crow's nest, and tooth-sticks are taught as the kindling sticks and firewood for building up the fire, and the remaining items should be understood to be the other substances.

Now, to teach the fire offering for the enjoyment of kingship, it says "then" and so on. "Especially" means that it is a particular case among the six rites mentioned before; or else, in relation to the ordinary rites of increasing and of subjugation, the rite for the enjoyment of kingship is called "especial."

"Having entered equipoise in its center,
if one makes aspiration and bathes,"

"In its center" means in the place of the fire offering. "Having entered equipoise" means with a mind undistracted from its focus on kingship and the like. Making aspiration, too, means making aspiration for the sake of kingship and so on. Bathing means washing with the water of the vase of Vajrasattva. The rest is merely to be understood. "The three grounds" means the three worlds: below the earth, upon the earth, and in the sky.

To teach another method, it says "butter" and so on, which is easy to understand. "Becomes glorious" means abiding in the rank of kingship. To show just that, it says:

"Whatever deed one has in mind,
all of it will be accomplished here,"

because it is not fitting for anyone else.

Now, to teach the characteristics of the disciple, it says "then" and so on. "Fully accept" means one is to take up or accept. "All the limbs" means the hands, the eyes and so on; or else, the components of the characteristics fit for being a disciple. For that very reason it says, "Disciplined, of peaceful conduct," and so on. "Disciplined" means having tamed the senses. "Of peaceful conduct" means conducting oneself well, that is, knowing the treatises, such as the treatises on grammar.

"And devoted to the three Dharmas"

means having faith in the Dharma of the three vehicles: the vehicle of the śrāvakas, that of the pratyekabuddhas, and that of great awakening.

"Ever delighting in the Great Vehicle"

means that, even while abiding in the vows of the śrāvakas, one is never separated from the generation of the mind of the Great Vehicle. As it is also said:

"Abiding in the vows of the śrāvakas,
one should generate the mind of the Great Vehicle."

"The application of mantra" means being properly equipped with the mantras of pacifying and the rest. "The application of tantra" means applications such as drawing yantras for the rites of pacifying and the rest.

"Skilled in all crafts and arts"

and what follows has already been explained.

"Delighting in the practice of meditative concentration"

means having faith in the cultivation of meditative concentration.

"Respectful toward hearing, reading and so on"

means having respect for hearing, that is, listening to, the scriptures of the Sugata, for reading them, and, as indicated by "and so on," for reciting them and the like.

"At all times respectful toward the guru"

means always attending upon the guru with the actions of body, speech and mind.

"Diligent in the fire offering and in virtuous deeds"

Here, by "and so on," gentle speech and the like are to be included.

Having thus taught the factors of virtue, to teach freedom from the factors of wrongdoing, it says, from

"Not delighting in sensual pleasures,"

up to

"Not of bad character, not harsh of speech."

These are to be connected with the preceding line,

"These are the characteristics of a disciple to be accepted."

To teach the characteristics opposed to these, it says, "Reviling all sentient beings," and so on, which is easy to understand.

"Such ones are to be rejected"

sums up the characteristics. The meaning is that others not mentioned, such as the miserly, the lazy and the like, are to be included as well.

In the passage beginning "the Seat of the Other," the acts other than one's own body, such as the fire offering, are "the other," and "seat" means place. The text that sets forth clearly the rites of the fire offering and so on is the chapter.

From the Smṛtinibandha, a commentary on the Glorious Catuṣpīṭha, the first section of the Seat of the Other.

Now, to teach the section on the water offering, it says "of water" and so on. "Fire offering" here means a sacrificial offering. "A marvel" means it has become an object of wonder, because just as merit is obtained through the fire offering, so merit is also obtained through the water offering. To set forth just that, it says "of water" and so on; the words "and so on" take in the burnt-offering substances, the hearth and the rest. "Characteristic" means nature.

In reply it says "water and so on" and so on. "The true characteristic" means the nature of reality. For that very reason it says "reality," which is easy to understand. Here, the reality of the water is that it is free of living creatures, clean and clear. By "and so on," the other realities are to be included as well, such as the reality of the vase and the like, namely that its base has no black streaks and so on.

"Having hung canopies,
one examines carefully the various cloths."

This means hanging canopies in the place where the fire offering is performed, and arranging banners, cloths and so on.

"Very spacious and square,
it is to be smeared all over with fragrance."

"Very spacious" is explained in the oral instructions as to be made four cubits in size. "Square" is easy to understand. "All over" means level. The meaning is: in a pleasant place secluded from people, on level ground, one smears it with cow dung, smears a square with sandalwood and the like, and then hangs the canopies and the rest.

"In the center, the vessel of water and so on,"

"In the center" means in the center of the maṇḍala just described. "The vessel of water" means an earthen vase free of faults such as a black base, with a mouth measuring fourteen finger-widths and a depth of two thirds of that, shaped like the hearth for pacifying. "Is to be placed" is the part to be supplied.

"Having smeared it with white fragrance"

means smearing it with white sandalwood.

"With water mixed with milk
the beautiful vessel is to be filled."

The water is of the characteristics mentioned before.

"Mixed with milk" is easy to understand. "The beautiful vessel" has the characteristics mentioned before. Having taught the central vase, in order to set out the vases of the directions and intermediate directions it says "the vases" and so on, which is easy to understand. "Scented water" means water with fragrant scents. The rest is simply understood from the words.

"Having generated oneself in an instant
as the wisdom ḍākinī,"

means meditating on oneself as the wisdom ḍākinī with three faces and so on, as described below.

"With things such as flowers, incense and fragrance,
having worshiped oneself,"

means that one should worship with substances such as flowers the circle of goddesses arisen within the vase, who resemble oneself. To teach just that, it says "after that" and so on. "After that" means after generating oneself.

"The water rites are to be applied"

means that, having meditated on the goddesses within the water of the vessel, one performs the fire offering; this is the summary. The explanation is given in the passage beginning "the two hands."

"Having joined the two palms together,
part them slightly in the manner of a lotus."

This is the lotus mudrā, which is easy to understand. The mantra stated is oṃ kamalabhe svāhā. The meaning is that one shows this mantra and mudrā to the vases and meditates on a lotus within the water. For that very reason it says:

"A lotus adorned with a moon disk
is set within the water."

"In the center, covered by a white parasol,
the seed is to be visualized."

This means visualizing, upon the lotus and moon disk, the seed hūṃ with its parasol, which is the very essence of one's own life breath; or else, which has arisen from one's own breath. For that very reason it says:

"From the seed that arose
from one's own breath, generate the form."

This is easy to understand. What is it like? It says "the cross-legged posture of the sattva" and so on.
