The meaning is this: from the complete transformation of the seed, one visualizes the wisdom ḍākinī, two-armed, with three faces, laughing, wrathful and amorous, holding in her two hands a staff marked with a vajra, seated in the cross-legged posture of the sattva, adorned with all ornaments, white in color, and wearing exceedingly beautiful garments.

In the passage beginning "the pledge mudrā," the pledge mudrā is described in

"Binding the two hands into fists against each other,"

and what follows. "Against each other" means back to back.

"The two index fingers are to be knotted"

means that their tips are knotted together. The meaning of these lines, too, is that one imagines the wisdom being, in female form, arisen from one's own breath, shows the pledge mantra and mudrā, and dissolves her nondually into the pledge being, in female form.

To teach that the yoginīs are also set out in order in the vases of the east and the other directions, it says,

"In the eastern vase, the Vajra Lady,"

and so on, which is easy to understand.

"Each one's own sign and
pledge mudrā is to be shown."

This is to be construed by dividing it into two, the sign mudrā and the pledge mudrā, which are to be shown respectively to the pledge beings and to the wisdom beings. The meaning is this: in the vases of the east and the other directions, one beholds the assembly of yoginīs, the Vajra Lady and the rest, arisen from their respective seeds, and shows to each of the yoginīs the sign mudrā described below, that is, the root mudrā. In the same way, one shows to the assembly of wisdom beings the pledge mudrā mentioned before, each in turn, and meditates that they dissolve nondually.

What is done after that? It says "flowers" and so on. "By the wise one" means by one who does not conceive of distinctions in the offerings and the like.

To teach the root mudrā, it says "the vajra fist" and so on.

"Having placed the left at the heart"

means placing the left fist at the heart, facing upward.

"With the right, cover it from above"

means placing the right on top of it, facing downward.

"oṃ jūṃ hūṃ svāhā is the root mantra" means that it is the root mantra of the goddesses. With that mantra one shows the mudrā; by reciting this same mantra three hundred thousand times one should also carry out the preliminary approach; and with this same mantra one should also present the sacrificial offering.

"White flowers such as kunda"

means that white flowers, or any others that are suitable, are to be taken.

"Having set dūrva grass at the tip,"

means inserting the tip of the dūrva grass from the base of the flower so that it comes out as far as the tip of the flower; and with this, one presents the sacrificial offering to the goddesses visualized within the vase, using the mantra just given. Where is it to be offered? It says:

"Upon the goddess's head, on the vajra
crown, it is to be strewn."

This is easy to understand. How is it to be offered? In

"Having recited the secret mantra once each,
it is to be offered with one application each time,"

"application" means the flower with its dūrva grass. "Once each" means one for each. "Having recited the secret mantra once each" is easy to understand. How many times? It says, "ten thousand in number." What comes of it? It says:

"One becomes all-knowing and one's life increases."

"All-knowing" means that intelligence, a keen mind and the like increase. This is the rite of increasing.

The rite of pacifying is stated in the passage beginning karbīra.

"Together with dūrva grass"

means joining it with dūrva grass as described before.

"The kindling sticks of nāgapuṣpa"

means that the nāgapuṣpa burnt-offering substance itself also serves as the kindling sticks and firewood.

"The kindling sticks of the lotus"

means the lotus together with dūrva grass. "In all rites" means in whichever rite one wishes, such as increasing. "If it is applied" means if it is presented as a sacrificial offering. "Pacifying the region" stands for the whole class.

"If applied to Rājagṛha and the like,"

Here, the words "and the like" take in ministers and so on.

"The mantrin should always scatter handfuls of water."

The meaning is that, having beheld the Blessed Lady within the vase as described before, one should always offer one hundred and eight handfuls of water every morning with the root mantra.

"In particular, there will be increase"

means that grain and the like will flourish.

In the passage beginning "the Seat of the Other," the text by which the water fire offering and the like are made fully clear is the chapter.

From the Smṛtinibandha, the commentary on the Glorious Catuṣpīṭha: the second section, that of the Seat of the Other.

Now, in order to teach clearly deity yoga, the commitments, and so on, the third section is spoken, beginning with the words "the far shore of saṃsāra." Here, although no request about deity yoga and the like has been made, the Teacher keeps in mind the request made earlier and, by way of another approach, teaches its limbs and subsidiary limbs. For it is said:

"Relying on another topic,
he teaches that very topic."

"The characteristic that carries across to the far shore of saṃsāra" is to be construed as: "Listen to the characteristic of the nondual Dharma by which one is brought to the far shore of saṃsāra."

To make deity yoga clear, that characteristic of nonduality is taught, beginning with the words "of entity and." "Of entity" means whatever exists as an entity. "Non-entity" means what does not exist as an entity. "Liberated" means having passed beyond these. For thus, when examined by valid cognition, an entity cannot truly exist, and so it is liberated from entity. If an entity is not established, then its nonexistence is not established either, because that depends on it. Or else, if the property-bearer does not exist, how could its property, nonexistence, be possible? Therefore it is liberated from non-entity.

"Pure and impure are completely abandoned": pure means free of affliction; impure means accompanied by affliction; completely abandoned means having become far removed from them. For thus, what is pure by nature is nondual wisdom, while the afflictions are adventitious.

Well then, is what is called "nondual" some essence free of the two, or is there some one thing in which the two have become one? To show that it is neither, the text says:

"Because the two entities are completely abandoned,
it is called the nondual Dharma."

When entity and the rest do not exist, how could one view an essence liberated from them as existing as an entity, or view as existing those third alternatives in which the two have become one? This is established by one's own valid cognition. As it is also said:

"Existence and nonexistence: this too is an extreme.
Pure and impure: this too is an extreme.
Therefore, having abandoned both extremes,
the wise do not dwell even in the middle."

"The nondual Dharma" is to be construed either as "all phenomena are nondual" or as "the nondual nature of reality." The word "called" marks it as a designation, a conventional expression; it is a conventional expression because of clinging. If one asks of what it is the conventional expression: it is the conventional expression for "the two entities are completely abandoned," that is, for liberation from the four extremes of existence and the rest. That too is what is stated in the words:

"All phenomena are nondual,
and the characteristics of phenomena are abandoned."

By this, all phenomena are shown to be emptiness, because they lack intrinsic nature. By that very point they should also be understood to be signless and wishless. In that regard, since beings have no cause, non-arising itself is signlessness. As it is also said:

"Not from self, not from other,
not from both, and not without a cause:
no entities whatsoever, anywhere,
ever arise at all."

If, being unborn, there is no essential nature, what would one aspire to? For that very reason it is also wishless.

To show that deity yoga too is nondual in that very way, the text says, beginning:

"Form is empty: beginning and culmination,
and middle, abide in equality."

"Form" means the aggregate of form; by way of implication, it takes in everything up to the elements and so on. "Empty" means the nature of the three doors of liberation. For that very reason the text says "beginning and culmination" and so on; this is easy to understand. Or else, the beginning is while one is undertaking the deity yoga; the middle is while one is completing it; the end is while it is transforming. "Abide in equality" means culminating in being empty and nondual.

In the lines:

"The pure and the impure, the middle:
some view them as equal,"

the impure is what is accompanied by craving; the pure is the opposite of that. The middle is what is free of the extremes of pure and impure, equal in a single taste. "Solely" means nonduality. "View" means taking that as one's object. Or else, the pure is the clean, and the impure is the other. The rest is as explained before.

To explain that very point further, the text says:

"What is to be relied on and not relied on, of the middle."

"To be relied on" means to be traversed; "not relied on" means not to be traversed. The middle is the abandoning of the two, and that itself is equality. To show this, the text says, beginning with "wisdom": "wisdom" means mind. "Arisen from" means that the self-aware mind too culminates in equality. "The body abides in equality" means that, in the manner stated before, it culminates in the nature of reality as equality. "Speech too, having abided in equality" means that speech too reaches equality. The meaning of these is as follows. Just as the mind culminates in equality because the aspects of cognizer and cognized are not possible, so too does the body. Just as the body, broken down into the tiniest particles, abides nowhere at all, so too does the mind. And just as body and mind are without intrinsic nature, so too speech, like an echo, is ultimately without intrinsic nature and culminates in equality. This is the meaning. As it is also said: "Generate the mind as like the body, the body as like the mind, speech as like the mind, and the mind as like verbal expression."

If one asks what comes of these, the text says:

"Arisen from the wisdom of equality,
one should view with the view of equality."

This is easy to understand.

Again a request is made, beginning with "equality." "How" means "how is the view of equality to be generated?" In the lines:

"How should I understand the self
of what is called the nondual Dharma?"

"self" means one's own aggregates and so on.

"The nondual Dharma" is deity yoga free of characteristics. "How should I understand" means "how should I do it directly?"

In the reply, which begins with "nondual," "just as it is, suchness" means unerring and undeceiving. It is construed as: "Listen, O Vajra, to how one who has that characteristic is to be generated." "The two entities" and so on is easy to understand.

In the lines:

"At the beginning of all yogas,
one should recite the three purities,"

"beginning" means first. The meaning is that at the start of every deity yoga one should recite the three mantras that come below. These are taught in the passage beginning svabhāva. svabhāva means the characteristic of true and untrue, permanent and impermanent, existent and nonexistent, what is to be taken up and what is to be discarded, the grasped and the grasper, and so on. śuddha is emptiness, the absence of essence. If one asks of what, it is sarva dharmā: they are called dharmma because they hold the characteristics of arising, abiding and perishing; that is, the aggregates and so on. To show that "just as these are completely pure by essential nature, so too am I like them," the text says svabhāvaśuddho'haṃ. The intention is that, in order to clear away the adventitious stains, one should focus on natural purity. In vajra śuddha, vajra is nondual wisdom, and śuddha means that the grasped and the rest lack essential nature. In yoga śuddha, yoga is deity yoga, and śuddha means free of ordinary nature.

"Having made it unsurpassed,
join the palms and recite them"

means that, in order to realize unsurpassed wisdom directly, one should recite those mantras. It is done as follows. One looks upon one's own heart-essence syllable at one's heart, and by the light rays radiating from that essence one makes the guru and one's own chosen deity appear. One makes offerings to them with the mudrās of flowers and so on, joins the palms, and then confesses misdeeds, rejoices in merit, fully dedicates the merit, goes for refuge to the Three, definitively offers oneself, and vows to follow the path. Then, having meditated on loving-kindness and the rest, one should recite the three purities in order to realize unsurpassed wisdom directly. That is the meaning.

To teach the excellent place, the text says, beginning:

"Of a pleasing place ..."

The meaning is that one should begin the meditative concentration on the deity relying on some place agreeable to the mind, such as a mountain.

The cleansing of the place is taught in the passage beginning "canopy." "Banner" means one without designs of fish, geese and the like; "victory banner" means one with such designs.

"In the ten places all around,
likewise make a maṇḍala of scent"

means that at the time of beginning the meditative concentration one should likewise make a maṇḍala of fragrant scent. "Ten places" means that one makes one large square maṇḍala on the ground, and then, by first scattering scent over it, it becomes a maṇḍala made so as to pervade the ten directions.

To teach that after the offerings and so on one should generate oneself in an instant as the wisdom ḍākinī and carry out the whole purification of the ground and the rest, the text says, beginning with "instant." "Instant" means in a moment. "Wisdom ḍākinī" means solely as the wisdom ḍākinī. "Assumed" means generated. "Delight oneself with offerings" means that one should meditate on the maṇḍala of the deities.

Its sequence is taught in the passage beginning:

"Having placed the hand palm upward ..."

Here, since the oral instruction states, "First, facing west, one should begin the meditative concentration," one first faces west. On the right hand, stretched out palm upward, one visualizes a blazing five-pronged vajra. With it one strikes the center, and then, going counterclockwise, one also strikes with the palm at the four places, east and the rest. Reciting the mantra oṃ hūṃ phaṭ, one visualizes the ground, down to the seventh layer beneath the earth, as having the nature of vajra, and in the manner stated before one should visualize the fence and the rest.

That mantra is taught in the passage beginning "homage." "Destroying" means the syllable phaṭ. The rest is easy to understand.

In the passage beginning "Meru," what is left unstated is this: after that, one properly visualizes, just as one wishes, Mount Meru arisen from the transformation of the syllables oṃ hūṃ svāhā, having the nature of four kinds of precious substance, and on top of it a city made of gold, adorned with seven kinds of precious things; and there one should meditate on the Blessed Lady.

"At the beginning of all mudrās,
one should bind the samaya mudrā"

is construed with "to attend upon." The meaning is that, having visualized the Blessed Lady in the golden city, one should first bind the samaya mudrā and attend upon her. That mudrā is taught:

"Binding the two vajra fists,
placing the left at the heart,
the right is to be put at the armpit:
know this as the samaya mudrā."

This is easy to understand.

The mantra is taught in the passage beginning "homage." "Burning" means svāhā. "Just as" is construed with "one should view it just as it is." If one asks what, it is "the seed of the vajra entity," that is, the syllable hūṃ. Put together, it becomes oṃ hūṃ svāhā. The rest is easy to understand.

Now, to teach the seats, the text says, beginning with "lion seat." "Five places" means the places of the center, the east, the north, the west and the south. In the places of the northeast and the rest are the seats of elephants and so on, which will be taught below.

"Having visualized the sun seat"

means that, common to all of them, one meditates on a seat of a sun disc; since visualizing the seat comes first, one meditates on the sun disc. That is the meaning.

"Arisen from one's own breath"

means that, having visualized the commitment goddesses, one draws the wisdom goddesses out of one's own body and dissolves them into the commitment goddesses.

"Visualize the wisdom ḍākinī in the center"

means that one meditates on the wisdom ḍākinī upon the lion seat in the center. To teach her particular features, the text says, beginning with "three faces."

"Covered by her loosened hair,
she is beautified by the five buddhas"

means that she is adorned with the five buddhas radiating within the crown of her freely loosened hair.

In the lines:

"She has three faces,
laughing, wrathful and graceful,"

the graceful one is the main face, the laughing one is the left face, and the wrathful one is the right face.

"The wise should meditate on her in the center"

means that one who is wise, knowing her as the complete purity of the space element, meditates on the wisdom ḍākinī upon the central seat. The rest only needs its words understood.

In the passage beginning "of wisdom," "arisen from my water" means "having the nature of my own water element." This is to be applied in the same way to fire and the rest: one should see Vajratārā and the others, who are the complete purity of the water element and the other elements, as set out counterclockwise in the places of the east and the rest.

The passage beginning:

"Arisen from my five entities ..."

presents a summary. The five entities are the elements, space and the rest. "Arisen" means having their nature. If one asks what, the text says "the ḍākinīs are five," meaning the wisdom ḍākinī and the others. "The five" means the five ḍākinīs. "As five" means as the five wisdoms; or else, each of them as fivefold wisdom. "Meditate" means to visualize with intense conviction.

"Her form is perfect and most beautiful"

means that she is adorned with all good qualities and is supremely pleasing. The rest only needs its words understood.

In the passage beginning "Īśāna," "lion-headed" means having a lion's face. "Her color white and yellow" means in keeping with the color of that part of the ground.

"Holding a vajra hook and making the threatening gesture"

means that with her right hand she holds a hook marked with a vajra, and with her left she makes the threatening gesture. The meaning of the rest is clear.

In the passage beginning "the corner of Agni," "seven precious things" means a vase filled with seven kinds of precious substance. "Her color white and blue" is here too like the part of the ground.

"Her hand is adorned with a hook"

means that she is beautiful with a hook held in her right hand.

In the line:

"The left makes the threatening gesture and has a noose,"

one with a threatening forefinger and a noose is so described; this is the left hand.

In the passage beginning "Nairṛti":

"Her color is red and blue":

here too one should see the goddess's color as the color of the two parts of the region combined.

In the lines:

"With two hands, a hook and a noose,
and the threatening gesture, she is most beautiful,"

here too one should see the right hand as adorned with the hook, and the other as beautified by the rest.

In the passage beginning "the corner of Vāyu":

"Her color is yellow and red":

here too it is as before.

"She sits upon a nāga seat"

means that she sits upon a seat of a coiled serpent.

"The same is to be applied as her ornaments"

means that her ornaments too, the crest jewel and the rest, are made of the eight great nāgas.

"She holds the threatening gesture, a hook and a noose"

is applied as before.

"The eight ḍākinīs of the center" is to be construed as: "the eight kinds of ḍākinī dwelling in the central palace, Siṃhinī and the rest, have been explained." The rest is easy to understand.

To teach those who dwell in the outer tier of the palace, the text says, beginning:

"Outside, the four kinds ..."

The meaning is that they dwell at the four gates of the outer tier of the palace. These are taught in the passage beginning:

"In the east, Ḍākinī, O Lord of Victors ..."

"O Lord of Victors" is an address to Vajrapāṇi. "In the east" means at the eastern gate. "Ḍākinī" means one whose name is Ḍākinī. The seat of a preta is a seat of a corpse.

"Putting both hands into her mouth,
she blazes fiercely, laughing 'ha ha'"

describes one who, having put the palms of both hands into her mouth, laughs with the light of her teeth shining forth. The rest is easy to understand.

In the passage beginning "in the north," "the north" means at the northern gate. "The Blazing Lady" means the one called the Blazing Lady.

"Above the crown of her head, her two hands
are joined in the palm gesture, blazing like a butter lamp"

describes one whose two palms, their light blazing like a butter lamp, are joined above her head.

In the passage beginning "of the west," "the west" means at the western gate. "The Sucking Lady" means the one called the Sucking Lady.

"From her two cupped hands
she drinks a stream of blood"

describes one who drinks the stream of blood, like a thread, trickling from the cup of her two hands.

In the passage beginning "in the south," "in the south" means at the southern gate. "The Ravisher" means the one known as the Ravisher.

"Holding the threatening gesture and a pestle,
she bewilders the minds of sentient beings"

describes one who holds a pestle in her right hand and, by threatening with her left, bewilders the minds of sentient beings, that is, brings them under her power. The rest is easy to understand.

To teach the summary, the text says:

"The one who knows all meditates on them all."

This means that the yogin meditates on all the deities of the maṇḍala just as they are. Here, then, upon the seats of lion, elephant and the rest, and the seats of pretas, upon a sun disc adorned with a white parasol, one visualizes the thirteen yoginīs, arisen from the transformation of their respective seed syllables, with the colors and emblems already described, all seated in the heroic posture with the left leg crossed and the right slightly extended. In particular, Siṃhinī and the others should be given faces in keeping with their own names.

In the lines:

"Before their forms
one should display the commitments,"

"before them" means before their eyes. "The commitments" means the samaya mudrās, the ones that come below. The word "then" has the sense of "also"; it gathers in the mantras as well.

To teach those mantras, the text says, beginning with "wind and." "Wind" means the syllable ya.

"Fire" means ra. "Seven" means seventh, the suffix not being expressed. Of these, the seventh from wind is sa, and the seventh from fire is hā. That is what is meant: it applies to hā. The seed of the Vajra Lady is sa itself. "Divided" means placed as a crescent moon, because they share the same property.

In the line:

"Pressed down properly with sound and drop,"

"sound" is the long ū, which is attached as a subscript. "Pressed down properly with the drop" means pressed down from above by the dot. Put together, it becomes hūṃ.

"It is called the stream of rain"

is a name given by way of its quality, because all of them are a gathering of the syllables of nectar.

"Homage first, the burner at the end"

only needs its words understood.

In the line:

"It is the seed of the wisdom ḍākinī,"

the meaning is that when the wisdom ḍākinī is generated, she is to be generated from the seed hūṃ alone, but when the samaya mudrā is displayed, it is to be displayed with the mantra oṃ hūṃ svāhā. Likewise for the others too: what generates them is the seed alone, while for displaying the mudrā those seeds are to be joined with "homage, and having burning at the end."

In the line:

"The first seed, at the head of the vowels,"

the seed at the head of the vowels is the letter a. It is "the first" because it is the first of the twelve remaining seeds apart from the seed of the wisdom ḍākinī.

"Homage first, the burner at the end"

shows how its mantra is extracted: before a comes homage, that is, one supplies oṃ; at the end of a comes the burner, that is, one supplies svāhā. This is to be seen in every case.

"In the east is Vajraḍākinī;
it is renowned as her seed"

means that the seed that generates Vajraḍākinī is a. Put together, it becomes oṃ e svāhā.

"The second letter of the vowels"

is the second letter of the vowel group, a.

"Join it with homage and the burner"

has already been explained; put together, it becomes āṃ svāhā. "This" means a.

"It is the seed of the ḍākinī, the Fierce Lady"

only needs its words understood.

"On this very seed"

means on this very seed a.

"Place a staff at its side"

means that at the upper side of that long a one draws out a long line like a staff. "Homage" and the rest have been explained; put together, it becomes oṃ āḥ svāhā.

"Of Vetālī" means the seed of Vetālī.

"On this very seed"

has been explained.

"Place a drop on top"

is easy to understand. It becomes oṃ. In "homage" and the rest, put together, it becomes oṃ aṃ svāhā.

"She is the mistress of the fierce"

is construed with "seed."

In the line:

"The first neuter seed,"

the neuter letters are the four ṛ ṝ ḷ ḹ, and the first of them is the letter ṛ. In "homage" and the rest, put together, it becomes oṃ ṝ svāhā. The rest is easy to understand.

"The second neuter seed"

is, in the manner stated before, the letter ṝ; put together, it becomes oṃ ḷ svāhā.

"Likewise the third neuter seed"

is, in the same way, the third, the letter ḹ; put together, it becomes oṃ ḷ svāhā.

"For the fourth neuter letter"

is the letter ḹ; put together, it becomes oṃ ḹ svāhā.

"Ulūkī, the maker of hūṃ": this is because the syllable hūṃ is the chief of the owl's cries.

"Of the fifth class,
take the second letter"

means the letter pha. "Seed" means that very letter. "Root" means the drop. "Sound" means u, and "joined" means that these are supplied below and above. That becomes phuṃ. In "homage" and the rest, put together, it becomes oṃ phuṃ svāhā. The rest is easy to understand.

"Of the fifth class,
likewise take the second letter"

is again pha itself.

"Join e and the drop"

makes it pheṃ. In "homage" and the rest, put together, it becomes oṃ pheṃ svāhā.

"Of the class" and the rest is repeated again, and is pha itself.

"Join oṃ and repha with the drop"

means that ra is supplied at the side and o and the drop on top, so that it becomes phroṃ. In "homage" and the rest, put together, it becomes oṃ phroṃ svāhā.

In "of the class" and the rest, as before, it is pha.

In the line:

"Join repha and the drop with the hook,"

the hook is i. One joins i and the drop above and joins ra below, so that it becomes phriṃ. In "homage" and the rest, put together, it becomes oṃ phriṃ svāhā. The rest only needs its words understood.

Now, to teach the mudrās, the text says, beginning with "the two fists." The meaning of these lines is this: with both hands bound in fists, one extends the two middle fingers and joins their tips, and reciting the mantra oṃ hūṃ svāhā, one displays it before the wisdom ḍākinī.

In the passage beginning "the two hands," one joins the palms of the two hands, sets the two thumbs at the base of the two middle fingers and moves them again and again, and with the mantra oṃ a svāhā one displays it before the Vajra Lady.

In the passage beginning "fist," binding both hands in fists and extending the two thumbs is the mudrā of the Fierce Lady; it is joined with the mantra oṃ āḥ svāhā.

In the passage beginning "that very mudrā," extending the two little fingers from that same mudrā of the Fierce Lady is the mudrā of Vetālī; it is joined with the mantra oṃ e svāhā.

In the passage beginning "that very mudrā," extending the two middle fingers from that same mudrā is the mudrā of Caṇḍālī; it is joined with the mantra o āṃ svāhā.

"That is the fourth mudrā" means that, counting the four yoginīs of the center apart from the wisdom ḍākinī, this is the fourth in number.

In the passage beginning "vajra fist," binding the two vajra fists, extending the two index fingers and joining their tips to each other is the mudrā common to Siṃhinī and the others, referred to as "the four corners," while the mantras are to be applied to each individually. Of these, oṃ ṛ svāhā is to be displayed to Siṃhinī, oṃ ṝ svāhā to Vyāghrī, oṃ ḷ svāhā to Jambukī, and oṃ ḹ svāhā to Ulūkī.

In the passage beginning "the two hands," one extends the two hands and puts the palms into the mouth, and with the mantra oṃ phuṃ svāhā one displays it to Ḍākinī in the east; afterward, saying hā, one gradually releases the mudrā. This is the mudrā of Ḍākinī.

In the passage beginning "the two hands," extending the two hands and joining the palms at the crown of the head is the mudrā of the Blazing Lady; it is joined with the mantra oṃ phroṃ svāhā.

In the passage beginning "homage," first saying hūṃ, joining the palms of the two hands in homage and placing them at the mouth is the mudrā of the Sucking Lady; it is to be joined with the mantra oṃ phroṃ svāhā.

In the passage beginning "the two hands," making the two hands revolve in a lotus and then placing the palms below at the place of the bhaga is the mudrā of the Ravisher; it is to be joined with the mantra oṃ phriṃ svāhā.

Now, wishing to teach the mantras and mudrās of scent and the other offerings, the text says, beginning with "homage." Extending the right hand fully and making as if anointing with scent is the mudrā of scent, and its mantra is oṃ hūṃ svāhā.

In the passage beginning "nectar," "nectar" is the letter sa. "Together with ra and the drop" means that ra is affixed beneath sa and the drop is supplied above, so that it becomes sraṃ. In "homage" and the rest, put together, it becomes oṃ svaṃ svāhā.

The mudrā is taught in the passage beginning "of scent": joining the thumb and index finger from the mudrā of scent is the mudrā of flowers.

In the passage beginning "the class," the letter of the first class is ka. Here too it is to be joined with "join it with sound and the drop": u is affixed below and the drop supplied above, so that it becomes kuṃ. In "homage" and the rest, put together, it is oṃ kuṃ svāhā.

The mudrā is taught in the passage beginning "that very mudrā": raising the middle finger from the mudrā of flowers is the mudrā of incense.

In the passage beginning "the end of nine," the last of nine letters counted from ya is the seed "end of nine," that is, the letter kṣa. Here too one should read "join it with sound and the drop"; put together, it becomes kṣuṃ. In "homage" and the rest, put together, it is oṃ kṣuṃ svāhā.

The mudrā is taught in the passage beginning "fist": binding the right hand in a fist and raising the thumb is the mudrā of the lamp.

In the passage beginning "the class," the third of the first class is the letter ga. In the line:

"Joining sound and the drop,"

"sound" is the short u, to be joined below; "the drop" is the anusvāra, to be joined above. Put together, it becomes guṃ. In "homage" and the rest, put together, it is oṃ guṃ svāhā.

The mudrā is taught in the passage beginning "the two hands"; this mudrā has already been explained.

"The torma mantra is joined to the previous one"

means that joining the mantra and mudrā just given is suited to the act of offering the torma. By these lines it is taught that, by joining scent and all the rest with mantras and mudrās, one should make offerings as appropriate.

"The nondual torma is as before"

means that the five meats, the five nectars, fresh meat, fish, garlic, onions and the like are the nondual torma. "As before" means that this torma too is offered with flowers and the rest joined with mantras and mudrās.

"By this very one" means by the reality of the torma. "Should be offered" leaves unstated "to all the deities."

For that very reason the text says, beginning with "first." "The five kinds of substance" are the five nectars; they are simply well known.

"And the hook of the various"

refers to what is spoken of as fivefold, go ku da ha na. It is a hook because it draws in and accomplishes the aims one desires.

The lines beginning:

"Scent, flowers, incense and the rest"

join on to the twenty offerings. "With mantras and mudrās" means with the mantra and mudrā of the torma.

"Afterward, with the twenty offerings,
the one who has wisdom should make offerings"

is easy to understand.

That is taught in the line:

"Having sung the song of hūṃ,"

which refers to the twenty offerings: with all of them one should utter the syllable hūṃ.

"Delight all the yoginīs"

means that this is the chief means of delighting the yoginīs.

In the passage beginning "vajra fist," the lines:

"Binding the two vajra fists
and placing them at the waist,"

mean planting the two vajra fists on the two hips in the manner of holding a vajra and bell.

"Move them in three parts with hūṃ"

means that, first uttering hūṃ, one moves them, dividing the body into three parts: the head, the waist and the lower body.

"Striking in the manner of striking"

means first extending the left hand and then extending the right hand over it, as if beating.

"Beating as on a paṭaha drum"

means raising the two hands to the right and left and shaking them, as if beating a paṭaha drum.

"Waving like a banner"

means extending the right hand alone.

"Covering like a parasol"

means holding the two hands as if grasping the handle of a parasol.

"Beating like a clay drum"

means acting as if beating with the two hands from above, as on a clay drum.

"Beating like a round drum"

means acting as if beating from the right and left with the two hands, as when beating a round drum.

The rest is easy to understand.

"With all the acts of that very reality
One should worship the eight yoginīs."

This means that one should please the eight chief yoginīs through the reality of the twenty acts, beginning with holding the vajra and bell.

"I emanate as what is called hūṃ;
One should sing the song of hūṃ."

Here, "the song of hūṃ" is a song in which the syllable hūṃ is foremost, such as:

"To the causeless reality that freely liberates beings
I bow down.
te na hūṃ te na te te hūṃ"

and so on; it is simply well known. "I emanate as what is called hūṃ" means singing the song of hūṃ oneself. This teaches the offering of song.

Then, in order to teach the offering of praise, the text states the passage beginning "Vajra":

"Vajra, you gaze with the power of joy and with eyes."

Here, "Vajra" is an address, calling out "Hevajra," that is, "O Vajra," because he is the indivisible embodiment of the dharmakāya. "Joy" means acting for the welfare of sentient beings. "Power" is his capacity for that, because he possesses the capacity to act for the welfare of beings. "Eyes" refers to the five kinds, called the flesh eye, the divine eye, the eye of wisdom, the Dharma eye, and the buddha eye. "Gazing" means focusing with them: the one in whom there is this focus on the welfare of beings is said to "gaze with the power of joy and with eyes."

"The reality of the syllable, its nature, sporting with emptiness."

Here, because it does not change, it is the syllable (akṣara), that is, nondual wisdom. Its reality is its nature. Its nature is the absence of essential nature: it has the nature of having no intrinsic nature. In "sporting with emptiness," emptiness is what was just discussed, namely emptiness itself. "Sporting" means playing within the minds of beings: he sports and gazes through emptiness.

"Free from entity and nonentity, you know the Middle Way."

Here, the middle, in which phenomena are free from being entities and nonentities, is suchness; one who knows phenomena through that distinctive suchness is called this.

"Having destroyed all that is unchanging, you contemplate the remainder."

Here, "unchanging" means the syllable. All collections of sound that consist of syllables are "all that is unchanging," and destroying them means abandoning them. In "you contemplate the remainder," the remainder is what remains beyond what sound expresses, namely the inexpressible itself. "Contemplating" means focusing on it completely.

"Union, full embrace, engaged in the work of water."

"Union" is the meditative union of wisdom and skillful means. "Full embrace" is joining, that is, meditative absorption. "Water" is the awakening mind; since that is what is to be pursued, it is the purpose of the work. One who has rightly entered upon it is called this.

"Having found the nondual Dharma, you have found liberation as well."

Here, the nondual dharma is the selflessness of phenomena. "Found" means fully realized: having thereby found freedom is having found liberation.

"With bliss you crush saṃsāra, and you give up liberation."

Here, bliss is the wisdom that knows suchness; crushing saṃsāra with it means destroying it. "You give up liberation" is said of one who, out of compassion, for the sake of beings, has set liberation aside. As it is said:

"Through knowledge one does not abide in existence;
Through compassion one does not abide in peace."

Thus the lines beginning "Union" teach the perfection of the means for one's own and others' welfare; the lines beginning "Having found the nondual Dharma" teach the perfection of one's own welfare; and the lines beginning "With bliss you crush saṃsāra" teach the perfection of others' welfare.

In order to teach praise of the Blessed One (the Buddha), endowed with such distinctions, by calling out to him and praising him with a brief hymn, the text states the passage beginning "All":

"Mantra of the yoginīs of all entities, you sport."

Here, "all entities" means entities of every kind, that is, through body, speech and mind. In "mantra of the yoginīs," the yoginī herself is the mantra, because she protects the minds of beings. "Sporting" means dancing through her: this is the Blessed One.

"You know the five yoginīs, the reality of the buddhas."

Here, the reality of the buddhas is the supreme wisdom that knows exhaustion and non-arising. One who, through that distinctive reality, perceives and knows the characteristics of the five yoginīs is called this. These lines teach the dharmakāya of the Blessed One.

"With many forms of illusion you ripen beings."

Here, illusion is emanation: through the forms of manifold emanations he ripens and liberates beings. "Ripen" is illustrative, so liberation should be included as well. With "you do," the words "by you" are to be supplied.

"Discerning the vajra, you cut through the delusion of dharmas."

Here, "vajra" means emptiness, and "discerning" means having realized the characteristic of nonconceptuality. This says that he has realized the emptiness of nonconceptual wisdom; the one in whom this is present is called this. In "you cut through the delusion of dharmas," dharmas are sentient beings, because they bear the characteristics of birth, death, pleasure, pain, and so on. Delusion is thorough affliction. With "cut through," the words "by teaching them, after fully realizing the vajra, in keeping with the particular capacities of beings as vessels" are to be supplied. These lines teach the sambhogakāya.

In order to call out to sentient beings and exhort them:

"Bow to the yoginīs' rapture, the gathering of the heart's dharmas."

Here, "heart's dharmas" means the dharmas of the essence, and "gathering" means accumulated. This indicates the Blessed One, who has accumulated the three bodies, the dharmas of the essence. He himself is the rapture of the yogins, that is, the nature of the yoginīs enraptured by great bliss. "Bow" amounts to saying, "O sentient beings, pay homage to the yoginīs endowed with such special qualities."

Now, in order to teach praise in the Sanskrit manner, the text states the passage beginning "Sentient beings":

"To the yoga that liberates sentient beings, praise and homage."

Here, yoga is the meditative union of skillful means and wisdom.

He himself is "the lord," that is, the principal one. Since he ripens and liberates sentient beings, he is the one who liberates sentient beings; and this is to be connected with "praise and homage to the Blessed One endowed with such distinctions."

"To the one reality, born from the self of all, praise and homage."

Here, the self of all is the dharmadhātu, and whatever arises from it is "born from the self of all." "The reality of the one" is the mind of the one, that is, nondual wisdom.

In the line beginning "saṃsāra," saṃsāra itself is the ocean, because it is hard for the childish to cross. Delusion is the cognitive obscuration; the one who is deluded by reason of dwelling in that ocean of saṃsāra is called this. "Cutting" means dispelling.

In the line beginning "of reality," "the sole one of reality" means this: since, knowing suchness, you have no other peer equal to you, you are the sole one of reality, the one who acts. The rest is easy to understand.

"Praise especially" means praising him as especially exalted.

In "One should make offerings with song and sounds of praise," "sounds of praise" means proclaiming supreme qualities, that is, uttering the reality of "Homage to the Vajra" and so on. "Song" is the supreme reality: offering with song alone.

"In between, sounding hūṃ and the bell" means ringing the bell, preceded by uttering the sound hūṃ; with that one should make offerings at the intervals.

"One should make the threefold aspiration" means one should aspire in three ways. That itself is stated in the passage beginning "the vajra palms": the meaning is that, having bound the vajra palms at the heart, one should aspire while reciting the three mantras beginning oṃ svabhāva śuddhra.

In the passage beginning "on the right," after making the threefold aspiration, one imagines a lotus in the right hand and shapes it as a lotus, and imagines a vajra in the left. Then, preceded by the lotus turning, one presses down the little finger with the left thumb, extends the remaining three fingers as a three-pronged vajra, and joins it upon the lotus of the right hand. This is the mudrā of the mahāmudrā, and its mantra is oṃ ha hoḥ hrīḥ svāhā.

"The mantra and mudrā for blessing into the mahāmudrā" means that after making the threefold aspiration, one makes it the mahāmudrā with this mantra and mudrā. Not only that: at the times of eating a little food and so on, and also at the time of the torma, one should taste the nectar with this mantra and mudrā and bless it into the mahāmudrā.

For that very reason it is said, in the passage beginning:

"The yogin should visualize
All the deities simultaneously."

The meaning is that at the time of purifying the liquor and the rest, one should complete all the deities of the maṇḍala simultaneously, that is, all at once, in a single instant.

"The one with wisdom should set out
The outer maṇḍala of the worldly ones and the rest."

This likewise teaches the torma rite connected with it. "Worldly ones" are the field protectors; by "and the rest," the deities of the maṇḍala are to be included. "The one with wisdom" is the practitioner, and this is connected with "should set out"; here the second case is used in the sense of the third. "Outer" means external to oneself. In order to give torma to them, one makes a triangular maṇḍala of red sandalwood or of saffron, and in the center, on a circle, one draws four lines, making nine squares. In the squares, beginning with the eastern one, one should visualize, in order, Black Rudra, Great Rudra, Black Devadatta, the Gnashing One, the Hideous One, the One Beyond Austerities, and the vināyakas. Each of those field protectors is surrounded by four of the thirty-two yoginīs, the Crone, the Fierce Lady and the rest, and each is made to hold its own emblem, and so on. In the central square is the embodiment of the thirteen yoginīs, the wisdom ḍākinī and the rest. In this way one should meditate on the outer maṇḍala.

In "Anointing each with scent," and "One should offer through each one's own application," the meaning is this: here too, at the times of eating a little food and so on, one should offer scents and the rest individually to the maṇḍala of deities completed in an instant; and at the time of the torma, one should offer individually, in the outer maṇḍala, to the transcendent ones, the wisdom ḍākinī and the rest, and to the worldly ones, Black Rudra and the rest.

The offering of the five nectars is stated in the passage beginning "The five nectars"; it is easy to understand.

The rite for blessing the nectar is stated in the passage beginning ha. "ha" carries the unstated words "is to be visualized within the five nectars." By it, their ordinary color is taken away. Likewise, by visualizing hoḥ, their ordinary smell is destroyed; and by visualizing hrīḥ, the potency of their ordinary taste is destroyed.

"Having recited the three three times,
One should visualize them as three deities."

This means that after visualizing the colors and so forth of the three syllables in this way, one recites them again three times and visualizes them, in order, as Lokeśvara and the others: ha as Lokeśvara, hoḥ as Vairocana, and hrīḥ as Akṣobhya. The intent is that one should visualize the five nectars in this way.

In the passage beginning "Lord of the Lotus," the Lord of the Lotus (Padmeśvara) is Amitābha, and he is Lokeśvara. "Beautiful form, golden in color" shows whatever state, of color and so on, is attained through being purified by him. "Beautiful form" means attaining a special color; "golden in color" is merely a figurative expression. "The color of the five nectars" is the unstated remainder. "Beautiful form, golden in color" is itself to be connected with "one should visualize it as Lokeśvara." One should visualize the other deities in the same way.

"Urine," literally "scented water," means water of an especially exalted scent, that is, having attained a special nature. And what is that? The text says "Vairocana": it is the very nature of Vairocana.

Here the color is white.

"Tasting" means having attained a special flavor: it is the nectar. And what is that? The text says "Vajreśvara, Vajrapāṇi." Vajreśvara, Lord of the Vajra, is Akṣobhya; and because he holds a vajra in his hand, he is Vajrapāṇi. Here the color is blue.

In order to teach the summary, the text says:

"One should meditate on the three deities in three ways."

The meaning is that, having destroyed the ordinary color and so on by means of the three syllables, one should meditate, through the special color and so on, on the very nature of vajra speech and the rest.

Now the blessing of the liquor, in order to make offerings to the yoginīs, is taught in the passage beginning "left":

"On the left hand, the moon hand,
In the center of the moon, the nectar's
Syllable, together with the curl of Sarvavid."

This means: one imagines a moon disk in the palm of the left hand, and in its center, by joining to the nectar syllable sa the syllable oṃ, called "the curl of Sarvavid," it becomes what is called oṃ. By that it is made into the nature of nectar.

Again, in order to teach the purification, the text says:

"The thumb finger is the earth itself;
The ring fingers are Mount Meru."

That is, one imagines the thumb of the right hand as the ground, and the ring finger as the seven Merus.

In the passage beginning "by the power of churning," the power is the very nature of the asura Bali: it is the power of churning the substances. "Nectar": if one asks what it becomes through the churning, the meaning is that the water, by means of the three, is to be known as having the very nature of the water of wisdom. "The three" are ha hoḥ hrīḥ. The meaning is this: one places the liquor in the palm of the left hand, visualizes the syllable soṃ upon it, and churns it thoroughly with the thumb and ring finger; then, by visualizing the three syllables beginning with ha, one visualizes them, in the manner described earlier, as the three deities with their colors and so on.

"To all the yoginīs
One should offer thumb drops, to each individually."

Here, a drop is a flick of the nectar water. "Thumb" means the thumb and ring finger. "Individually" means to each yoginī separately. After that, one names each of the yoginīs of the maṇḍala by her own name and offers her a drop of nectar.

The passage beginning "after that" teaches the offering to oneself that follows. "By one's own reality" means in the form of the wisdom ḍākinī.

"One should visualize it as oneself"

means meditating on oneself as her.

"The stūpa of the five expansions"

Here, the stūpa is the body; since it is manifest as the five aggregates, it is the five expansions. Alternatively, it is "expanded" because it destroys the afflictions. What is it? It is meditated on as a stūpa. "Fivefold" refers to the body that condenses the five families. "Threefold" means by the three thumb drops. The meaning is that first one offers to all the yoginīs with thumb drops, and after that one offers with thumb flicks to one's own body, endowed with the yoga of the wisdom ḍākinī.

In order to teach the places of the three drops, the text states the passage beginning "tip of the tongue." "Tip of the tongue" is the tip of the tongue; "the tilaka" is the point between the eyebrows; and "above" is the crown of the head.

"The intelligent one should honor the three places"

means that one endowed with the yoga of the deity makes offerings with thumb drops in this way at those three places.

The verses for giving the liquor in the maṇḍala of the feast are taught in the passage beginning "Look!" Here, "most beautiful" means most utterly pure. What is? "This dharma," that is, all outer and inner phenomena. With "Look!", the words "without clinging" are to be supplied. To teach just that, the text says:

"In this there is no conceptuality: let it go!"

"Let it go" means one taste. Of what? Of conceptuality, that is, the conceptions of grasped and grasper and so on. Why? Because phenomena do not exist: the meaning is that although they appear as enemy, friend and so on, like the sky they are pure by nature.

"The brahmin, the dog and the caṇḍāla, these three"

Here, by way of the word "brahmin" as an illustration, brahmins, kṣatriyas and vaiśyas are to be included. By way of the word "dog" as an illustration, all ordinary animals are to be included. Likewise, by the word "caṇḍāla," all śūdras and those who dwell at the margins are to be included. "Of one nature" means alike in essential nature. The meaning is this: holding humans, animals and so on, and brahmins, caṇḍālas and so on, to differ in caste and the like is nothing but one's own conceptual thought at its core; for in a body that has arisen from blood and semen, from attachment, aversion and the rest, not the slightest distinction in semen and so on can be found. "Eat together" means that all conceptual thoughts are to be drunk down. The rest is easy to understand.

In order to teach the act of the one who receives, the text states the passage beginning "the lotus turning"; the mudrā of the lotus turning and the rest has already been explained.

"Having imagined the mind as like crystal"

refers to the mind free of the stains of conceptual thought: through the absence of clinging, it focuses on the nature of reality.

In order to teach the words the receiver speaks, the text states the passage beginning "attachment":

"Freeing from the stain of attachment."

Here, attachment is craving, because it makes the reality of mind unclear. "Freeing" means making free: by this, craving is gradually destroyed. "Then why are aversion and the rest not mentioned?" That is true, but here attachment is taught as the principal one. For the principal cause of saṃsāra is pleasant feeling, and that arises from attachment; so the intent is that if attachment together with its retinue is abandoned, the other afflictions will also be exhausted.

"The dharma that has come well from the priceless"

Here, "dharmas" are the phenomena of thorough affliction and of complete purification. "Well" is one's own mind, and "come" means arisen from it. Why? Because everything is self-awareness.

Alternatively, "come well" means this: because the phenomena of things such as the aggregates arise from one's own mind, they go into nondual suchness; the meaning is "Tathāgata," the Thus-Gone.

In "priceless": that which is to be taken in exchange for what is given up is a price. Since there is none in this, it is priceless. The meaning is that, since there is neither taking nor discarding, it is by nature to be honored in every way.

"Wholly free of grasping and not grasping"

Here, "grasping" is clinging to the purity that is free from attachment and the rest, and "not grasping" is the opposite of that. "Wholly free" means being released from both: from clinging to all phenomena as things, and from clinging to them as pure. "Reality": so that the mind may enter that reality which is free from clinging, this is to be connected with "I pay homage to the Dharma."

"For the sake of the accomplishment of union,
Enjoying all, just as it is,
The yoginī should purify it thoroughly,
And say, 'Enjoy it with pleasure.'"

Here, "union" means the genuine substances. "Accomplishment" means the samaya, because one is to be satisfied with the samaya. "Sake" means food, drink and the like. Having taken up the vessel of liquor with the verses mentioned earlier, "just as it is" means that whatever has been obtained, the yoginī thoroughly purifies and makes pure; and "enjoying all" means it is to be distributed to all and partaken of. "Enjoy it with pleasure" means that, having handed over the vessel of liquor, one should also say in words to the seniors, "Enjoy it with pleasure."

In order to explain "One should set out the outer maṇḍala," the text says:

"With red substances
One should smear a triangular maṇḍala."

The meaning is that one smears the outside in a triangle with red sandalwood, saffron and the like, then makes it square, and, as stated before, makes a circle with nine squares.

The mantra and mudrā for summoning the deities of the outer maṇḍala are taught:

"Having bound the two vajra fists."

Its meaning is this: having bound the two vajra fists, one puts them back to back, interlocks the two little fingers, raises the two index fingers, and places the left index finger behind the slightly bent right index finger. The mantra is the one beginning oṃ. "The mantra and mudrā of summoning" is just as the words say. The passage beginning "red flowers" is easy to understand.

The torma substances are taught in the passage beginning "the torma of enjoyment." The torma of enjoyment is the nondual torma; to explain it, the text says:

"Fresh meat, fish meat and the rest."

By the word "rest," garlic, onion and the like are included. For that reason it is said, "For all the food substances": "all food" has already been explained, and "all substances" means grain liquor and prepared liquor. As has also been said:

"What is prepared from grain, bark and the rest
Is named as the world commonly knows it;
What comes from sugarcane and the like
Is held to be grain liquor and prepared liquor."

"One should set it out together with the five nectars"

Here, "together with the five nectars" means that the five nectars are poured into it, and "set out" means setting down their respective seeds.

"Placing the vessel on the left side,
One should point the wrathful one on the right."

This means that one places the torma vessel in the left hand, and with the right hand binds a fist, raises the index finger, and points. "The wrathful one" is the index finger. Alternatively, "side" means dividing into two portions: one places two tormas, on the left and on the right; with the left one should offer to the deities of the maṇḍala, and with the right one should offer to the guardians of the directions. In that case, "the vessel" is the wheel of the maṇḍala, and "the wrathful one" is the guardians of the directions.

From "oṃ the lone tree" up to "the archer lady" is the mantra for summoning the world guardians. "Caṇḍāla" means a butcher's house, and "vessel" means the ocean. Accordingly, it connects with "Those who dwell in those places, the lone tree and the rest, come here!" And who are they? The text states the passage beginning "the black, the fierce": having invited them, one should install the retinues of the field protectors visualized in the nine squares mentioned earlier. Among them, in the east is Black Rudra; in the north, Great Rudra; in the west, Devadatta; in the south, the Black One; in the northeast, Īśāna's quarter, the Gnashing One; in the southeast, Agni's quarter, the Hideous One; in the southwest, Nairṛti's quarter, the One Beyond Austerities; and in the northwest, Vāyu's quarter, the vināyaka. They are three-eyed and four-armed, and are clad in the weapons, garments and ornaments as taught. Those in the four directions are, in order, blue, yellow, white and red in color. Those in Īśāna's quarter and the others are, in order, black and yellow, blue and red, red and white, and white and yellow in color.

In front of Black Rudra is the Crone; on the left side, the Fierce Lady; behind, the Hideous Lady; and on the right, Umā. These are, in order, of the colors of the Ḍākinī and the others.

In the east, north, west and south of Great Rudra are Jayā, Vijayā, Ajitā and Aparājitā, of the colors of the Lamp Lady and the others.

In the east, north, west and south of Devadatta are the Good Black Lady, the Great Black Lady, the Stout Black Lady, and the Powerful Lady, of the colors of the Sucking Lady and the others.

In the east, north, west and south of the Black One are the Moon Lady, the Fierce Lady, the Hateful Lady and the Hanging Lady, of the colors of the Ravisher and the others.

In the east, north, west and south of the Gnashing One are the Mistress of the Thirty-Three, the Ravisher, the Lamp Lady and the Sucking Lady, of the colors of the Crone and the others.

In the east, north, west and south of the Hideous One are the Fierce Form, the Great Form, the Gnashing-Fanged Lady and the Single-Skull Lady, of the colors of the Powerful Lady and the others.

In the east, north, west and south of the One Beyond Austerities are the Lady with the Skull Garland, the Garlanded Lady, the Khaṭvāṅga Lady and the Great-Souled Lady, of the colors of the Good Black Lady and the others.

In the east, north, west and south of the vināyaka are the Sword-in-Hand Lady, the Axe-in-Hand Lady, the Vajra-in-Hand Lady and the Bow-in-Hand Lady, of the colors of Jayā and the others.

Among them, those from the Crone through the Great-Souled Lady sport holding in their hands the five kinds of offerings, flowers and the rest. The four beginning with the Sword-in-Hand Lady hold weapons in their hands, just as their names say.

"Likewise the five ḍākinīs of the great lady,
Who accomplish all the rites."

These are the Blazing Lady and the others.

"The great queen of the maṇḍala of union,
Likewise Vajreśvara"

refers to the maṇḍala of the wisdom ḍākinī. "The principal ones" means the guardians of the directions.

"The great body of the Tathāgatas"

means that all of them are of the nature of the body of the Tathāgatas. Of what sort? The text says:

"She who brings forth the dustless yoga."

"Dust" means the afflictions, because they are of the nature of clinging to things. One in whom it is absent is dustless, that is, free of dust. Yoga is meditative absorption. "Bringing forth" means drawing it out, that is, accomplishing by it.

"By this command of Vajreśvarī,
All, from every side, come here!"

Here, "by this" means by this. Vajreśvarī is the chief lady of the maṇḍala, the wisdom ḍākinī. Her command is the wisdom ḍākinī's decree, the command having the character of the mantra stated earlier. "All" are the outer deities, and "Come here!" is the summoning.

And what is to be done after that? The text states the passage beginning oṃ kakka:

"Having filled their mouths with nectar."

Here, the nectar is the torma. "Filling their mouths" means: having looked upon those of the outer maṇḍala in the manner stated earlier, one should fill the mouths of the deities with the torma by means of the mantra beginning oṃ kakka.

"Contemplate the state of the rite."

Here, the rite one has in mind is the state of the rite: one should focus on the rite of pacifying or another. Alternatively, the nectar is the liquor; "filling the mouth" means the practitioner fills his own cheeks; and "the state of the rite," according to the oral instructions, is spraying it from the mouth into the remainder torma. "Contemplating" means focusing on the rite. Alternatively, some say that pata means taking a step, so that after that one should dance, moving the feet and so on.

"Having meditated on the yogins and yoginīs"

is the first part of the sentence "having meditated on the yogins and yoginīs, one gives the torma." And what does that bring about? The text says:

"All the rites are accomplished."

That is, all the rites, pacifying and the rest, will be accomplished.

Now the mantra and mudrā for giving the torma are taught, in the passage beginning:

"Having bound the two vajra fists."

These mantras and mudrās are here explained in full; earlier, the aspect of flowers was taught in brief.

"Having applied the rites and the rest as is fitting,
One applies the torma rites and the rest."

This means that, for whichever of the eight rites, pacifying and the rest, one is engaged in, one should give the torma for that by this procedure. The passage beginning there lists the eight rites and is easy to understand.

In order to teach the summary, the text says:

"Through the reality of the outer torma,
The torma rites and the rest are accomplished."

"Outer" means to the outer deities. The reality of the torma is the procedure as it should be. The intent is that if one gives the torma by it and performs the rites, they will be accomplished.

Now, in order to teach the secret offering, the text states the passage beginning "then." "Special" means especially exalted, that is, the supreme offering. This shows making them into the nature of Vairocana and the others.

In the passage beginning "Wholly abandoning the allowable and the unallowable," "allowable" means what has been made allowable and permitted through cleanliness and the like, and "unallowable" is the opposite. "Wholly abandoning" means that, by becoming accustomed to those things that rest on the two distinctions as being the nature of Vairocana and the others, ordinary conceptuality is overthrown.

To explain what was taught, the text states the passage beginning:

"The five kinds, the hook and the rest,
Are to be made into the five realities."

"The five realities" are the nature of Vairocana and the others. "To be made" means they are to be purified by them. Which by which? The text states the passage beginning "the great hook." The great hook is the one whose first letter is na.

"Held to be the wisdom deity"

Here, the wisdom deity is Vairocana, and "held" means one strongly resolves it to be him.

"The vajra hook, Akṣobhya"

Here, the vajra hook is the one whose first letter is ga, and Akṣobhya is Akṣobhya himself.

"The hook of place, the Lord of Jewels"

Here, the hook of place is the one with śva at the beginning, and the Lord of Jewels is Ratnasambhava.

"The king's hook, Amoghasiddhi"

Here, the king's hook is the one with the letter ha at the beginning. "Amoghasiddhi" is easy to understand.

"The left hook, Amitābha"

Here, the left hook is the one with śva at the end, and Amitābha, "Immeasurable Light," is Amitābha, "Boundless Light." These lines show that those hooks are to be made into the nature of Vairocana and the others, arising from their respective seeds, which are given below. For that very reason the text says:

"In the five hooks and the rest,
All the realities are to be purified."

The meaning is that one should meditate on the five hooks as the reality of the corresponding deities.

On the lines

"With whatever is desired, all the deities
And all the yoginīs are to be satisfied,"

"whatever" refers to whichever of the hooks it may be, and "is desired" refers to the seed of each respective deity: the hook is blessed by that seed. Or else, "whatever is desired" means that one should satisfy each of the deities and yoginīs with whatever that one is fond of.

To show that the samaya substances are to be distributed in such a way that those who lack samaya do not in any way perceive or know of it, the text says:

"Not delighting in desire,
One should not partake for the sake of enjoyment."

Here, in "delighting in desire," desire means the objects of desire, and delight means attachment: as much as one desires, and in whatever way one desires. "Not" is the negation, because through these others would come to understand. Or else, "delighting in desire" is ordinary attachment: one should not eat with such attachment. "Enjoyment" means savoring the taste of what is conditioned; therefore one should not partake of it and eat it in that way. The meaning is that, on the contrary, it is to be tasted for the sake of samaya. Or else, "enjoyment" means special elaborate preparations, the principal ones and so on: one does not partake by way of these. Why? Because the thought is that others would come to know of it. As it is also said:

"So that it does not become known,
Do everything in a concealed and concise way.
If it is known, the samaya is broken,
There is no accomplishment, and one is no practitioner."

On the line "All one's own desires are reality itself," desire means clinging, and the word "all" is to be applied to every object, form, sound and the rest, and to the seer, the seen, the seeing and so on. "One's own aim is reality" means that one does not apprehend those signs, and so enters into reality. "Perform the acts of worship and so on" means that by this one enters into the special worship of the Tathāgatas.

The passage beginning "In its center, the heroes" is given to teach the unsurpassed worship. "In its center" means in the center of the feast maṇḍala. "The heroes" means the heroes and the yoginīs. What are they to do? The text says, "One should join āli and kāli": without clinging, method and wisdom enter into union. To show that by this the yoginīs are pleased, the passage beginning "Through the perfect union of āli and kāli" is given. "The nectar arisen in its center" means the fluid that arises from melting through joy and abides in the center of the lotus. "All flavors are to be tasted" means that, having first purified it well, one takes what abides in the lotus with the tongue and eats it oneself. "The remainder of all the substances" means what is left over from the flowers and so on that one has enjoyed oneself. "Is to be assigned to the outer maṇḍala" means that after one has enjoyed them oneself, from what remains one first gives to the wisdom consort with the lotus of the mouth, then places it again in the outer lotus vessel, and offers it also to the others of the feast maṇḍala who possess samaya.

Next, to show that the song of hūṃ and the auspicious song are to be sung, the passage beginning "With the song called hūṃ" is given. Here, the song of hūṃ is the one given below, beginning "Freely liberating beings." "One should fill it with auspicious sound" means that one fills it not only with the song of hūṃ but also with an auspicious melody. That very song is stated in the passage beginning "Embodied ones." "Embodied ones" means sentient beings, because by the force of karma they enter this or that destiny. "For the sake of" means for their benefit. "Wisdom" means the bodies of the yoginīs, because they separate one from suffering. "Was requested" means making a request: asking for the sake of perfect union. Then what did he do? The text says, "Embracing and so on, the two join the two." Preceded by embracing, kissing and so on, method and wisdom having entered into union, he is adorned with emanations of union, arising, ceasing and so on, which are like illusory persons; and he remains present for the benefit of beings. With this, auspiciousness is proclaimed by way of the perfect fulfillment of others' benefit.

To show auspiciousness proclaimed by way of the perfect fulfillment of one's own benefit, the text says, "Homage to you who are freed from existence." Here, "existence" means the arising and ceasing of things. "Freed" means separated from that. The meaning is that here the arising and ceasing of things do not exist ultimately, but merely appear conventionally, false, like an illusory person. Therefore the intention is that, although some arising, ceasing and so on may appear through this, one should not cling to them, because they do not exist in the ultimate sense.

To show that after the auspicious song the bell is sounded, the text says:

"With auspicious sounds as one wishes,
Saying hūṃ hūṃ, ring the bell."

Next, to show that aspirations are to be made, the passage beginning

"Clasping the vajra palms tightly,
Placing them firmly at the heart,"

is given. Its meaning is this: clasping the palms tightly at the heart, one recites aloud the words from "May all sentient beings be free from illness" up to "establish them in the abode of the yoga treatises," and so makes aspirations.

Next, to show that the deities are requested to depart, the passage beginning "Binding the two vajra fists" is given:

"Binding the two vajra fists,
Place the left at the heart;
Extending the right to the ground,
After that, request them to depart."

This means that with this mudrā one requests the wisdom ḍākinī, the wisdom deity seated in the center of the nine squares, together with her retinue, to depart. The mantra is oṃ ātmani tiṣṭha hūṃ svāhā.

To show that, after the wisdom goddess has been requested to depart, the samaya wisdom ḍākinī is withdrawn, the passage beginning "Then by one's own breath" is given; it is easy to understand.

To show that the world protectors are requested to depart, the passage beginning oṃ sarba duṣṭaṃ is given. The passage beginning oṃ sarba duṣṭaṃ is the mantra for requesting the outer deities to depart. The mudrā is stated in the line "By snapping the fingers three times": the meaning is that one crosses the two hands over each other and snaps the fingers three times.

As for the word "chapter": the text spoken here, beginning from the yoga of the deity and ending with the torma, makes all this very clear, and so it is a chapter.

This concludes the third section, the presentation of the stages of the Seat of the Other, from the Smṛtinibandha, a commentary on the Glorious Catuṣpīṭha.

Now, in order to learn the particulars of the five nectars and the rest, the request made to the Blessed One is stated in the passage beginning "The five nectars." The line "The particulars of the five nectars" is connected with "I wish to hear from the Blessed One." Here "particulars" means distinguishing them one by one: earlier they were known without distinction, but here one wishes to know them, each distinguished individually.

In the line "By what are the five hooks blessed?" "by what" means by what particular feature, and "blessed" means accomplished. So it is to be construed as: "I wish to hear the particulars of those hooks, from what particular feature they are accomplished." Or else, it means by what seed they are blessed.

In the line "Nondual, the dharma of nonduality," "nondual" is suchness. "Nonduality" is the essential nature of the Perfection of Wisdom, a particular aspect of suchness. "Dharma" is its essential nature, the characteristic of having no intrinsic nature.

"How are dualistic conceptions abandoned?" refers to dualistic conceptuality: conceiving the nondual Perfection of Wisdom in the two aspects of existent thing and nonexistent thing.

"What are āli and kāli like?" means: what kind of union of method and wisdom is it?

In the line "What is the mind of the outer mudrā?" "the outer mudrā" is the verbal sign language, and "mind" is the mudrā of signs. Some texts read "sign" here, but there too the meaning is simply the mudrā of signs.

In the line "And the ways of the yoga treatises," "yoga" means the application of medicines as a means to accomplishment. That itself is a treatise: it is called a treatise because by it the minds of sentient beings are corrected and brought close to the sublime Dharma. "Way" means that by it sentient beings are brought to accomplishment. For through those accomplishments of medicine they are made to have faith in the path of the Buddha; through that faith they enter it; having entered, they come to practice; and from practice they attain perfect awakening itself. Thus, because this leads them step by step to perfect awakening and makes them attain it, it is a way. Or else, the yoga treatise itself is the way: because of it one engages in recitation, meditation and so on, and by the power of the merit of that one is led step by step to awakening and made to attain it.

The answer is given in the passage beginning "The Blessed One said." "First" means at the outset, and "should be known in particular" is supplied. What should be known? "The nectar vajra water": since it is both a portion of the vajra and of the nature of nectar, it is the water of the nectar vajra, that is, the awakening mind.

That single thing, divided into five particulars, is stated in the line "It enters as the nature of the five," meaning that it becomes the five great elements, whose nature is fire and the rest.

To explain what was thus taught, the passage beginning "What is warm to the touch is fire" is stated. "Warm to the touch" means known as warm by touching. In "What appears like smoke is wind," what appears like smoke is vapor, and that is the wind element. In "The nature of the fluid is called water," "fluid" means flowing downward and being oily, and "nature" means a portion: the oily and flowing portion is water. The word "water" means moisture. In "What appears as form is earth," "appears as form" means showing solidity; the meaning is that this portion is the earth element.

In the line "Wisdom, blessing, the vajra itself," it is wisdom because it makes known: the four great elements just described appear in the drop of the awakening mind. "Blessing" means abiding: opening room and abiding there. "The vajra itself" means space, because it is indivisible. This teaches the following: since the four great elements appear to abide in the drop of semen, one should know that space, which opens the room for them, is also present.

"One should meditate on them as five deities" means that one meditates on those five great elements as being of the nature of Amitābha, Amoghasiddhi, Akṣobhya, Vairocana and Ratnasambhava; the meaning is that one meditates on the single drop of semen as the five deities.

How is one to meditate on them? The passage beginning "Without remainder" is given. "Shape" is shape because it is connected with water; it is the letter sa. In "without remainder," kāli is a portion, that is, the moon. "Sa" means what enters together with it: because it is the seed of nectar, it is sa itself.

In the line "The consumer, fire itself," "the consumer" is the letter kṣa, because one finds kṣaya tvad. That itself is fire, because it shares the nature of fire.

"Likewise always wind" is the letter ya, because it is always of the nature of wind.

In the line "The goose, of equal meaning, sa, the lord," "the goose" is the letter haṃ. "Sa" is the seed of earth, that very letter. "Of equal meaning" means common to all things. What is it? It is "the lord": the meaning is that it is the witness of, and the ground for, all the states, past and the rest, of all places, such as emptiness and so on.

Thus the letter ha, called "the goose," is itself also sa, and since it is also the lord common to all things, it is sa, of equal meaning, and the witness.

"They are to be joined with sound and bindu" means that those four seeds are to be furnished with the bindu and the vowel sign u. By this they become suṃ yuṃ kṣuṃ hūṃ hūṃ.

In the line "The five letters of wisdom," "wisdom" is the letter hūṃ, and it is a letter because it is unchanging. This is the seed of space. "Five" means that in this way there are five kinds of seed.

"In its center, of the moon,
In the center, the letter of nectar":
this means that in the center of the awakening mind there is a moon maṇḍala, and that in the center of the form of the moon maṇḍala one meditates on the letter of nectar, ā.

"The five are to be declared as five" means that each nectar in turn has five kinds of element. Just as it was taught that one blesses the awakening mind, by the letters suṃ yuṃ kṣuṃ hūṃ hūṃ, the seeds of the five elements, as Akṣobhya, Amoghasiddhi, Amitābha, Vairocana and Ratnasambhava, and then above that again meditates on the letter of nectar, ā, upon a moon maṇḍala, so too one should examine the remaining four nectars in the same way. The meaning is that each of them abides as five portions of the great elements, and since each is blessed as five kinds of buddha, they become twenty-five.

In the line "Freed from thing and non-thing," a thing is arising, and a non-thing is non-arising. Freed from both means having abandoned the two, because one abides for a long time.

In the line "By the purification of the vajra water," "vajra water" is an illustrative mention, so the others are also to be included. "Purification" means making pure. The meaning is that when one makes use of it, the yogin is freed from thing and non-thing and becomes accomplished.

In the line "By that, delusion is put far away," delusion is the perception of impurity. "Put far away" means removed to a distance, because one possesses the perception of purity. Or else, by partaking of the nectars in this way, one gradually becomes free of the delusion that is bewildered about reality, because wisdom increases.

In the lines
"Just as the fine substances of outer nectar
Are purified by being cooked with fire,"
it should be seen here too that, just as outer substances cooked by fire become fine and pure and like nectar, so it is here. What is meant? The passage beginning "The belly" is given. In the lines
"The belly and the rest, by the fire of wisdom,
The excellent remainder: excrement and urine,"
"the belly" means what comes from the belly, excrement, urine and so on. To show just that, it says "excrement and urine." "Remainder" is construed with "cooked": purified by the fire of wisdom. Or else, it is construed with "the fire of the deity's wisdom": when excrement, urine and so on are purified by it, they become excellent; substances that by their nature have become special are called excellent because their power is superior.

In the lines
"Having cooked with fire, purify with fire;
Always offer them as a fire offering in the center,"
"Having cooked with fire, purify with fire" means that one first cooks them with the fire of the deity's wisdom, and after that burns the firewood of ordinary conceptions with the fire of the wisdom of samādhi. "Always offer them as a fire offering in the center" means that within the body, in the center, in the very fire of wisdom, one offers them again as a fire offering: the mantrin should always eat them.

In the lines
"The seed of the vajra water,
