Having recited it with the seed,"
"the seed of the vajra water" is the mantra that blesses the nectar. "With the seed" refers to the union of method and wisdom. The meaning is that, since it is to be purified by the union of method and wisdom, one should recite the very mantra by which the nectar is made pure. This teaches the purification through the equal union of vajra and lotus. Or else, it teaches that one should recite the very seed by which it was purified, and then eat.

For that very reason it is said:
"In its center, upon the sun,
Imagine the charnel ground letter and burn it."
"In its center" refers to the letter ā meditated on in the center of the nectar; this is to be connected with the earlier "recite it together with." If one asks what it is together with, it is the passage beginning "the sun." The charnel ground letter is kṣuṃ. "The sun" is the fire of the deity, blazing like the sun. This teaches that one should recite a kṣuṃ and burn. Further, one meditates on kṣuṃ upon the sun. The rest has already been explained.

"What is called the nectar water
Is renowned as the deities of three kinds":
this means that the nectars, the awakening mind and the rest, are to be made into the nature of the three deities, Lokeśvara and the others. With this the summary of the text is taught.

Now, in order to abandon clinging to the sequence, after answering the question on the five nectars, the text teaches the answer to the question about āli and kāli with the passage beginning "Āli." "Āli" is the taste of incomparable bliss. It is called "li" because by this one takes it and makes it auspicious: that is the vajra. That is also the bee, because it draws out and drinks the juice of the flower. Moreover, here it denotes method. That in the Hevajra and other texts āli is used to denote the nature of wisdom should be seen as a different intention, because there is no fixed assignment of meanings to words.

Or else, bhrama means confusion and ra means destroying, because the vajra itself is the support for destroying confusion. Some say that bhrama is saṃsāra and ra is sporting: because this vajra sports in the bliss of saṃsāra, it is called bhramara. It is also the Terrifier, because, being the support for realizing nonduality, it gives fear to the childish śrāvakas.

To teach the two aspects, the text says:

"Āli, reaching to the limits of space,
In the nature of the sphere of phenomena,"

and with this the awakening mind is taught. It is space in this sense: it is like the opening from which sentient beings are born and come forth. In pari anta, pari means bliss all around, and anta is derived from the verbal root in the sense of binding: because this awakening mind binds bliss all around, it "reaches to the limits." Dharma is so called because it holds its own characteristic, and these are sentient beings; and because this holds and nourishes them, it is the sphere, that is, the awakening mind. "In the nature" means the essential nature; the second case is used in the sense of the first. These terms teach synonyms of the awakening mind.

To teach the particular qualities of that very sphere, the passage beginning "the moving and" is given. The moving are so called because at all times they run after and engage in pointless activities: these are ordinary people. The unmoving are so called because they never engage in what is pointless: these are those who put investigation first. In "those of secret mind," "secret" is the wisdom in which method and wisdom are nondual; it is secret because ordinary people do not know it. "Mind" is the mind that bears its aspect. Those in whom these are present are those of secret mind: the buddhas and bodhisattvas. "Bodies" means embodied beings, these same ones. "Without remainder" means with none left out. "The producer" is the cause. The meaning is this: all embodied beings, whether complete or incomplete, arise from the awakening mind, which has the nature of the sphere of phenomena.

Having thus taught the essence of āli, then, in the course of teaching the location of the awakening mind, which comes up in connection with it, the text presents blood and semen as the heart of the five nectars, and further presents semen alone as having the nature of the five nectars. For this the passage beginning "of the ten and eight" is given. "The channels of the ten and eight" are the eighteen places, such as the breasts; this is connected with "having drawn it from them." "All the senses" means all the doors of the senses, such as the eye, and is construed with "from their center." "The essence nectar" is the awakening mind. "Āli" is the vajra. "Center" means "from the center": the seventh case is used in the sense of the fifth. "Dissolves" means that it is brought down into the center of the lotus. By what is it to be drawn? "By the sound of the terrifying vajra," that is, by the sound of the vajra opening the lotus. "The substance of union" is the awakening mind.

What is to be done after that? To teach the purification, the passage beginning "of wind and fire" is given; its meaning has already been explained.

To teach the extraction of the nectar letter, the text says, "The first vowel, the supreme seed." The first vowel is the short ā; since it is the most excellent of syllables, it is the supreme seed. "In its center" means in the center of the nectar, upon the moon. "Hold" means imagine. The rest is easy to understand. Or else, "in its center" means upon the crescent moon and bindu of hūṃ.

Or else, in the passage beginning "of all the senses," the awakening mind itself is "all" because it drips and flows. It is "the lord of the senses" because it makes reality known and because it is the chief giver of bliss. "Its" refers to the channels. "Center" means in the hub of the vajra. What happens through this? The text says, "Āli dissolves into the center." "Āli" is semen. "Center" is the wisdom consort. "Dissolves" means nonduality, that is, innate joy. "Becomes" means becomes directly manifest. These lines teach that one draws the awakening mind from the eighteen places, and when it reaches the center of the hub of vajra and lotus, one should make innate joy directly manifest.

How is it drawn? The text says "the terrifying vajra," and "sound" means the form of a sound. What is that? In the passage beginning "of wind and fire," "wind" is ya and "fire" is ra. "Seven" means the seventh. "Aim" means what comes at the end, because it is what is striven for. For just as the fruit at the very end of the path of a sādhana is called the aim because it is striven for, so here too the seventh from wind and fire, sa, and ha are the aim, in the sense of coming at the end. Or else, "seven" refers to the seven horses, and "aim" is their end. Construed in this way it reads "the seven-horsed," and since one speaks of asva and hala, the seven-horsed are sa and ha.

In "Join the seed of the Vajra Lady," the seed of the Vajra Lady is sa itself. "Join" means that, since it is of the nature of nectar and so accords with the crescent moon, it is made into the crescent moon of ha. The rest has already been explained.

Having thus given the answer concerning method, to teach the answer concerning wisdom, the passage beginning "Kāli" is given. "Flower" is the source of phenomena of women, because it is opened by the vajra sun. In "kāli," kā means bliss and li means tasting it: because this flower tastes the flavor of bliss, it is kāli. "Pressed down all around" is to be construed with the following "by the vajra-bearer," and the vajra-bearer is bliss. "Pressed down" means seated: the meaning is that inside the white triangular source of phenomena, upon the hub of a variegated lotus, at the core of the vajra, Vajradhara, Great Bliss, is seated. As it is also said:

"Because it guards bliss,
It is called the blissful."

Moreover, to teach that this is not bliss alone but is of the very nature of the innate, the text says "the body, the flower." Here, "flower" refers to the awakening mind, because it arises from the lotus flower. "Body" means the very nature of the body of the Blessed One. As it is said in the Hevajra:

"The Blessed One takes the form of semen."

On the lines
"It gives birth to all of saṃsāra,
And is called the earth and the mother of all,"
kāli itself is spoken of with words such as "earth" and "mother" because it has become the source of all sentient beings and the support of the bad and the good destinies.

To show that bliss too is taught by the word kāli, the passage beginning "of the water of wisdom" is given. "Here" refers back to the flower: here, in the source of phenomena. "The river of wisdom": the wisdoms, the mirror-like and the rest, are water because they dispel the torment of suffering, and that is bliss. That is spoken of by the word "wisdom," and the word "likewise" is to be referred back to the word kāli. "Ocean" is the source of phenomena itself, because it is the abode of that water of wisdom. As for "wisdom": wisdom is knowledge free of elaboration, which is bliss itself, and because the term is applied figuratively to its abode as well, that is also called so. "Sea monsters and fish" are the buddhas who are the purified forms of attachment and the rest; "filled with them" is to be supplied. As it is also said in the Hevajra:

"From the union of vajra and lotus,
What appears as solid is earth;
The nature of solidity is delusion;
Delusion is held to be Vairocana."

Here too, kāli should be seen as being indicated by "sea monsters and fish." As it is also said: "The precious casket of the buddhas."

In the passage beginning "Nondual," "nondual" means that one relies on the innate, in which semen, designated as āli, and bliss, designated as kāli, are not two. Or else, "nondual" is the outer mudrā. "Mud" means the mud-born, the lotus, and "center" is its heart. What is there? The text says "the sprout of method": it is a sprout because it is the heart of the lotus, and it is method because it is close to that bliss; it is the channel Khāgamukha. "Opened like a bandūka" means that it is opened like a bandūka flower. In the line "The abode of honey and nectar," honey is semen and nectar is menstrual blood; it is their source.

Then, to show that the menstrual blood among these is also of the nature of kāli, the text says:
"That very nectar of kāli
Is surrounded all about by the flower water."
"Flower" is menstrual blood, because it is close to the fruit. It is water because it drips like water. "Surrounded" means filled. These lines teach that when the channel called Khāgamukha opens, whether all at once or gradually, semen and menstrual blood will drip.

Now, in the course of thus teaching kāli, since the menstrual blood, the heart of nectar, is of the nature of the five kinds of meat, the procedure for partaking of it is taught with the passage beginning "the doubled letter." The doubled letter is kṣa, because it combines ṣa and kā. "Properly join the bindu and the nāda" means furnishing it with u and the bindu; when combined it becomes kṣuṃ. "The mother of the family of the deities" is the seed that generates the yoginīs. Or else, "the deity" is the god of fire, and "the mother" is its source.

Now, to teach that the heart of nectar, the awakening mind, and the menstrual blood are blessed together by the letter ā and by the kṣuṃ just mentioned, the passage beginning "the moon" is given. The meaning is that one meditates on them, each as fivefold, within the hub of the lotus.

To teach the remaining conventions of āli and kāli, and to teach a summary of the heart of nectar, the passage beginning "Āli" is given. Here āli and kāli are terms for male and female. "Tathāgatī" is so called because through her one goes thus; she is the action mudrā. "Born from the two bodies" refers to the menstrual blood together with the semen, and to the awakening mind. In "the supreme rite of nectar," nectar is an illustrative mention, so meat is also to be included. "Supreme" means excellent, because these two comprise everything.

Now, to teach clearly the extraction of the mantra of the letter ā, the passage beginning "the third class" is given. The third letter of the third class is ḍa. "The latter staff" is the u that has the form of ra, and it is to be attached beneath that. "The vajra-bearer" is ḍa itself, because it resembles the form of a vajra. "Bind it with the great girdle" is said in order to connect the two. This teaches that this is the ā that combines ta and ra, not the a made up of ta and ba.

Now, to teach the benefits of the seeds of the five nectars, the passage beginning "the five nectars" is given. Here the five nectars are: the awakening mind, taught in the passage beginning "First the vajra nectar water, / Āli reaching to the limits of space"; the two nectars taught in "the remainder: excrement and urine"; the menstrual blood taught by "the flower water"; and the nasal discharge taught by the oral instructions. Further, wherever the five nectars are mentioned and blood is mentioned by the word ra, menstrual blood itself is to be understood, not ordinary blood. Each of these five, too, is to be accomplished individually as fivefold, in the manner described before.

"By that very" refers to the rite of purification just mentioned: by that very reality. "One should wash the embodied self" means that the afflictions are to be purified. "The particulars of the five nectars" means the particulars of their purification. "By which it is known" extends as far as "by which, when it is eaten." "One does not die" means that one comes to be far from death, aging and the like; death is an illustrative mention. If one does die, then it is said, in the passage beginning "the door of the destinies," that the door of the destinies is the path of the destinies. Of what kind? The text says, "a pure body, without fault": it is the path of going to a human or divine body. "Seeing" means going there. The lines
"Knowing all the applications of nectar,
One should taste the nectar at will"
are easy to understand.

Alternatively, the meaning of this is that, through the union of the nectar of the mind of awakening, the non-duality of skillful means and wisdom, one should taste the flavor of the innate.

Now, to set out the answer concerning "the five hooks," the text says, "the five foods," and so on. "Particular" is according to the sequence stated earlier. The line "By which, when it is eaten with knowledge" means: if one eats it preceded by knowledge of the rite of purification. "One will swiftly see the destinies" has already been explained. Alternatively, "the destiny" is liberation and "seeing" is attaining it. "Swiftly" means in this very life. The intent is that what is otherwise hard to find even in countless eons will be found in this very life in which it is seen.

As for the hooks that are taught, in the passage beginning "Great Intellect": because it possesses intelligence it is "Great Intellect," that is, the elephant. Because it is utterly mute it is called "speech," a word used by way of antiphrasis: the cow. Alternatively, because this meat makes the voice sweet, it is "speech." Because it seeks rivalry with its own kind, it is "rival of its kind," that is, the dog. As for "touch-goer": because when ridden it goes far, it is the "touch-goer," that is, the horse. "The great hook" is easy to understand. "Like crystal" means that it is to be eaten after it has been purified and cleansed so as to be like crystal.

For that very reason the text says, in the passage beginning "consciousness": the seed of consciousness is the syllable hūṃ. "Should be held" means meditation: by recitation of the syllable hūṃ and by meditation one should purify the great hook. "With a crystal mind" means a mind clear like crystal, because it is free of the stains of conceptual thought.

"The hook of the obstructor": because it has the nature of a vināyaka, it is the "obstructor," that is, the flesh of the elephant. "One" means "the same": the rite of purification is the same as for the great hook. For that very reason the text says "hūṃ," which is easy to understand. "Lord of the place" is the dog, because it is powerful in its own place. The rest is easy to understand.

In the passage beginning "the hook of the horse," "the all-knowing" is the yogin, who, through the particulars of the hooks, possesses knowledge of the suchness of the seed syllables. "The vajra hook" is the hook that has "go" at its beginning, because through this hook the body becomes like a vajra and cannot be broken by sickness, aging and the like. For that very reason the text says, "It is called the vajra body."

To teach the benefits, the text says, "The yogins purify their wrongdoing": by the teaching of these hooks, the wrongdoing of karma and affliction of the yogins will be cleansed. For that very reason it is aṃkuśa: here aṃ means aim, that is, the result, and kuśa means doing and accomplishing.

To teach the times for the torma, the text says, "on the fourteenth," and so on: on the fourteenth and the other days of the waxing and waning halves of the month, one who is endowed with the yoga of the deity should give the torma according to the rite. How? The text says, "with great effort," that is, with diligence. To teach that if one gives the torma constantly and with certainty one will attain accomplishment, the text says, "constantly," and so on. "Enjoyment" means wealth and pleasures, characteristic of the higher realms. "Release" is liberation, the state of a buddha. "All actions will be accomplished" means that the rites of enrichment and the rest are accomplished.

To show just this, the text says, "the king's palace," and so on. The lines
"Even if one goes to the king's palace,
One swiftly becomes glorious"
mean: one endowed with the yoga of the wisdom ḍākinī, having purified a pill made from the ten substances and put it in the mouth, if one then goes to the king's palace, one's glory will increase immediately. "Beloved by friends" means becoming deeply cherished. "Pleasing" means brought under one's power. "By merely seeing" means by looking. In the lines
"Even the poor, destitute of wealth,
Will have a household prosperous and flourishing,"
the poor are the destitute, those without wealth. "Will have a household prosperous and flourishing" means that the household will become filled with wealth. To all of these one should apply the same procedure as before. The passage beginning "at the time of death" is easy to understand.

In the passage beginning "with fragrance," "fragrance" means any fragrant substance whatever. "Combined" means burned. Combined with what? The text says, "the ten substances." The ten substances are the five nectars, namely those known as Vairocana, Ratnasambhava, Amitābha and Akṣobhya, and the one known as Amoghasiddhi, the excrement and the rest; and the five hooks, go ku da ha na.

"The mantra of ten syllables" means the five heart syllables of the five buddhas, hūṃ hūṃ kṣuṃ yuṃ suṃ, and the five heart syllables of go ku da ha na, sriṃ griṃ hūṃ jṝṃ hūṃ: ten in all. "With homage, and ending with craving" means inserting oṃ at the beginning and svāhā at the end. Put together, it becomes oṃ hūṃ hūṃ kṣuṃ yuṃ suṃ sriṃ griṃ hūṃ jṝṃ hūṃ svāhā.

"The yogin, having recited it a hundred thousand times" means that one endowed with the yoga of the wisdom ḍākinī should first perform the approach by reciting this mantra a hundred thousand times. "Having blessed the torma of the ḍākinīs" means that this is preceded by the torma. "The practitioner fumigates with its smoke" means that, immediately after the mantra recitation, one fumigates oneself with the smoke over which the mantra has been recited one hundred and eight times. "All beings will be bewildered" is a synonym for their becoming attached and brought under one's power. One should understand that this very procedure is the rite of approach and so forth for the others as well. The rest, being easy to understand, is not explained.

The lines
"One should apply them in rites;
What I have taught is beyond doubt"
mean that if one applies and accomplishes the rites of subjugation and the rest with those substances, saffron and the others, they will certainly be accomplished. This is the intent, and it is the conclusion.

Now, to teach the accomplishment of the yoginīs, the text says, "messenger," and so on. If one asks why the yoginīs are to be accomplished, the text says:
"Through the work of a messenger's task,
In order to seek accomplished yoginīs."
That is, in order to seek accomplished yoginīs as messengers who perform all tasks; this extends as far as "to make one's own." The answer is given in the passage beginning "messenger." "By applying them thoroughly to the work of messengers" means that the yoginīs are to be made messengers. "Because they are the heart of all actions" means that they are chief, because they are powerful in all actions.

To teach the rite of accomplishment, the text says, "a single tree," and so on. The lines
"At the foot of a single tree,
Recite a hundred thousand times according to the rite"
teach, first, the approach, for accomplishing the Blazing Lady. Here "a single tree" means one that has no companion within the distance of an earshot. "Recite a hundred thousand times according to the rite" means that the yogin who is devoted to keeping the sacred commitments, preceded by the giving of torma, recites one hundred thousand times before the painted image.

To teach that very rite, the text says, "one cubit," and so on. "Painted scroll" means, according to the oral instructions, one made from a cloth bearing menstrual blood. If one asks how large it should be made: "one cubit together with a quarter," that is, one cubit plus one quarter of a cubit. "Draw" means drawing the form of the Blazing Lady. How large? "To the measure of twelve finger-widths," that is, having a height of twelve finger-widths. How? The passage beginning "yellow in color" is easy to understand. "With effort, one should begin the rite of worship" is a brief statement. To explain it, the text says, "with fragrance and flowers," and so on. This teaches that, having worshiped with red fragrance and red flowers, one fumigates with an incense of dried meat and gives the torma of enjoyments.

In "the five substances and the five things," "the five substances" are the five nectars, and "the five things" are go ku da ha na, the five meats. "Having worshiped, recite a hundred thousand times likewise" means that, after the approach of a hundred thousand recitations preceded by torma, one should then perform the accomplishment with a hundred thousand recitations, again preceded by torma.

To teach the signs of accomplishment, the text says, "In the first," and so on, which is easy to understand. When the messenger has come, in order to bring her into accord by showing the mantra and mudrā of the sacred commitment, the text says, "Having uttered her mantra," and so on. "Her mantra" is the mantra recited at the time of the approach and so forth. "Having uttered" means reciting that very mantra also at the time of binding the mudrā. To teach that, after she is pleased, one fills a skull cup with the liquid of the five nectars and brings her under one's power by giving the sacred commitment substance, the text says, "the five nectars," and so on; the meaning is clear.

To teach requesting the accomplishments once she has been brought under one's power, the text says, "with effort," and so on. The lines
"Bless me in non-duality with the yoginī,
And now accomplish it!"
mean: "Yoginī, accept me, so that you and I may join our two organs in union." One should say this in order to accomplish her as consort. The rest, being easy to understand, is not explained.

The lines
"She enjoys union for half a watch,
The yoginī who craves sexual union"
mean that, because the yoginī, the Blazing Lady, greatly craves sexual union, she becomes pleased only after being satisfied by engaging in union for the duration of half a watch, and not otherwise.

In the lines
"If she is not accomplished,
Then recite the seed of the Fierce Lady,"
the seed of the Fierce Lady is the syllable kṣuṃ. "Recite" means to recite it interspersed with that mantra: if she is not accomplished by the former rite, one should accomplish her by reciting kṣuṃ oṃ dīpeśvari hūṃ sraṃ pheṃ kṣuṃ a hundred thousand times. "Merely tormented by the seed" means by reciting it interspersed with the seed of the Fierce Lady. "The crown of the Blazing Lady's head will split" completes "if she is not accomplished"; the intent is that she will certainly be accomplished.

The lines
"The practitioner striving for the benefit of beings
Should accomplish the Blazing Lady as consort"
mean that if one wishes to train beings through giving food, wealth and the like, then one should accomplish the Blazing Lady as consort.

To teach the accomplishment of the remaining others, the text says, "of the accomplished," and so on. "Others" means the remaining ones other than the Blazing Lady. "Ḍākinī": from among them the Ḍākinī is named; she is the white one. "On a painted cloth of two cubits less" means two cubits short by one quarter. The lines
"Bless the offerings and the torma,
And then draw the form"
mean that, having drawn the form, one should accomplish her through recitation preceded by torma. "In the region of a charnel ground" means at a place where corpses are burned. If one asks what is special about it, the text says, "everywhere," and so on, which is easy to understand. Here too one should apply the rites of approach, accomplishment, pleasing, bringing under control and the rest, just as taught before.

"The seed of the Vajra Lady" is the syllable suṃ. "According to the rite taught before" means the rite of interspersing the mantra taught in another chapter; here it becomes suṃ oṃ ḍākinī hūṃ hūṃ phu suṃ. It should be understood in the same way elsewhere as well. The rest, being easy to understand, is not explained in detail.

The lines
"If she is not accomplished by the action taught,
One should accomplish her by another action"
teach, in brief, that if she is not accomplished by the peaceful action mentioned before, one accomplishes her by another, wrathful rite. To explain this, the text says, "oneself," and so on. The meaning of these lines is this: meditating on oneself as the wisdom ḍākinī, pressing down the painted image of the Ravisher with the left foot, one should recite the Ravisher's own mantra five hundred times and accomplish her by threatening.

To teach accomplishing her by the action of destruction if she is not accomplished even by threatening, the text says, "if," and so on. "In the midst of the heroes" means in the center of the circle of yoginīs. "Killing" is the syllable phaṭ. "Loud" means reciting fiercely.

"By meditating on the seed hūṃ" means meditating on the pieces of the painted image as the syllable hūṃ. "One should imagine eating everywhere" means offering them into the fire as a fire offering. "The sovereign lady of all" is the chief lady, that is, the Ravisher.

To explain that very point again, the text says, "If she is not accomplished by the action taught," and so on; this is to be connected with the two actions taught, the ordinary one and that of threatening. "The black one" is the riṣṭaka tree; taking "bālataga and the names of the black tree" as a pair, one should make the connection.

"White mustard seed together with blood" means that blood, white mustard seed, the other mustard seed, poison and nimba are smeared with pungent black oil and burned together with the pieces of the painted image; blood and white mustard seed are mentioned merely as examples. The lines
"Wailing, she comes,
And will surely accomplish the actions"
mean that, tormented by suffering and afraid that her body will be destroyed, she will swiftly come into the presence of the practitioner and do just as she is commanded.

This wrathful rite is not for the Ravisher alone: one should understand that other yoginīs too, if they are not accomplished by their respective actions as stated, are to be subdued by this same rite. For that very reason the text says, "Or the ḍākinīs will die"; "the ḍākinīs" means the yoginīs.

Here, the answer to the question
"The nondual dharma, without duality:
How is the conceptuality of two to be abandoned?"
is taught by the lines
"Being freed from entity and non-entity,
By that one goes far from delusion."
The answer to "the way of the yoga treatises" is taught by the passage beginning "By combining with fragrances." As for the remaining questions and answers, one need only understand each of them separately.

Now, in entering the maṇḍala of the gathering, in order to make supplication to the gatekeeper Nīladaṇḍa, the text says, "Blue Wrathful One," and so on. "Blue" mentions one part, dropping the first half of the word: here only "Blue" is said for the one who holds the blue staff, just as Amoghasiddhi is called "Amogha" and Samantāvalokita is called "All-Seeing." "Pleasing commitment" means a commitment agreeable to the mind; it is a commitment because the wrathful one himself is not to be harmed. If one asks how he is beautiful, the text says,
"Holding in his hand a staff
Bound with precious, cherished jewels."
The plural word in "holding precious jewels" is used to include jewels of various kinds, differing in color, shape, qualities and the like. "Cherished" is because those jewels are as dear to him as life. "Bound" means adorned. These words are a calling out to the wrathful one.

If one asks what is to be done after calling him, one should make supplication with the words beginning "I." "I" connects with "please send me into the midst of the assembly of yoginīs." Why is this? "In order to deliver me from saṃsāra," that is, in order to deliver me from the terror of saṃsāra. "Hero" is praise of the wrathful one, because he has no fear of saṃsāra, or because he is victorious in the battle with the afflictions. "Grant" is praise of oneself; it shows that one is fortunate to enter the maṇḍala of the gathering. "Excellent" means virtuous, because it is the very nature of the city of liberation. "Send" means "send me!" Some say that by this verse the disciple too should be brought into the maṇḍala in the same way as entering the maṇḍala of the gathering.

Now, although supplication concerning the mudrās of speech and body was made earlier, because it was interrupted by much explanation, and because it was interrupted by teaching the other accomplishments of the four yoginīs not included in the saṃvara, out of a sense of doubt the urging is taught again; therefore the supplication is taught with the passage beginning "yoga." "Great secret" is because it is hidden from ordinary people by way of signs. "The secret by the secret" means what is to be known by the yogins and yoginīs.

The answer is given in the passage beginning "of speech." "On the right side, the yoginī" means that the yoginī gives the sign with the right side of the yogin, or, standing on his right side, engages in the signs. "On the left, the yoginī herself" is to be understood in the same way. The passage beginning "putting the finger into the mouth" teaches the bodily signs of the yoginīs. The passage beginning "go ghu" teaches the verbal signs of the yogins. "Squeezing the thumb" is the binding of the middle finger. As for the rest, one need only understand the signs.

In the passage beginning "the Seat of the Other," "section" means making clear: because this text, from the reality of the five nectars up to the culmination in the signs of body and speech, shows these fully, clearly and extensively, it is a "section." "The Seat of the Other": the four chapters that come to be heard after the four chapters of the Seat of Self, beginning with the fire offering and culminating in the accomplishment of the yoginīs, are "other" because they teach the practices for the benefit of others and teach the chief of the practices; and because they are a source of accomplishments, they are a "seat." "Complete" means brought to conclusion.

This is the fourth chapter of the Seat of the Other, from the commentary on the Glorious Catuṣpīṭha called Smṛtinibandha.

Now, with reference to the Seat of Union, the text says, "what is to be indicated," and so on. "Other" means the third seat; it is "other" in relation to the Seat of the Other. Within it, first, in order to understand the four kinds of reality, the supplication beginning "what is to be indicated" is given. "What is to be indicated" is the four, the vajra and the rest. "Characteristic" is their nature, that is, their reality.

As for "Having understood the four realities in what way, how should they be applied?": the four realities are the vajra, the bell, the rosary, and the wisdoms of the saṃsāra of the five classes of beings. The meaning is: how are their realities to be understood, and, having understood them, how are they to be applied, that is, how are they to be taken up?

The answer is given in the passage beginning "liberation from saṃsāra." "The characteristic of liberation from saṃsāra" is the characteristic of the vajra and the rest: because this nature of reality liberates beings from the ocean of saṃsāra, it is the characteristic of liberation from saṃsāra. Its nonduality is set out in the passage beginning "First is the reality of the vajra," which is easy to understand.

To explain clearly that very "characteristic of liberation from saṃsāra," the text says:
"By the waves of the four realities,
One carries across to the far shore as one wishes."
The meaning is that, within the ocean of conception, by the waves of the sublime conception of the four realities, one carries the hosts of sentient beings across to the far shore, that is, one brings them to the attainment of liberation.

Of these, to teach first the reality of the vajra, the text says, "of the center," and so on. "Of the center" is the fifth prong of the five-pronged vajra. Because it is completely pure, it is a "deity": Vairocana, the complete purity of the earth element. Alternatively, "deity" is the mirror-like wisdom. "First of all" means the first of all, in relation to Ratnasambhava and the others; or else it is the east of all, in relation to the remaining prongs. "The five prongs are the deities" means that they are the complete purity whose nature is the five wisdoms.

In "The lotus, the eight limbs of awakening," the lotus is the eight-petaled lotus located at the end of the grip, and the limbs of awakening are the eight limbs of the path of awakening. "Ground" means location. "Placed" means set out. If one asks who they are, the text says, "Padmapāṇi," and so on. Padmapāṇi is Lokeśvara. "Of the sky" is Gaganagañja, that is, Ākāśagarbha. "The lord of yakṣas" is Vajrapāṇi. "Earth" is Kṣitigarbha. The passage beginning "These eight deities" is a summary.

In "The thirteen in the center, in the egg," the center is the grip of the vajra, "tritasa" means the deities, and the egg is the location. "The wisdom deity is to be dissolved" means imagining the great Vajradhara together with the wisdom ḍākinī. "On the eight petals of the lotus" means on the eight petals of the other lotus at the end of the grip. "The goddesses" are Vajrāṅkuśī and the rest. "One should place them in their proper positions" means that Vajrāṅkuśī and the others are set out in order on the petals of the east and the other directions, and the Inciting Lady and the others are set out in order on the petals of Īśāna and the other intermediate directions. "The one named Vajraśṛṅkhalā" is said in relation to the other one called Śṛṅkhalā mentioned earlier: "named" shows that the names are alike, while the meaning is that they are different.

"The ḍākinīs of the five prongs" means that the five lower prongs are imagined as the five ḍākinīs. "One should imagine them as before" means that the five prongs are set out separately, as explained. For that very reason the text says, "in the east," and so on. "The deities of the reality of the vajra" are the deities mentioned before. "The wise should understand this" is the summary of the reality.

To teach the characteristics of measurement and the rest, the text says, "three parts," and so on, which is easy to understand. "Beautiful like the utpala and the kumuda" means that the prongs of the vajra are round and beautiful like utpala and kumuda flowers.

To teach the reality of the nine-pronged vajra, the text says, "directions," and so on; by the word "and so on" the intermediate directions are to be included. "The central prong is the Buddha himself" means Vairocana himself, as with the five-pronged vajra. The remaining eight prongs are imagined as the bodhisattvas Padmapāṇi and the rest and as the eight goddesses Vajrāṅkuśī and the rest. For that reason the text says, "nine deities." "The deity himself" means that all of them are meditated on as the great Vajradhara himself. The rest is as explained before.

"Prongs arising from a makara" means that the prongs other than the central prong are to be made as emerging from the mouth of a makara. "The rays abide on the moon" means abiding on the disc of the moon. These are the common characteristics. "Nine-pronged" means having nine prongs: the complete purity of the nine branches of scripture.

In "The wisdom vajra liberates beings," the vajra is the five-pronged one, and wisdom is the five wisdoms, the mirror-like wisdom and the rest; the meaning is the complete purity of the five wisdoms. "Liberates beings" comes from the common actions. Alternatively, "beings" are beings, and "liberate" means completely purify; the meaning is "having the nature of the vajra." If one asks from what, the text says, "Because whatever is the vajra itself," and so on: the reality of the vajra is the nature of the vajra. What is it like? The text says, "One should contemplate oneself as having the nature of the vajra." "Oneself" is the conception of self and other. "Having the nature of the vajra" means the intrinsic nature of the vajra. "Contemplate" means one should understand; the meaning is that one should understand all conceptions of self and other to be, ultimately, nondual.

In "The yogin, vajra essence," "essence" is the essence of the mind, the nature of reality, and "vajra" is nonduality. "The yogin" connects with "should take it to mind." The meaning is that it is to be viewed as having the nature of the vajra.

If one asks what is to be done after that, the text says, "Having shown the purity of the vajra": one makes it firm by saying, vajraśuddhaḥ sarvadharmā vajraśuddho'haṃ. To make this clear, the text says,
"Phenomena are nothing other than the vajra;
All things are that very vajra."
The meaning is that, because ultimately all phenomena are without distinction, there is nothing other than the reality of the vajra.

"The essence of vajra action" means that the actions of pacifying and the rest also have the essential nature of the vajra, because the essence of the dharma-sphere pervades everything. "The application of radiating" means radiating from nondual wisdom as the maṇḍala of deities. The passage beginning "The vajra carries across saṃsāra" teaches the activity of the vajra; being mostly easy to understand, it is not explained.

In the passage beginning "So too the afflictions are darkness," the meaning is this: just as a lamp destroys darkness and makes things clear, so the vajra of wisdom destroys the darkness of the afflictions, and then that lamp of wisdom removes all obscurations and illuminates "all directions," that is, all phenomena; it makes them clear.

To teach that what is to be accomplished and the rest also have one taste in the nondual vajra, the text says,
"All the accomplishments of the vajra and the rest
Are to be viewed as having the nature of the vajra."
To teach that bondage by the afflictions and liberation from it also have the nature of the vajra, the text says,
"The binding of phenomena is the vajra;
Striving for supreme liberation is the vajra."

To teach the accomplishment of beings through skillful means and wisdom, the text says,
"Having pressed the vajra on the lotus,
One should accomplish all that reality."
The meaning is that all worldly and transworldly phenomena, which are like a lotus, are pressed down and seized by the nondual vajra and accomplished. Alternatively, the meaning is that all beings are the vajra and the lotus, and are accomplished by the reality of skillful means and wisdom.

To teach that this vajra is also to be held by the master, the text says,
"The vajra that has entered the lotus
Should be accomplished by the wise."
The meaning is that this vajra that holds all phenomena is to be held by "the wise," that is, by the master who practices the reality of the vajra.

Now, to teach the reality of the bell, the text says, "of the bell," and so on. "The sound of wisdom" is the sound of the Perfection of Wisdom: "All phenomena are emptiness. All phenomena are without conditioning. All phenomena are by nature beyond sorrow," and so forth. To teach that this very sound pervades all places, times and the rest, the text says,
"Reaching to the limits of the sphere of space,
It abides at the seat of awakening."
The meaning is that, until one abides at the seat of awakening, that sound is proclaimed and meditated on as pervading all places and the rest. To explain again "reaching to the limits of the sphere of space," the text says, "by the knowledge of all," and so on, which is easy to understand.

The meaning of the passage beginning "What are called the eight directions" is this: on the directions and intermediate directions of the eight lotus petals, the eight goddesses, Tārā and the rest, are set out as forms or as seed syllables. "Fair-Colored" is Vasudhārā. "Sweet-Voiced" is the Inciting Lady. "Beautiful Lady" is Bhṛkuṭī. "Vajra Garland Lady" is the one who has the vajra garland. The rest, being easy to understand, is not explained.

"The upper lotus and vajras" means the upper prongs of the vajra. "One should imagine them as before" means imagining them as Vairocana and the rest, as taught in the section on the vajra.

"The central wisdom staff" connects with "should be shaken." How? The text says, "āli kāli": here āli is the vajra and kāli is the bell. "Joining" means holding them together: one takes up the vajra by joining the right thumb and ring finger, and holds the bell by joining the left thumb and ring finger. "One should shake it toward the eight directions" means that one should sound it eight times, toward the eight directions beginning with the east, and not more or fewer than that. What does that bring about? The text says, "āli kāli," and so on: through the union of skillful means and wisdom, the bonds of saṃsāra are cut.

In "The vajra bell is skillful means," the bell marked with a vajra is the vajra bell; because it accords with the result, it is skillful means. "The vajra of skillful means abides in the center" means that the vajra itself is skillful means, because the two accord with each other. By this it is taught that at the time of torma and the like one does not accomplish with the bell alone, without the vajra, or with the vajra alone, without the bell, but accomplishes by joining them as a pair.

"From the center" has as its implied remainder "should be held": the meaning is that it should be held from the center of the grip. To teach the rite of holding, the text says, "the right," and so on, which is easy to understand. In "One should apply skillful means to the bodies," skillful means is the vajra and bell, with which the body is adorned. How? The text says, "One should imagine one's chosen deities," which has already been explained.

The verses for holding the vajra and bell are given in the passage beginning "With the song of hūṃ"; "song" means uttering the words. The verses beginning "By beautifully brandishing the vajra" are to be recited while brandishing the vajra. The lines
"By beautifully brandishing the vajra,
One should deliver beings into liberation"
mean that the nondual vajra frees beings from the view that grasps at things as real, and then they behold the ultimate truth.

For that very reason the text says "liberation" vimukta: vi means conceptual thought, and mukta means freed. Alternatively, vi means correct discernment and mukta means unmixed, to be construed as "one should deliver beings by that discriminating wisdom." From what? The text says, "liberation," and so on: it is to be made the vajra Dharma freed from conceptual thought. Alternatively, vi is conceptual thought and mukta is respect toward it, because the list of verbal roots says, "mukta is accompanied by respect."

"The vajra, supreme and utmost essence" means that it is the essence because it is firm, and it is supreme because its nature is emptiness. The passage beginning "oṃ" is to be recited when holding the bell.

"Of the other form of wisdom and means" means that, having made oneself into the form of the deity, one rings the bell in order to rouse the other deity forms. "Of wisdom and means" means through the nonduality of emptiness and entities. "By the dharma of wisdom and means" is that very mode of nonduality; it is construed as "or that mode is to be realized by beings." The word "or" is to be construed in a different order, because it applies to conceptual thought: it means ringing and causing to ring. For that very reason it says "not knowing" and so on, with reference to the garment.

To summarize the text, it says "vajra" and so on. "Reality" is the ultimate: the vajra is held through the purity of the ultimate. "Of the Dharma" is the sound of such words as "All phenomena are emptiness"; through the purity of that sound the bell is rung. In a second way, the vajra is purity, namely the Mind; the bell is the body, namely the Body; and the ringing is the Speech. "The dharma of reality" is purity: these are to be held.

"The yogin who acts for the benefit of beings should view it as wisdom and means" means that this mode should also be applied at the time of empowering a disciple.

"A yogin without that reality is like the sound of an elephant's bell," and so on, means that those who do not know reality should not ring the bell, and that the master who practises reality should hold the vajra and bell.

Now, in order to teach the reality of the rosary, it says "rosary" and so on; since these lines are mostly easy to understand, they are not analyzed. "The eight directions and intermediate directions, and the center itself: the nine buddhas" means the eight bodhisattvas, Padmapāṇi and the rest, together with Vajradhara, making nine. "One should bless it with the nine deities" means that they are to be placed upon the threads. In order that thread spun by a girl be taken, it says "form and so on." "With a rosary spun by her, the accomplishment of liberation will arise" means that if one recites mantras with a rosary strung on thread spun by that girl of special qualities, one will gradually attain liberation through accomplishments such as pacifying. The rest is easy to understand and is not analyzed.

The mantra for purifying the rosary is spoken: "syllables" and so on. The syllables are the vowels and consonants. Whatever thing arises from those syllables as its cause is "the thing arising from the vowels and consonants as cause," namely the mantra essences. "Beautiful" means pure. By what is it purified? Since it is to be purified by selflessness, it says, "If one recites the numbers without intrinsic nature." "Without intrinsic nature" means having no essential nature, like an echo, that is, through selflessness. "Numbers" means the mantras. What comes of this? It says, "The essence of the yoginī is accomplished": the wisdom of the yoginīs is accomplished, or their mantras are especially accomplished. For that very reason it says "brahmin woman" and so on, which is easy to understand. Alternatively, it is like this: the brahmin is the body, the kṣatriya is the mind, and the śūdra is speech; accomplishment comes through their becoming one. Accomplishment of what? Of the net of yoginīs, the assembly of mantras.

"By this mantra" and so on: from "the thing arising from the syllables as cause" up to the final "svāhā" is this mantra, and with it the rosary is to be prepared. In "rosary," that is, counting garland, the counting is the mantras; and since one strings, that is, repeats them by means of it, it is a counting garland, a garland. "By" and so on intends that if one recites mantras with this, the deities will be accomplished, but not otherwise.

Now, in order to teach the reality of the wisdom of the saṃsāra of the five destinies, it says "wisdom" and so on. "Distinction" means particularity. "Dwelling far apart" means being established as distinct, that is, the distinctions of wisdom by which beings are set apart as distinct. "It is spoken of as common wisdom" means that although this is so, all are of one taste in the nature of wisdom, by the reasoning, "All beings are mere consciousness." "Lowly and very lowly" and so on are the asuras. "Yakṣa" is simply what is well known. "Non-gods" are the rākṣasīs. "Accomplished ones" are the gods. "Egg" means the egg-born. "Imperceptible" means those not perceived by the senses. The rest is easy to understand.

To teach the divisions of the gods, it says, "Twenty-three abodes of the gods." "Abodes" means grounds, or separate abodes: the separate abodes of the gods are twenty, together with the remaining three. "Doubling from higher to higher" means that the lifespans of those gods increase twofold from each level to the next above. Their sequence is this. The separate abodes of the gods who enjoy desire are six: the Four Great Kings, the Heaven of the Thirty-Three, Yāma, Tuṣita, Nirmāṇarati, and Paranirmitavaśavartin. Above these, the gods of the form realm are a further seventeen. Of these, the separate abodes of the first meditative concentration are two: Brahmakāyika and Brahmapurohita. Mahābrahmā is not counted separately, because it is not distinct from these. Those of the second meditative concentration are three: Parīttābha, Apramāṇābha and Ābhāsvara. Those of the third meditative concentration are also three: Parīttaśubha, Apramāṇaśubha and Śubhakṛtsna. Those of the fourth meditative concentration are eight: Anabhraka, Puṇyaprasava, Bṛhatphala, Avṛha, Atapa, Sudṛśa, Sudarśana and Akaniṣṭha. The seventeenth is those of subtle perception, namely the gods without perception and the gods of the four formless spheres. Some say that Mahābrahmā too is included among those of subtle perception. Thus there are twenty-three.

Among these, a hundred human years make one day and night of the gods of the Four Great Kings. Thirty of those make a month, and twelve months make a year. As for the lifespan of the gods of the Four Great Kings, they live for five hundred such years. Likewise, as for the length of life of the gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three, counting a thousand human years as one day and night, and reckoning months and years in sequence from that, they live for a thousand years. As for those of Yāma, counting two thousand human years as one day and night, and reckoning in the same sequence, they live for two thousand years. In the same way, the remaining three should also be reckoned, each doubling the last. For those of the form realm there is no counting of days and nights, so their lives should be reckoned by the count of eons; and for them too the lifespan should be doubled from each level to the one above. The rest is easy to understand, so it has not been explained in detail.

On the passage beginning "the Seat of Union": "chapter" (prakaraṇa) means "that which thoroughly clarifies." Because this text clearly sets forth the suchness of the vajra and bell and so on, it is a chapter.

This concludes the first section, the Seat of Union, of the commentary on the Glorious Catuṣpīṭha called Smṛtinibandha.

Now, in order to teach the section on accomplishing pacification and the other rites by means of wheels and so on, the text says, "The particular features of accomplishment by the wheel," and so on. "Accomplishment by the wheel" refers to the rites to be accomplished, namely pacification and the rest. "Particular features" means distinctions; it is to be construed as "listen to the distinctions of the rites of pacification and the rest mentioned earlier." In order to teach what is to be accomplished, it says "pacification and" and so on. In order to teach the wheel used for accomplishment, it says "compartments" and so on. "Nine compartments" means the eight spokes of the wheel and its hub; these nine are the nine compartments. "Surround it outside with a rim" means that one visualizes it encircled on the outside by a rim like a belt. If one asks what it is like, the text says "the wheel" and so on: it has the color that corresponds to the rite, pacification or another.

The mantra is given: oṃ and so on. "All those seeds": the words to be supplied are "should be placed in the compartments of the wheel." The meaning is that the mantra seed syllables, the letter tā and the others, are to be written in the compartments of the east and the other directions. On "Place the homage and the last in the center": the homage is the letter oṃ, and the last is hā. One should visualize these seeds, the first and the last, in the central compartment. "In the center, the name and the word of protection should likewise be joined" means that between oṃ and ha one should visualize the name of the person to be accomplished and the word rakṣa, set in among them as words; "joined" means set in among. Put together, it becomes oṃ Devadatta rakṣa ha.

If one asks how those wheels are to be meditated upon, the text says "sameness" and so on. "Sameness, empty" means emptiness taken beyond a merely partial emptiness, that is, the emptiness of all things. "One's own body should be washed" means washing away the stain of ordinary conceptions with the water of the emptiness of all things. On "Through the knowledge of the sameness of all, the abode; the wheel of protection": "knowledge of the sameness of all" is what is called the meditative absorption of knowing all as empty. "Abode" means the support, that is, the body of the meditator. It is protected by the wheel of protection; the meaning is that by meditating on the wheel, preceded by meditation on emptiness, the body is protected. Alternatively, the meaning here is that after meditating on emptiness one should protect the outer place with the wheel of protection, the vajra net and the rest. "Make it principal" means that at the time of the rite of subjugation, oneself and others are principally red in color.

"When one possessed of wisdom meditates on protection, he will be far removed from all fears" means that this wheel of protection keeps at a distance all fears, such as the king's prison. On "Beings into non-entity, by the entity of union, upon fear": "beings as non-entity" is the meditative absorption of the emptiness of all things. "Into" means "from that"; it is a second-case ending with the sense of the fifth case, the ablative. In "the entity of union," "union" is union with the deity, and "entity" is meditation. If one asks upon what, it is "upon fear." The meaning here is that at the time of pacifying fears such as illness, one meditates on oneself as the body of the deity, endowed with the color and other features that accord with the rite.

That deity yoga itself is taught in the passage beginning "the moon disk." Their meaning is this: after the yoga of emptiness, upon an eight-petaled lotus, on a moon disk, there is a blue lotus arisen from the seed letter tāṃ, marked with tāṃ; this transforms completely, and one should meditate on oneself in the form of Tārā. At the end of the generation, the placing on the six limbs is taught: oṃ tāre on the head, and so on. By these the six places are blessed.

Now, in order to teach the specifics of the hands and so on, it says "two hands" and so on. "Granting fearlessness to all" means granting fearlessness to all people; the words to be supplied are "the right hand." "The other" is the left; "holding an utpala by the ear" means holding an utpala whose petals touch the ear. "Having meditated on all as radiant" means that at the time of protection one meditates on the Blessed Lady and the mantras as blue-green in color, and contemplates in particular that protection is accomplished by all the light rays of her body and the light rays of the wheel together with its mantras. "In particular, recite the mantra" means that at the time of protection one recites in particular the garland mantra oṃ tāre tuttāre ture Devadatta rakṣaḥ svāhā. "It is the mantra of all protection" means that it is the mantra for protection from all fears, such as being bound in prison by the king. The rest is easy to understand.

"Other wisdom, and laziness and the rest" is connected with the earlier "should be contemplated." One who wishes to become keen of mind, too, should set out the mantra counterclockwise on that same earlier wheel and set out the letters a and the rest on its rim as well; if one then performs meditation and recitation, the dull-witted come to possess intelligence. "Having drawn the wheel, it should be worn" means drawing the wheel on birch bark or the like and wearing it on the body. "Excellent protection" means that even by that much alone it becomes the supreme protection. "By this" refers to the sequence taught earlier; the meaning is: by the sequence of meditating on the mantra through the yoga of Tārā for the sake of protection, reciting it, and writing it.

In order to sum up, the text says "protection" and so on: for the sake of all protection. "The union with suchness" means that one should accomplish the wheel of the suchness of deity yoga.

Now, in order to teach the wheel of wisdom, the text says "wheel" and so on. "A wheel of eight petals" means a wheel of eight spokes. "Having encircled it outside with a rim" means that one visualizes the outside of the wheel encircled by two lines. "In the center, the first and the last" means that, as with the wheel taught earlier, on this wheel too one should write in the central compartment the first and last letters, oṃ and hī. "All the recitations should be placed" means writing the remaining mantras to be recited on the outer spokes. "Having encircled the rim with vowels" means writing the vowel letters on the rim. So it comes to this: in the center one writes the seeds oṃ hā; on the spokes, the seeds prajñe hūṃ svā; and on the rim one writes the vowel letters. Here too, this is to teach that if one meditates on the wheel through deity yoga preceded by meditation on emptiness, one will obtain the benefits stated below.

The text says "all the limbs" and so on. "All the limbs dissolved" means the destruction of the aggregates and all the rest. In order to teach this, it says:

"With the characteristic described before,
having contemplated sameness as empty."

"With the characteristic described before" means making objects, faculties and consciousnesses into the characteristic of emptiness by means of the passage beginning "form is empty," taught in the third chapter, the Seat of Self; here too, by that sequence, one purifies oneself through sameness. "It should be purified as taught before" means meditating as in the case of emptiness without appearance; the meaning is that this too should be purified by emptiness, so that it is like an illusion. "By meditating on the wheel in this way" means then meditating on the wheel as explained before. oṃ and so on are the mantras to be written, and the mantras beginning pra should be written counterclockwise.

Here, at the very beginning, for all yogins: seated on a comfortable seat, one looks at one's own seed syllable at the heart, and with its light rays makes the guru, the buddhas and the rest appear. One worships them with offerings of flowers and the like that arise from those light rays, performs the confession of misdeeds and the rest, meditates on emptiness, and then meditates on oneself as the deity. This is common to all yogas. Beyond that, in order to teach the special meditation of the Perfection of Wisdom, the text says "the moon disk" and so on. "Having meditated on oneself as wisdom" means meditating on oneself as the Perfection of Wisdom. By these words it says the following: upon a lotus and moon, upon a lotus with its stem arisen from the seed hūṃ, there is a volume of the Perfection of Wisdom marked with hūṃ. From its complete transformation arises Prajñāpāramitā, white in color, her two hands in the mudrā of teaching the Dharma; from the crooks of her two elbows grow two lotuses with stems, marked with two volumes of the Perfection of Wisdom. One meditates on oneself as having the nature of Tārā in this form. That is what is meant.

Its benefit is stated: "this seed" and so on. "The sprout of wisdom" means the branch of wisdom, because it arises from the seed. The intent is that, just as the sprout arising from the seed of a tree divides into many branches, so wisdom will increase. The rest is easy to understand.

In order to teach another application, the text says "the heart" and so on. Of the four tiers of the wheel, the heart wheel has nine segments. The second tier has twelve. The third tier has sixteen. The fourth is the rim. Here, "nine compartments" means nine together with the central hub. "Middle" is so called because it lies between the heart tier and the third tier. "Outer" is what lies outside that. "Final" is so called because it is the last of all. "In the center" means in the center of the heart tier, that is, on the hub. "One's own mind" is the letter hūṃ, which has the nature of the wisdom ḍākinī. "Placing homage and burning at the end" has already been explained. Put together, it becomes oṃ hūṃ svāhā.

"The eight letters" are the seed letters of the eight yoginīs, namely Ḍākinī, the Fierce Lady, Caṇḍikā, Vetālī, Siṃhinī, Vyāghrī, Jambukī and Ulūkī, which are, in order, suṃ kṣuṃ yuṃ hūṃ smryuṃ hmryaṃ ymryuṃ kṣmryuṃ. "Endowed with nāda and bindu" has already been explained. "Place them in the heart" means that on the four compartments of the directions, beginning with the east, one places suṃ and the rest counterclockwise, and on the compartments of the intermediate directions, beginning with Īśāna, one should place smryuṃ and the rest clockwise. "On the twelve, the vowel seeds" means writing the twelve vowel letters in order, beginning with the east, on the twelve compartments of the middle tier. On "They should be written leaving out the neuters": the neuter letters are ṛ ṝ ḷ ḷ. "Leaving out" means discarding; the meaning is that the remaining ones, a and the rest, are to be taken.

"The letters of the three classes": one takes the fifteen letters of the three classes, namely the class of ka, the class of ca and the class of ṭa. "One remaining letter" means one letter of the remaining classes, namely the letter ta. Alternatively, it is "remaining" because it is what remains beyond the fifteen. Taken together, they make sixteen. "They should be placed in the sixteen positions" means meditating on them in order in the positions of the sixteen outer compartments. "Endowed with nāda and bindu" needs only to be understood. "The letter of killing" is the letter phaṭ. If one asks how it is made to encircle, the text says, "Encircle everything all around as is fitting"; the meaning is that it is set around the rim so as to go all the way round, with no fixed number.

The colors of the wheel are stated in the passage beginning "of the first." The first is the tier of nine compartments, and it is white. The second is the tier of twelve compartments, and it is yellow. The third is the tier of sixteen compartments, and it is red. The fourth is the rim, and it is blue. The oral instructions explain that for a fierce rite the tier of sixteen compartments too should be made blue. These four tiers of the wheel should be understood as a meditation on the body of the wisdom ḍākinī. For that very reason the text says "as before" and so on, which is easy to understand. "Should be meditated upon": the words to be supplied are "the wisdom ḍākinī." "This wheel" is said in order to make the wheel manifest. "By meditating on the thing in equipoise" means through deep equipoise and familiarization. On the passage beginning "Everything blazes all around": "blazing like fire" means that, just as a blazing fire with its light burns wood, so the wheel blazing everywhere burns up and annihilates the firewood of all the hosts of māras, the obstructors and the rest. "Even those wholly covered by afflictions" means even those of the afflicted type. The rest is easy to understand.

In order to teach meditation by another application, the text says "sameness" and so on. The meaning of

"Having generated the wisdom of sameness,
having meditated on oneself as space,"

is that, from the wisdom of sameness purified by emptiness, one generates oneself as the wisdom ḍākinī and then meditates on what follows. That is stated in the passage beginning "light rays": one meditates at the heart on a sun disk possessed of light, and in its center takes up, that is, meditates on, the seed letter. If one asks what that is, the text says "class" and so on. The first letter of the first class is the letter ka. The hook is the letter ī, and "tormenting" means writing it above. "Having joined the repha at the root" means attaching it below as a letter. "Nectar" is the crescent moon. "Bindu" means the drop; these are set above. Taken together, it becomes kriṃ. The rest is easy to understand.

"King" is simply well known. "The king's palace" is the city where the king resides. On "The one with a mind should contemplate": "the one with a mind" is the yogin. "Should contemplate": the words to be supplied are that one makes oneself too red, with the nature of the light rays of the seed kriṃ resting on the sun at one's own heart, and if one contemplates the king and the people of the palace as also flushed and deep red in color, they come under one's power.

Now, in order to teach the bringing of a girl under one's power, the text says "the doubled letter" and so on. "The doubled letter" is the letter kṣa. "The drop" needs only to be understood. On "At the root the sound should be joined": "the root" means below. "The sound" is the short u. Although the long ū is not explained as being the sound, here by the oral instructions the short u is to be taken; just as, for example, the "hook" is the long ī, yet in some contexts the short i is taken. On "nectar's end": "nectar" is water, and its end is fire; here the letter kṣuṃ too has the nature of fire, and so it is spoken of in that way. Alternatively, "nectar" is the syllables svāhā. "At the end" means placed at the end; and it should be understood that oṃ is placed at the beginning of these. Thereby it becomes the mantra oṃ kṣuṃ svāhā. "With one hundred thousand one brings her under power" means this: one endowed with the yoga of the wisdom ḍākinī first performs the approach to this mantra by reciting it one hundred thousand times as before. Then, contemplating as red in color whatever girl is to be won, the practitioner of the yoga of the wisdom ḍākinī, at the time of accomplishing the aim, recites oṃ kṣuṃ so-and-so vaśamanāya svāhā, interspersing her name; then without doubt she will come under one's power.

In order to teach another application of the seed kṛṃ, the text says "girls" and so on. The passage beginning "whoever is contemplated" means this: if one wishes to meditate on some man, one meditates on a red letter kṛṃ at one's own womb and also meditates on the seed letter kṛṃ at the man's heart; then, having performed the approach of that very seed one hundred thousand times, if one recites it interspersed with his name, without doubt he will come under one's power.

In order to teach another application, the text says "the previous characteristic" and so on.

"Having contemplated, as taught before,
emptiness, and the self of all,"

the words to be supplied are: at the end of meditating on emptiness, one meditates on oneself as the wisdom ḍākinī, yellow in color, and then meditates on what follows. If one asks what these are, the text says "the moon disk" and so on. "Nectar" is the letter sa. "The all-knowing mother" is the letter oṃ. "With" means joining, so that it becomes "by joining." "Various light rays radiate" means agitated with light. Here the oral instruction is this: abiding in the yoga of the wisdom ḍākinī, one performs the approach to this mantra a hundred thousand times. At the time of accomplishment, one meditates on a deep red letter soṃ on a yellow moon disk below the navel of the one to be won, and if one also meditates on the letter kṛṃ within her womb, she will flow.

The application for summoning jewels is stated in the passage beginning "color." "Color" means red color.

"Yellow" means yellow in color. "Summon everything with one's own breath" means summoning with the hook of the light rays of one's own breath. Here too the oral instruction is this: one meditates on a yellow letter soṃ upon a moon disk at one's own heart, contemplates the jewels and other things to be summoned as having the form of a red letter soṃ, and summons them with the hook of the light rays of the seed soṃ that issues with one's own breath.

In order to teach another application, the text says "of the practitioner" and so on. "The practitioner" here means the one to be won. "On the body" means at the heart. The meaning of the passage beginning

"Resting in the center of a sun disk,
encircled by the seed of wind,"

is this: one contemplates the one to be won as red in color, and at that person's heart, on a sun disk, one meditates on a red seed kṛṃ, encircled by the letter yuṃ, as the nature of the mind. The meaning of the passage beginning "Resting in the center of a moon disk" is this: upon a moon disk one emanates oneself as the wisdom ḍākinī, red in color, holding a hook in her left hand, while with her right she has, at the tip of her threatening forefinger, the seed kṛṃ resting on a sun. Having caught the one to be won by the throat with the hook in the left hand, one contemplates that by moving the threatening forefinger with the seed kṛṃ one moves the kṛṃ resting at the heart of the one to be won, and recites oṃ kṣuṃ so-and-so's mana ākārṣaya svāhā. That is what is meant.

The passage beginning "the doubled letter" has already been explained. On "Having meditated and recited thus": "meditating" means meditating on the letter kṛ as explained before, at the time of winning the one to be won. "Reciting" refers to the letter kṣuṃ. "And if the finger too is moved" has already been explained. "The mind of the one to be won will be moved" means the mind will be drawn. "It will be accomplished with one hundred thousand" means that it will be accomplished by reciting both seeds, kṛṃ and kṣuṃ, one hundred thousand times each, at the time of the preliminary approach. At the time of winning another, however, one should recite the seed kṣuṃ, and for that there is no fixed number.

Now, with regard to increase, the text speaks of "the wheel" and so on. "Eight-petaled" means eight-spoked, together with a rim. The rim should be encircled with the vowel letters, and for that very reason the text says "the vowel letters" and so on.

"Having taken the third letter
of what is the fifth class"

means taking the letter pa. "Having joined i and the repha" needs only an understanding of the conventions. By the oral instructions the bindu too is to be taken, and taken together it becomes bri. "It should be carefully placed in the center" means writing it in the central compartment. If one asks what, the text says, "this seed of the one possessed of wisdom." "This seed" is the letter briṃ. "Possessed of wisdom" means endowed with wisdom. On "and joined with the seventh of the sounds": the seventh of the sounds is the letter ri. "Joined" means endowed with it, and it is construed with "having taken the third letter of what is the fifth class." By the oral instructions the bindu too is to be taken, and thereby it becomes briṃ. "That same on the petals" means writing that same briṃ on the petals of the directions and intermediate directions as well; the meaning is that one writes briṃ, joined with the seventh of the sounds, on the spokes of the wheel. In order to show this clearly, the text says, "This very one should be placed on the petals." Some say that "the seventh of the sounds" should be taken as the letter e, and that thereby it becomes briṃ.

"It should be meditated upon as golden in color" refers to the color of the wheel. If one asks how one meditates, the text says "as taught before" and so on. "The wise should meditate as taught before" refers to meditating on the meditative absorption of emptiness.

"After that, one should generate oneself
in the form of the goddess Vasudhārā"

means that at the end of emptiness, upon a moon disk, there is an ear of rice arisen from the seed briṃ and marked with the seed briṃ; from its complete transformation arises the goddess Vasudhārā, yellow in color and two-armed, and one meditates on her. In order to teach her particular features, the text says "beautiful in form" and so on: she is adorned with all ornaments, with a jeweled crown marked with Vajrasattva; with her right hand she grants fearlessness to all people, and with her left she holds an ear of rice. One meditates on her as a body raining down wealth and grain, and at her heart one should meditate on the wheel taught earlier.

In order to teach mantra recitation for when one grows weary of meditation, the text says "taught in the center" and so on. The seed taught in the center is the letter briṃ. "Homage" and the rest are easy to understand. Taken together, it becomes oṃ briṃ svāhā. The rest needs only an understanding of the conventions.

In order to teach another application, the text says "oneself" and so on. "Oneself" means in oneself. If one asks what, "the whole arrangement" is the wheel in which all the seeds are set out. "Another particular" means another particular rite; it should be read as "shall be explained."

"Having meditated on oneself, with the earlier characteristic,
as the suchness of emptiness"

means that one first meditates on the suchness of emptiness and then meditates on oneself as the wisdom ḍākinī.

"Resting in the center of a sun disk,
one should meditate on a moon disk"

means meditating, at the heart of the goddess, upon a lotus, on a sun and moon as the base of the wheel. "Resting in the center of a moon disk" is connected with "one should contemplate an eight-petaled wheel." "One meditates on the reality of wisdom and consciousness" means meditating on the letter hūṃ. If one asks where, the text says "in the center," that is, on the hub of the wheel. "Outside" and the rest are easy to understand. The placing on the spokes is stated in the passage beginning "of the directions." "The petals of the directions" are the spokes of the directions. "Nectar" is sa. "The all-producing curve" is oṃ; joining them, it becomes soṃ. This teaches that the seed soṃ is to be written on the four spokes beginning with the east, and they should be arranged counterclockwise.

"And on the four petals of the intermediate directions,
adorned by the five buddhas"

means meditating on four seeds hūṃ clockwise on the four spokes of the intermediate directions. "The complete moon disk" is connected with "should be meditated upon in one's own body"; the meaning is that one meditates on such a wheel resting on a moon at the heart of one's own body.

"Blazing with various light rays,
beautiful with the seven precious things"

means that then, with the light radiating from the wheel, one endows all beings with the seven precious things, the precious elephant and the rest; then one gathers it back and dissolves it into one's own body, and meditates on that again and again until it becomes familiar.

"In order to know whatever mind one wishes,
one should apply its light rays"

will be explained below. If one asks how one meditates on one's own body, the text says:

"Fearless and deathless,
one should meditate on the thing by means of the thing."

That is, in a state free from fear and the like, one meditates, by means of the reality of emptiness, on the reality of the deity together with the wheel, until it becomes familiar. Alternatively, fear and harm are dullness and agitation, and one meditates until one is free of them. "Torpor and" and the rest are easy to understand. By these, the aims of oneself and others are taught.

Now, in order to explain "In order to know whatever mind one wishes" and the rest, the text says "in equipoise" and so on. Having thus meditated on the wheel until it is thoroughly familiar, one emits white light rays from the wheel, pervading the ten directions; one draws in the wisdom of whoever's mind one wishes to know, places it into one's own mind, that is, into the letter hūṃ, and when one looks, one will know that person's mind. "Having bound the mind in equipoise" teaches that, again, by meditating on that same wheel with the knowledge of suchness until it is familiar, one crosses to the far shore of saṃsāra and attains liberation.

In order to teach another application, the text says "the moon disk" and so on. "Emanated by the seed of wisdom" is connected with "the noble Vairocana as principal." Here the meaning is that one generates the wisdom being in an instant merely from the seed of one's own mind. Alternatively, the seed of wisdom is a, and one generates him from that. In order to teach his particular features, the text says "from the lotus seat" and so on.

"Having bound his two hands in the great mudrā,
he binds them at the region of the heart"

means that he crosses his two hands and holds them at his heart in the manner of embracing the goddess. Some say that he is actually together with the goddess. "He emanates clouds of buddhas" means meditating that buddhas of five kinds radiate from one's own body. If one asks what they do, the text says:

"With various inexhaustible emanations
they liberate the realm of beings."

When they pervade all the world realms, they perform the work of liberating beings from saṃsāra. "Inexhaustible" is said because those light rays are skilled at burning up the mass of suffering. If one asks what comes of this, the text says "wisdom" and so on. "Vajra wisdom" is indestructible wisdom. "Liberating beings" is its power; it is to be construed as "one will obtain that."

The extraction of its mantra is stated in the passage beginning "The first vowel, the supreme seed." The first vowel is a; it is the supreme of seeds, the most excellent of syllables. "At the end, the strong dot should be joined" means placing two dots at its side. "Ending with homage and burning" has already been explained. Taken together, it becomes oṃ āḥ svāhā. "This seed" is meant in the sense that the Blessed One is generated from this seed. The intent is that if a yogin in meditative equipoise meditates and recites, it will be accomplished.

On "the Seat of Union" and so on: this text is a chapter because it thoroughly clarifies protection, the wheel of wisdom and the rest.

This concludes the second section, the Seat of Union, of the commentary on the Glorious Catuṣpīṭha called Smṛtinibandha.

Now, in order to teach the section on the descent and the rest, the text says "blessing" and so on. "Blessing" means the descent.
