"The forms of application" means that yogins put into practice and meditate on the application of the descent. If one asks what it is like, the text says, "by which it is shown to beings"; that is, the Dharma is taught to beings by means of the descent that goes before it. Alternatively, darśana means seeing: the application of blessing by which, if one looks at a being, wisdom will descend. "Sequence" means the ritual of the descent; that is the sequence.

The reply is given in the passage beginning "beings." "In order to heal deluded beings" means in order to heal the disease of the afflictions of beings sunk in the mire of delusion. For in this way, from the descent of clear vision faith is born; from faith one meditates on the meditative absorption of the deity; by meditating, ignorance is removed; and thereby the disease of the afflictions is healed. Therefore it is construed with "listen!" If one asks what, the text says, "the suchness that truly liberates from saṃsāra"; that is, the path of the suchness of the unerring descent of clear vision, by which, in the way described before, one will be liberated from saṃsāra. "Having blessed, one teaches the Dharma" is a branch of teaching the Dharma according to the particular vessel.

In order to teach the Dharma, the text says "at the heart" and so on. "Having contemplated a moon at the heart" means meditating on a moon disk at one's own heart. "The seed hūṃ" and the rest are easy to understand. If one asks what comes of this, the text says "light rays" and so on. "In the world realms of the ten directions," that is, "having burned," is connected with "first making it emptiness, with the characteristic as before"; one meditates that the tongues of fire of the hūṃ burn everything and it becomes empty like space. "One should meditate on a fire maṇḍala" means meditating, at the heart of the one to be worked on as well, on a triangular fire maṇḍala marked in its center with raṃ.

If one asks what is done with that, the text says "the places" and so on. "All the places" means, again, all the major and minor limbs of the one to be worked on. "Burned" means burned without remainder, to mere ash.

In the middle of the fire maṇḍala,
One should imagine the five buddhas,

means that, again at the heart of the one to be worked on, in the middle of fire, lotus and sun, one meditates on the seed hūṃ. Speaking of that seed itself, the text says "the staff" and so on, which has already been explained. "That seed" is that very seed hūṃ.

If one asks how these are meditated on, then, to teach first the accomplishment of oneself, the text says "lotus" and so on. "In the nature of the wisdom ḍākinī" is to be connected with "one meditates on oneself." If one asks how, the text says "the emanation of wisdom," meaning generated from hūṃ, the seed of wisdom. Its particular features are spoken of in "two arms" and so on:

On the vajra crown above,
Adorned with the five buddhas,

means that on top of the crown is a five-pronged vajra marked with the five buddhas: in the east Vairocana, in the north Ratnasambhava, in the west Amitābha, in the south Amoghasiddhi, and in the center Akṣobhya.

The khaṭvāṅga and the vessel of yoga,

means that the right hand raises a khaṭvāṅga and the left holds a skull cup.

One should meditate with unmoving breath,

means one meditates until the mind has become stable.

To show the extraction of the mantra for the approach and the rest, the text says "the third group" and so on; these have already been explained. Condensed, it becomes oṃ āḥ svā hā.

Having recited three hundred thousand,

refers to the prior approach.

With the act of the nondual torma,
After that, perform the activities.

Here "after that" means after the approach has been completed. "The activities" are the rites of descent mentioned earlier. "Perform" means one should put the descent into practice. Of these, it teaches the following. After doing the approach, one burns all phenomena with the tongues of flame of the hūṃ visualized at one's own heart, and meditates on emptiness. At the end of that, from a hūṃ resting on lotus and sun, one generates oneself as the wisdom ḍākinī just as described, and at her heart too one meditates on the seed hūṃ resting on a moon. Then one imagines at the heart of the one to be worked on a fire maṇḍala marked with raṃ, which burns the whole body without remainder to mere ash. From the heap of ash one takes it up through the application of hūṃ, and at its heart, on lotus and sun resting on the fire maṇḍala, one imagines hūṃ, the essence of the five buddhas. Then, beginning with giving torma to the ḍākinīs, one recites this very mantra, oṃ āḥ svā hā, over white mustard seed, and when one strikes the one to be worked on with it, the clear appearance will descend.

To show the remaining rite, the text says:

Endowed with the five nectars,

"Endowed with the five nectars" is liquor.

Having recited eight hundred,

means reciting the previous mantra one hundred and eight times; a hundred with eight added is what "eight hundred" means. "Endowed with the rite" means having performed the rites just described. "Giving" means giving it to drink.

In "having descended" and so on, "the vajra song" is "Play, play!" and the rest. "You, vajra" is calling upon Vajradhara. "Play!" means "please engage in play!" It is said twice out of great joy. If one asks in what he is to abide, the text says "all, entirely" and so on: "all" is phenomena, and "entirely" is utterly pure; this is connected with "play!" "You consume" means "make them exhausted and depleted." If one asks what and by what, the text says:

By the application of the weak and the fierce,

"The weak" is evil and "the fierce" is merit: "consume both the merit and the evil of sentient beings!" "By the application" means by your application. If one asks what that is, the text says:

In an instant, liberate me, equal, from saṃsāra.

"Equal" refers to the Blessed One's union in equipoise of method and wisdom. "In an instant" means from one instant, because one enters buddhahood in this very life. "Liberate me from saṃsāra!" is a supplication made by calling upon the Blessed One.

By this song one dances;
One should sound the vajra and bell.

This means that through the song, accompanied by sounding the vajra and bell, the one being worked on will engage in dance and the like such as he has never done before; "dance" is only given as an example. The mantra beginning oṃ tiṣṭha is to be applied and shown after the descent, to restore him to his normal state.

This does all the satisfying,

Here "satisfying" means giving him liquor to drink. "Does all" means that one binds the student with a blindfold of silk, leads him near the maṇḍala, places him there, and puts the accomplishment into practice.

After requesting the descent to depart, he spoke of showing the maṇḍala:

All the yoginīs should be shown,

means that the blindfold is untied and he is made to look at the yoginīs. After that he spoke of the order of teaching the Dharma, in "the inferior" and so on. The intent is that, having learned from the casting of the flower which of the three kinds of vessel he is, superior, middling and so on, one should teach him the Dharma suited to that, that is, the various methods of accomplishment. These are to be learned in detail from the rites of empowerment.

Now, to show descent through mere recitation of mantra as well, the text says oṃ yo ga ā be śa aḥ and so on.

The yogin, well settled in equipoise,
Should first do the approach with this,

means that, settled in equipoise in the yoga of the wisdom ḍākinī, one recites this mantra a hundred thousand times as the approach.

Next the rite of bringing down is taught. In the passage beginning:

Possessed of the nondual torma,

the line

The practitioner should strike with this

means that, having recited over white mustard seed the mantra with which the approach was done earlier, one gives torma to the ḍākinīs and strikes the person to be worked upon. How? The text says:

With the terrifying sound of hūṃ,

that is, having uttered the sound hūṃ.

To show how, after the descent, one requests it to depart again, the text says:

With the mantra stated earlier,
One restores the self just as one wishes.

The mantra stated earlier is oṃ tikṣa yo ga and so on.

Now, to show bringing down by a gaze, the text says oṃ ma na and so on. If one has first done the approach with this mantra, oṃ ma na ghu ru ghu ru ghri ti ci li ci li hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ, a hundred thousand times in the yoga of the wisdom ḍākinī, then afterwards, by merely reciting it once, one will bring down upon whomever one looks at. To show this very point, the text says "the yogin" and so on, which is easy to understand.

Making offerings, whomever he looks upon,
By the sight he brings down, and they dance,

means that, at the time of enjoying the food and drink of the gaṇacakra feast, whomever one looks at is brought down and will dance. Here too the release is done with the earlier mantra.

To show another application, the text gives oṃ be tā lī and so on. Here too the approach should be done in the yoga of the wisdom ḍākinī, as stated earlier. The accomplishment is taught in the passage beginning "ash". These lines show that if one consecrates ash of the well-known kind by reciting that same earlier mantra over it seven times, and scatters it on a corpse or on anything else, then for as long as seven days that thing will not be eaten by wolves or anything else whatsoever.

Another application is taught: oṃ phu ru phu ru man tre ja hūṃ phuḥ phaṭ and so on. Here too the approach should be done as explained before. The accomplishment is taught in the passage beginning "on a red thread". The meaning of these lines is that one recites the same earlier mantra eight hundred times over a red thread, and whoever has it tied around his neck turns into a jackal and roams about. The release is taught again in the words

When parted from the thread,

and so on, which is easy to understand.

To call to mind all of these, namely that, having meditated on the mantra of the wisdom ḍākinī taught earlier as her body and having done the approach, one afterwards brings down by striking with mustard seed that has been recited over, by reciting the mantra, and even by a mere gaze, the text says:

Through the yoga of the stages of blessing,
In meditative equipoise one should meditate and recite.

Now, to teach other rites, the text says "the moon maṇḍala" and so on. Their meaning is this: after meditating on emptiness, upon a lion throne, on top of a moon maṇḍala, one generates Vajraḍākinī from her own seed syllable, just as described; and in front of her one looks upon the person to be worked upon, standing on a wind maṇḍala. This is the one to be driven away.

On the two soles of the feet one should meditate
On the seeds of fire and wind as a seat,

means that, on top of the wind, one places the two feet of the person to be worked upon on two wind seed syllables. So the wind seed syllable itself is the seat, which means a platform.

Endowed with sound and bindu,
Abiding in the midst of the wind seed,
Encircled by wind, set in the heart.

Here "bindu" is the mind: the syllable hūṃ, which is the very nature of the consciousness of the person worked upon. "Sound" is what is uttered in speech, that is, together with recitation. Where is this imagined? The text says, "abiding in the center of the wind seed," that is, it is meditated upon on top of the wind seed syllable. "Encircled by wind" means resting on the wind. The meaning is this: in the heart of the person worked upon there is a wind maṇḍala; on it the wind seed syllable yuṃ; and on that the syllable hūṃ, meditated upon together with recitation in words.

Imagine all of it as blue in color,

means one meditates on everything, from the body of the person worked upon down to the seed syllable hūṃ, as blue.

At the tip of one's own breath
One should fit the arrow of wind,

means that one imagines the person to be worked upon, described above, as being in motion; one imagines the wind seed syllable at the tip of the breath-wind issuing from one's own nostrils, forceful like an arrow, and imagines that by it the person worked upon is driven along.

oṃ yuṃ be raṃ ba yuṃ so-and-so yuṃ phaṭ.

This secret mantra of the yogin,
Recited eight hundred times, will bring success,

refers to the time of accomplishment. At the time of approach, one endowed with the yoga of the wisdom ḍākinī should do the approach a hundred thousand times.

Now, to show driving away also by drawing a yantra, the text says "birch bark" and so on. The words "and so on" include cloth bearing menstrual blood and the like. The poison is simply the well-known kind. The blood is blood from the finger. These are to be mixed with mustard seed, salt and so forth. With these substances, on birch bark or the like, one writes the mantra taught earlier, with the name interspersed. How is it to be written? The text says:

Having taken up the supreme yoga,

that is, abiding in the yoga of Vajraḍākinī taught earlier.

One should write, having set everything in place,

means that one writes after meditating on the person to be worked upon as before.

Wrapped with a red thread
Recited over eight hundred times,
It should be hung at the top of a tree,

means that after that one binds the yantra with a red thread over which the same earlier mantra has been recited eight hundred times, and hangs it at the top of a tree. Where at the top of the tree should it be hung? The text says, "it should be blown by the wind," that is, it is hung on the side where it is moved by the wind. "Meditate" means that, after hanging it, one meditates on the person worked upon, as described earlier, as blown and driven by the wind. What comes of this? The text says:

Wherever the wind inclines,
In that direction he will go,

that is, in whatever direction the yantra is moved by the wind, in that direction the person worked upon will wander.

When the yogin drives away,
It will succeed; of this there is no doubt,

means that for a yogin endowed with the meditative absorption of the deity and with mantra recitation, it will succeed without fail.

Now, to teach another application, that of summoning, the text says "the moon maṇḍala" and so on. The meaning of the first verse is that it teaches that a yogin of Vajraḍākinī should first do the approach by reciting the mantra that comes below a hundred thousand times.

The secret torma of nonduality
Should be placed in the middle of that.

Here "of that" is to be connected with the line that comes below,

Having smeared a maṇḍala with red,

because words depend on what is intended. "Should be placed in the middle" means that, with the torma going first, one writes in the middle the name of the person to be worked upon, interspersed with the mantra; for it says,

And having written the name in the middle.

The meaning of this is as follows. In the middle of an area about the size of a cowhide, smeared with red scent, one sets out the torma, and one writes this: oṃ ba jra ḍā ki nī de śa oṃ ā karṣa ya ā karṣa ya oṃ hrī hūṃ phreṃ phruṃ phruṃ so-and-so ā karṣa ya svā hā.

Fixed with eight pegs of acacia,

means that eight acacia pegs, eight finger-widths in length and shaped like a single-pointed vajra, are driven in at the directions and intermediate directions of the maṇḍala.

Endowed with a torma filled to the brim,

means that this rite is first combined with the torma.

And having recited over a red thread,
One makes a fence by winding it seven times,

means that one then recites that same earlier mantra eight hundred times over a red thread, and with it one encircles and winds the tops of the pegs seven times round, like a fence.

Having drawn him in with the vajra hook,
If one recites this secret mantra,

means that one then meditates on oneself as Vajraḍākinī holding a hook and a noose in her hands; piercing the heart with the hook and binding the neck with the noose, one sees the person worked upon arrive, and recites the mantra.

To show the great power of this application, the text says "a hundred leagues" and so on. The meaning is that through the might of this application even someone dwelling beyond a hundred leagues is summoned and will come. To show the summing up of the text, it says "maṇḍala" and so on, which is easy to understand.

Now, to teach the application of binding, the text says "the sun maṇḍala" and so on. This first verse teaches that, preceded by meditation on emptiness, one meditates on oneself as the ḍākinī, the Fierce Lady, arisen from her own seed syllable upon a sun maṇḍala on a lion throne, and then performs the rites that come below. This is stated in the passage beginning:

Having caught a female fish.

The meaning of these lines is this. One catches a fish and, preceded by giving torma to the ḍākinīs, writes on a base of birch bark or the like the mantra oṃ phruṃ phruṃ so-and-so's mu kha bandha bandha hri hri hri phruṃ phaṭ. One puts the mantra into the fish's mouth, presses the fish's head down with the left foot, and recites that same mantra eight hundred times. By this the mouth will be bound.

All activities will be bound,

means that when one has written the interspersed mantra one time, all things such as the movements of the mind will be bound.

Another application of binding is taught in the passage beginning:

Everything is to be done in water.

Here "another rite" refers back to the earlier application of this same mantra.

Having properly applied this mantra,
One should roll it up in dough,

means that one writes the mantra as before on birch bark or the like, rolls it up in a dough of rice flour or the like, and makes pills. What is to be done with them? The text says:

Where the fish live,
One should put them in the mouths of the fish.

"Where" means that one watches for a lake or the like where the fish eat dough and such, and on seeing such a place one gives the pills to the fish as food. How many are given, and how? The text says:

Having made eight hundred pills,
If one recites once for each and has them eaten,

that is, one recites the mantra once over each of the eight hundred pills, one by one, and if one keeps giving until the fish have eaten up all the pills, all activities will be bound, just according to how the name was interspersed.

The binding of the bhaga is taught in the passage beginning "of wind". The meaning of the first verse is that, in the manner described earlier, one meditates on oneself as the goddess Caṇḍikā dwelling on a wind maṇḍala and performs the rites that come below. This is stated in the passage beginning:

The rites of the nondual torma.

Here too "of that" is to be connected with the words below,

Having made the shape of a lotus with cow dung,

that is, one makes the shape of a source of phenomena from cow dung and does the procedure in its center. What is to be done? To show that very procedure, the text says:

The name and mantra should be placed.

One writes on birch bark or the like the mantra oṃ staṃ bha ya staṃ bha ya bha ga mu draṃ hūṃ phruṃ phruṃ so-and-so phaṭ and places it inside the shape of the source of phenomena one has made.

This secret mantra and the name,
Having pressed them down with the left foot,
One should recite eight hundred times,

means that the practitioner presses down with his left foot that mantra interspersed with the name, placed inside the source of phenomena made of cow dung, and recites that same earlier mantra eight hundred times. By this, one will certainly be bound. For that reason the text says "wife" and so on, which is easy to understand.

To show what is to be done if one wishes it pacified, the text says "at the end" and so on: with a peaceful mind, by dipping the mantra garland in water and milk, or by washing it with water and milk, it will also be pacified.

Another application is taught in the passage beginning "of wind". The meaning of the first two verses is that one generates oneself as Vajraḍākinī seated on a seat of wind and of a preta, endowed with the features just as described, and performs the rites that come below. This is stated in the passage beginning "nondual". Here too "there" is connected with

Having made it in the shape of a lotus,

made from a preparation of rice flour or the like.

What is to be done there? The text says:

Having joined the name with the mantra,
One should fill it with the hair of a dog.

That is, having made the shape of a source of phenomena as before from a preparation of rice flour or the like, one again writes as before oṃ kro dhe śva re phuṃ phroṃ so-and-so's bha ga duṣṭa ya phaṭ, places it there, and fills it with dog hair. Pressing it down with the heel as before and reciting eight hundred times, the bhaga will rot and be afflicted with stabbing pains. For all the rites, at the time of approach one should recite the mantra a hundred thousand times, and at the time of accomplishment one should recite it eight hundred times together with the interspersing.

The vajra should be placed in the secret place,

means that the nondual mantra garland is to be placed inside the bhaga. The release is taught:

If it is taken out, she will be freed from that,

that is, the mantra together with the dog hair is taken out.

Another application, that of melting the channels, is taught in the passage beginning "the moon maṇḍala".

In the center of a moon maṇḍala
At the navel, meditate on the syllable of nectar,

means that at the navel of the person to be worked upon one meditates on the nectar syllable saṃ upon a moon maṇḍala. What does this do? The text says:

Drawing it out from all the channels,
One should make the nectar water drip.

"Nectar water" is the seminal fluid. "Drawing out" means that, urging it forth from the channels with the light rays of the three syllables, one brings it down from every place. What is done after that? The text says "the bandūka flower" and so on: within the channel called Khāgamukha, which lies in the center of the bhaga and is open like a bandūka flower, one meditates on the syllable of the hook, that is, the syllable kriṃ. To explain this, the text says:

One should meditate in secret, and in secret.

Here "secret" is the Khāgamukha channel, and "secret" again is the syllable of the hook. What further is done after that? The text says "wrathful" and so on. "The wrathful finger" is the index finger. The nectar syllable is suṃ. "Generating" means meditating on it as generated. To show these earlier points in summary, the text says:

By contemplating the three applications,

and so on. "The three applications" means setting out the syllable suṃ at the places of the body, at the Khāgamukha channel, and at the tips of the index fingers. "Contemplating" means meditating. What comes of this? The text says:

From the places of her body
One should make the nectar water drip.

"Nectar water" is the seminal fluid. "From the places of the body" means from all the major and minor limbs. After "should make drip," the words "one meditates" are to be supplied. "A bhaga very hard to tame" means an exceedingly unruly woman. "Examining" means fondling and subduing.

The application of a drug that causes madness is taught in the passage beginning "radiant and". "Radiant" is the medicine known as Radiant. "Made supreme" means olma sé. "The five parts of the maddening plant" are the leaves, flowers, fruit, roots and bark of datura. "Great guggulu" is human brain. "Great black butter" is what comes from human marrow. "Nectar water" is liquor: one pours equal portions of the medicines, radiant and the rest, into liquor and gives it to drink. For this reason the text says:

Have him taste it with the faculty of the tongue.

This is to be done to a person who harms one's guru, the buddhas and so on, and not otherwise.

Eating butter, he will be freed,

means that by giving him melted butter to drink he will be released from it.

The application of an eye ointment is taught in the passage beginning "the preta's cloth".

From a preta's cloth that has not fallen,
One should make the wick of a lamp,

means that one takes the cloth of a corpse that has not yet fallen from the shoulder to the ground, and makes a wick from it. "Lord of nāgas" means lead. "Marrow butter" is what comes from human marrow. "The place of torma" means in a charnel ground.

With the heart mantra of the ḍākinī,

refers to the mantra of the wisdom ḍākinī.

The smoke should be brought down,

means that smoke is produced. The meaning taught here is as follows. A yogin of the wisdom ḍākinī, preceded by a torma with meat, fish and so on, in a charnel ground, at a crossroads, at the foot of a solitary tree or the like, places on three skull cups a skull cup resting on the vessel, butter, wick and so on mentioned before, recites the mantra of the wisdom ḍākinī eight hundred times and produces the smoke. Then again, in a charnel ground or the like, preceded by a torma, if one smears that soot on the eyes, one will see all the ḍākinīs.

The application of causing division is taught in the passage beginning "the vessel of leftovers". "Charnel ground charcoal" is simply as understood. "Pungent substances" are mustard seed, poison, blood and so on.

Filled with wrath, one should rub them,

means that with a mind full of wrath one rubs the two bowls against each other. The meaning of these lines is as follows. A yogin of the wisdom ḍākinī, preceded by torma, takes two bowls that have been used for food, and on them, with the ink and pen described, draws the figure of the person to be worked upon, and in his heart writes the mantra garland oṃ ki ri ki ri and so on. Again, preceded by torma, one rubs the bowls together with wrath and recites the mantra oṃ ka li barta and so on one hundred and eight times. The rest is easy to understand.

The rite of pacification is taught in the passage beginning "nondual". This shows that if a yogin of the wisdom ḍākinī, preceded by torma and with a peaceful mind, meditates that goddesses arising from his own body and holding vases of nectar confer empowerment, there will be pacification.

In the passage beginning "the Seat of Union," the word "chapter" is used because this text clearly sets forth everything from the bringing down of the clear vision up to the arising of strife; therefore it is a chapter.

From the commentary on the Glorious Catuṣpīṭha called Smṛtinibandha, this is the third, the analysis of the Seat of Union.

Now, in order to teach the union of wisdom and method through the purity of vajra and bell, the text says "nondual" and so on. "Nondual union" means the union of method and wisdom without duality. "Essence" means the supreme, and it is connected with "I wish to hear".

It is expressed by the name "nondual."

Here "name" is what serves as a conventional designation, and "expressed" means it is to be expressed by that.

How is the union to be undertaken?

means how it is to be put into practice. Thus the meaning is: How is the essence of nondual union expressed by the name "nondual"? What is its essential nature like? And how is it to be put into practice?

The answer is given in the passage beginning "nondual". "Nondual union" has already been explained. "That very" means its intrinsic nature, and is connected with "listen!" "Correctly" means without distortion. That very thing is stated:

The suchness of holding
The vajra, bell and mudrā each.

Here suchness is the indivisibility of the conventional and the ultimate, the utterly pure sound of the perfection of wisdom, without division; one should hold a vajra and bell of that kind. "Holding" means meditating. So the meaning is that one should meditate on the suchness of the vajra and bell, in which the utterly pure sound of the perfection of wisdom and the conventional truth are indivisible.

"Mudrā" is wisdom, the purity of the bell, and it is of two kinds, outer and inner wisdom. "Each" refers to method, which likewise has two divisions, outer and inner. Alternatively, the mudrā is nondual union, and that is threefold: outwardly, the union of a man and a woman, the action seal; inwardly, the union of the nature of reality and things; and as wisdom, the union of compassion and emptiness. "That very" is suchness, and "holding each" means holding correctly. Alternatively, vajra and bell are outer method and wisdom. These themselves are the mudrā, because each seals the other with joy. For that very reason the bell is marked at its top with a vajra. Alternatively, the mudrā is the bliss arising from the union of those two, the coemergent essential nature.

To teach all of these, the text says "that very" and so on. "Self" means one's own body, because it is relative to another, the wisdom partner. "That very thing is the vajra, because it is joined inseparably with wisdom": this means that method too is to be called by the word "vajra," because it is united with wisdom inseparably; it is meant as sharing the qualities of a vajra. Alternatively, there is the sense "many"; and ja means beautiful: because this method makes many wisdoms beautiful, it is vajra.

In the second sense, "self" is conventional truth. When that very thing is analyzed, it culminates in ultimate truth and so is vajra: it is called vajra because it has the character of being indestructible, and it is suchness. Likewise, ultimate truth too does not exist apart from conventional truth. They are to be held as indivisible through suchness. For that very reason the text says "of the Dharma" and so on. Here "dharmas" are the aggregates and the rest, because they bear their own characteristics such as impermanence. "Bell" is the nature of reality, pure by its intrinsic nature, because by it everything is accomplished. Alternatively, it is both Dharma and a bell, and so it is the bell of Dharma, which is the nature of reality. That very thing is the goddess, because through this suchness all phenomena are made clear to yogins.

The union of embrace is the mudrā,

Here "embrace" means the union of one's own body with the action seal, and the union of ultimate truth with conventional truth. That itself is the mudrā, because they are entwined with one another inseparably. These words teach that they are utterly indivisible. For that very reason the text says:

Method dissolves into that.

"Into that" means into wisdom. One's own body dissolves into the wisdom of the action seal in the manner of an embrace; the conventional mind, or self and other, dissolves indivisibly into the mind of coemergent wisdom; compassion dissolves into emptiness, and the conventional into the ultimate. Therefore one should not hold the vajra alone, apart from the bell; one should hold the mudrā of suchness, in which the vajra dissolves into the bell. That is the meaning.

Now this nonduality of the conventional and ultimate truths is, here, the meditative absorption of the deity's body purified by emptiness. To show what is to be done first in that state, the text says "first, for now" and so on. "First" means at the beginning, at the beginning of the yoga, and it is connected with

Having bound the vajra palms firmly together.

"For now" has the sense of sequence: the purity of the fence, the net and so on is to be explained, but setting those aside, the three purities to be recited are what will be explained. That is the meaning. Or else, the meaning is that among the purities of practitioners, the three purities are recited. "Purities" means the meanings. "Reciting" means uttering their mantras: after meditating on emptiness, one should recite oṃ sva bhā va and so on. After meditating on the vajra arisen from the seed syllable, one should recite oṃ ba jra and so on. After generating oneself in the form of the deity, one should recite oṃ yo ga and so on.

Having thus taught the purity of the yoga tantra, in order to teach the purity of the fence and the rest, the text says "of apprehended and apprehender" and so on. From the indivisibility of the conventional, characterized as mantra, deity and the like, and the ultimate, which is emptiness, there is an emanation into two by force of need. Teaching liberation from form and the rest is giving, namely the giving of Dharma. Alternatively, releasing the grasping at things into emptiness is the giving of Dharma; its purity is the fence.

Everything should be given away like grass.

Here "everything" means wealth and the finest wealth, limbs and the finest limbs, that is, material things. "Grass" is given as an example, and spittle, mucus and the like are to be included. How is this done? The text says:

With a mind endowed with yoga.

"Yoga" here is the union in equality in which the three spheres, giver, receiver and gift, are not apprehended; one who has such a mind is so called. These, together with the other, the giving of fearlessness, make the three gifts of Dharma, material things and fearlessness. The meaning is that the three gifts, purified, are supreme.

The purity of the net is taught in the passage beginning "to all sentient beings". The giving of fearlessness is protecting from fears, and this too is to be joined with non-apprehension as stated before. Because it engages in virtuous dharmas, this teaches the discipline of gathering virtuous dharmas; it can also be regarded as a part of giving. To teach the discipline of restraint, which consists in turning away from wrongdoing, the text says:

Do not put beings beneath oneself, and so on.

By the words "and so on," other kinds of turning away from nonvirtue are to be included. To teach the discipline of working for the benefit of others, the text says:

Do not obstruct the welfare of beings,

which is easy to understand. What results from these? The text says:

Above is the net of discipline.

The meaning is that one covers from above with the net that is the purity of the three disciplines.

To teach the purity of the pegs, the text says:

Stopping harm to others, and so on.

"Harm to others" means doing harm to others, and "stopping" means turning it back. This teaches the patience of accepting suffering. Alternatively, it is to be read as "harm by others," teaching the patience that thinks nothing of harm done by others; this is the second, by way of the sense of the third. By the words "and so on," patience toward the profound nature of reality and the rest are to be included. Thus the three kinds of patience are taught. What results from this? The text says:

The pegs of patience are driven in below.

That is, suffering and the causes of suffering are pressed down below by patience; the meaning is that one drives in below the pegs that are the purity of patience.

What do these three accomplish? The text says:

With pegs, fence and net,

and so on. "Hostile māras": they are hostile because they corrupt and transform the body and so on; they are the māras of the afflictions, of the aggregates, of the Lord of Death, and of the sons of the gods. These very ones are enemies, because they destroy happiness. "Driving away" means repelling; it is for that purpose.

But are not the net and the rest emanated by one's own mind? How then can they repel? The text says:

Māras will not act as māras.

"Māras" are those just mentioned. "Will not act as māras" means they will not do harm. The intention here is this: when the net and the rest are made by the mind, the māras too are made by the mind, and so the māras themselves do not exist apart. How could they do harm? Harming depends on them, and if the māras do not exist, how could they harm? That is the thought.

But if one thinks, "It is utterly unacceptable to say that māras, and the suffering they cause, do not exist even while they are being experienced," the text says:

They are restrained by one's own mind.

That is, since at that time they are experienced, it is to be established that everything is one's own mind; one should understand inwardly that external objects are untenable, because they are undermined by reasoning. What results from that? The text says "they are restrained": just as māras and the like are emanated by the mind and exist as experienced, so the binding by the net and the rest and the driving away of enemies will also be well accomplished as experienced. To show this very point, the text says:

When one's own mind is tamed,
How could māras do harm?

"The mind tamed" means knowing one's own intrinsic nature. By knowing the intrinsic nature the afflictions are tamed, and when one is free of the noose of the afflictions the other māras turn back as well, because the afflictions are their root. These lines show that when the intrinsic nature is known, the māras do not exist, just as the net and the rest do not exist; and that just as māras exist in experience, so the net and the rest exist, and therefore the perfect protection is well established. As it is said:

Just as water poured into water
Becomes of a single taste,
Since mind itself is the source of faults and virtues,
Where is there anything opposed, or its antidote?

Now, to teach the purity of the square maṇḍala smeared with scent at the place of meditative concentration, the text says "equality" and so on. "Mind of equality" means that the mind and mental factors of the three realms are by their intrinsic nature luminous and are equality itself. The meaning is that, with the purity of the equality of mind, one smears the square maṇḍala of scent.

The purity of strewing flowers is taught in the passage beginning "on the body". When someone's body is cut with an adze and someone else's is smeared with sandalwood, the mind remains even toward both, without attachment or aversion; that is the patience in which the mind abides in equality. With that purity of patience, the yogin strews flowers.

The purity of incense is taught in the passage beginning "equality". "Pure, pure by nature" refers to sva bhā va śuddhaḥ sarba dharmāḥ sva bha va śuddho 'haṃ. By this one perfumes with the incense of purity in equality.

The purity of the lamp is taught in the passage beginning "wisdom". "The lamp of wisdom" means the lamp of the purity of wisdom, and "one should light it" is to be supplied. The passage beginning "the three realms" means that the lamp of wisdom, having destroyed the darkness of the three realms, illuminates the path of suchness just as one wishes; the intention is that one should be joined with that purity.

By these it is taught that when one begins meditative concentration, one should first purify oneself with these purities; I have already explained this clearly in my means of accomplishment (sādhana).

To show the summary of the text, the passage beginning "with the mind" is stated. The meaning is that by these, the giving of fearlessness and the rest, one should establish all sentient beings in happiness, and at the time of offering and at the time of the torma one should fully apply these purities.

The excellence of the place where one begins meditative concentration is taught in the passage beginning "pleasing"; it is easy to understand. By these the activities common to every undertaking of meditative concentration are taught.

Now, in order to generate the innate wisdom whose nature is nondual yoga, the union of skillful means and wisdom is taught, in the passage beginning "oneself". "Oneself" is the practitioner's own body. "Having generated the vajra" means one should generate oneself as Vajrasattva arisen from a vajra. That is, seated in the place taught before, one binds the vajra palms together at the heart, recites the three purities, and meditates on emptiness; then, in the city on the summit of Mount Meru, upon a seat of a lion and a moon, one should generate oneself by means of a vajra arisen from hūṃ.

On "Emanate wisdom, called the bell": wisdom is the maiden. "Emanate it, called the bell" means one meditates on the bell arisen from a as the Perfection of Wisdom.

On "Having set a cubit between": "between" means between oneself and wisdom; one should meditate, knowing that she is in front of oneself, separated by about a cubit. "One should remain gazing at equality" means that oneself and wisdom, the two, remain directly opposite each other, each looking at the other's face.

To explain this at length, "Having meditated on hūṃ at the heart" is stated. "Having meditated on hūṃ at the heart" means that after making offerings, going for refuge to the Three Jewels, and so forth, one meditates on the syllable hūṃ upon a moon maṇḍala at the heart.

If one asks what one should do after that, "Through awareness all is empty" is stated: by the mere touch of the light of the flames of hūṃ, one meditates on the three realms without remainder as emptiness. To show this, "the three realms" is stated; it is easy to understand. "As before" means recalling the way, taught earlier, in which the aggregates and the rest are emptiness.

To teach the celestial mansion, the passage beginning "equality" is stated. The passage beginning "palace" refers to the celestial mansion. "As vast as one wishes" means of whatever extent one wishes.

To teach the characteristics of the vajra arisen from the hūṃ meditated on earlier upon the moon maṇḍala, the passage beginning "the deities" is stated. Its meaning is this: one meditates on it as a vajra blessed by the twenty-eight deities, distinguished in sequence as taught earlier in the section on ash.

The passage beginning "blazing with various light rays" teaches the special features of hūṃ, the seed of the vajra. By this too it is taught that various light rays radiate from the drop of the seed hūṃ resting upon the lion and the moon, touch the three realms, are gathered back into it, and one should meditate on it as a vajra.

"One should emanate one's own body in the nature of Vajrasattva" means that from the complete transformation of the vajra, marked at its hub with the seed hūṃ and blessed by the twenty-eight deities, one generates oneself as Vajrasattva. The body color and so forth will be explained below.

The rite of the eight limbs is taught: "Having arranged them by setting out the syllables, the wise one, having performed the rite, hūṃ." Here the wise one is one skilled in distinguishing the places and the mantras; one meditates on a white syllable hūṃ at the palate, the two eyes, the throat, the two shoulders, the navel, and the secret place.

The passage beginning "of the five buddhas" teaches the color of the seed hūṃ, not the body of Vajrasattva, because his color appears below as differing from this. "Adorned with the five buddhas" is the characteristic of the syllable hūṃ. Alternatively, it means that the head of Vajrasattva is adorned with the five families.

"Together with the great mudrā at the heart" means that the two hands holding the vajra and bell are made to embrace at the heart.

On "One should meditate on the supreme state": the supreme is the perfect fulfillment of one's own aims and those of others. The state is the secret place, which is its support. For this very reason the passage beginning "From the blazing of various light rays" is stated; it is easy to understand.

"Having meditated in the meditation on the entity" means once the meditation has become thoroughly familiar.

To teach mantra recitation, "One should fill it with the sound of hūṃ" is stated: one recites aloud the mantra hūṃ interspersed with oṃ svāhā. If one asks how many times one should recite it, "with a mind as one oneself wishes" is stated, meaning as much as one is able. To show this, "One should recite the seed of wisdom" is stated.

To teach the color, "Blazing intensely like refined gold, attached to the aim of all sentient beings" is stated. "All sentient beings" here is the wisdom consort; the aim is bliss; attachment is clinging to it. Alternatively, it is the attachment, through great compassion, to removing what all sentient beings do not want and accomplishing what they want. Because of this, although the Blessed One, the utterly pure sphere of reality, is white like a conch or the moon, he appears yellow like refined gold, since he is mingled with attachment.

Now the generation of wisdom is taught, in the passage beginning "then". "Then the characteristic of emptiness, whose nature is the characteristic of before" is to be connected with "having meditated": having meditated on the whole continuum of oneself and others as emptiness in the way taught before, one should generate the goddess.

This is taught in the passage beginning "the moon maṇḍala": "Knowing all the characteristics of before, one should meditate on the nature of the bell through the twenty-three deities." This means one meditates upon the moon maṇḍala on a bell arisen from the seed āḥ, marked with the seed āḥ and blessed by the twenty-three deities, and one should generate it as the goddess.

The special features of the goddess are taught in the passage beginning "various light rays". "A body of slightly mingled color" means that, through the mingling of white and red, she appears yellow, like the Blessed One. "Fully adorned by the five buddhas" means her head is adorned with the five buddhas. "The mantras of eight syllables should be set on the eight limbs" teaches the armor; it is easy to understand. "The two hands are the great mudrā; one should meditate on the supreme state" should be regarded as explained in the section on the Blessed One.

On "One should recite her seed, arisen from āli and kāli": "her seed" is the syllable ā, and since it is of the nature of skillful means and wisdom, it is "arisen from āli and kāli", the vowels and consonants. Alternatively, āli is ā and kāli is ra, and "arising" means extraction: it recalls the extraction of the mantra taught earlier. "One should recite" means, as taught earlier, one recites oṃ āḥ svāhā as much as one is able.

"Through the power of a mind of attachment, with the radiance of refined gold" teaches the color together with its reason, which has already been explained above.

"To liberate sentient beings they embrace completely; embracing the breasts, the two faces kiss" teaches the pure activity of both skillful means and wisdom in liberating sentient beings.

The passage beginning "the tip of the vajra" further explains what was taught in the previous section: "On the tip of the wrathful one's finger one should imagine the syllable of nectar."

If one asks what one should do after that, "To the left side of the goddess, at the tip of what is shown, one should take hold" is stated. "What is shown to the left side" is the channel of Khāgamukha. "The tip" is the opening together with the syllable of the hook. "Taking hold" is drawing out; for that very reason "by moving with this finger" is stated. "The water of nectar" is the seminal fluid. The rest is merely to be understood from the conventional signs.

Now, to teach the stirring of that maiden's passion, "Having let go of the union of the two" means fully abandoning the state of the meditative union of skillful means and wisdom. "Move toward the east" means performing the karaṇa posture toward the east. "Having looked toward the east" teaches that the wisdom consort's face is turned east: in order to draw out and purify the sentient beings dwelling in the east, the wisdom consort's face is turned east, and one performs the karaṇa toward the east.

The karaṇa is taught in the passage beginning "the playful stance". "The playful stance" means the goddess's left foot is slightly bent. On "Pressing with the stance of the vajra foot": "vajra" is Vajrasattva; Vajrasattva's right foot presses the sole of the goddess's left foot. Here "the goddess" is to be understood as the sole of the goddess's left foot.

On "Resting on the left thigh of the bell": "the left thigh" is to be extended to the word "vajra"; it means Vajrasattva's left thigh is placed upon the goddess's right thigh. "Seizing the breasts with both hands" means the two breasts of the wisdom consort are held by Vajrasattva's two hands. "With a face amorous and laughing" means performing amorous gestures together with laughter. On "One should press the lower one with the sharp ones": "the lower one" is the lower lip; "the sharp ones" are the teeth; it means pressing the lip with the teeth. These are the actions of Vajrasattva.

What the wisdom consort does is taught. On "The bell joins her two hands at the secret place": it comes from the oral instructions that "the secret place" refers to the neck; it means the wisdom consort embraces Vajrasattva's neck with her two hands.

On "Likewise one should recite the nondual seeds": the seeds are āḥ, the heart of wisdom, and hūṃ, the heart of skillful means; it means reciting these two. "Nondual" is the heart of the nonduality of skillful means and wisdom, and "one should meditate" is to be supplied. "Move" means the karaṇa. If one asks why, the passage beginning "the east" is stated. Thus "move" teaches the action of body, "reciting the seeds" teaches the action of speech, and "meditating on the nondual" teaches the action of mind. The intention is that by applying the three activities equally in this way, one should liberate the sentient beings of the east.

Now, in order to liberate the sentient beings dwelling in the south, the passage beginning "the goddess" is taught. "One should turn the face toward the south" teaches that the wisdom consort's face is turned south. In this yoginī tantra, because wisdom is the principal and because she is the object of joy, the facing in each direction should be understood as that of the wisdom consort.

"Having moved the goddess's left" means the goddess's left foot is stretched out and the right is slightly bent. "And having moved the vajra too" means Vajrasattva's right foot is stretched out and the left slightly bent. "Likewise the two soles are pressed" means that the right and left of skillful means and wisdom each press the other's soles.

"With the two hands of the bell one should clasp the waist and press" means that with her two hands the wisdom consort clasps both flanks of skillful means and presses them. "The vajra should press the right breast and likewise the secret place" means that Vajrasattva with his left hand takes hold of the secret place, that is, the wisdom consort's neck, and with his right hand holds her breast. "The bodies of vajra and bell should be joined again and again" means that wisdom and skillful means kiss again and again in order to arouse passion in each other. "By reciting the two seeds, the sentient beings of the south are liberated" means that by the karaṇa together with the meditation, as stated before, and by the recitation, one should liberate the sentient beings dwelling in the south.

To teach the karaṇa of the west, the passage beginning "the west" is stated. "The goddess should look toward the west" is easy to understand. "The west" is to be connected with "in order to liberate the sentient beings of the west". "The even stance" means the goddess's two feet are placed about a cubit apart. On "With one side of the vajra one should press the right thigh": "the vajra" is Vajrasattva.

"One side" means remaining on one side. "The right thigh" is the goddess's right thigh, which is pressed by the left hand of skillful means. "The two embrace from behind the neck" means that the right hand of skillful means, resting on the knee, embraces the wisdom consort at the neck, while the two hands of the wisdom consort embrace skillful means at the waist. "Having curled the tongue of the bell, one should touch the tongue of the vajra" means that at the time of kissing, the wisdom consort's tongue touches and stirs the tongue of skillful means.

On "Likewise, having joined, both recite": "likewise" is to be carried over to the meditation; "joined" is the karaṇa; "both recite" is the mantra recitation. These are the three kinds of action.

Now, to teach the karaṇa whose purpose is to liberate the sentient beings dwelling in the north, the passage beginning "the vajra" is stated. "The vajra" is Vajrasattva. "In the middle" is connected with both the preceding and the following phrase: Vajrasattva sits evenly in the middle of the wisdom consort's two arms, and with his two hands clasps the wisdom consort at the middle of her waist. "With both hands of the bell, too, one should pierce near the ears" means that with her two hands the wisdom consort sets her nails at the roots of both ears of skillful means; by this the seminal fluid is held back somewhat.

"Having joined the lips of both, one should drink her nectar" means that at the time of kissing, both join their lips to each other, and by sucking each other drink the nectar from above; or else, by joining their lips to each other, the nectar, that is, the awakening mind, is made to descend, and one tastes the flavor of its bliss.

To teach the mantra recitation as before, the passage beginning "by the yogin" is stated. "One should play with vajra and bell" teaches the meditation of skillful means and wisdom and the karaṇa.

To teach the dedication by which whatever virtue arises from the three kinds of action in this way establishes all sentient beings in bliss, the passage beginning "all sentient beings" is stated. "By whatever path, may beings achieve buddhahood" means: may they attain buddhahood by going on the path of the suchness of mind, which is endowed with bliss and free from harm. It continues up to: "O sentient beings, practice the path of the suchness of mind by which you will achieve buddhahood!"

To teach that at the time of the meditative union of skillful means and wisdom one performs the deity yoga in the manner of an instant, the passage beginning "yoga in the manner of an instant" is stated. "By joining the bell as a deity" means that joining oneself as the deity Vajrasattva and joining the wisdom consort as the bell goddess are generated by yoga in the manner of an instant; and knowing them, in the manner of an instant, as endowed with mantra and mudrā, the yogin skilled in the rite of skillful means should meditate and contemplate.

To teach the setting of the armor, which has the nature of the yoginīs, on both one's own body and the body of the wisdom consort, the passage beginning "the syllables" is stated. "In all the places" means in the places beginning with the palate. "That is the deity itself" is because they are of the nature of the eight goddesses. "One's own body" means on one's own bodies; "the goddess" means on the goddess's body. "Contemplating" means meditating on the suchness of mind by way of setting the armor. "Again one should do that very thing" means one should recall that armor again and again. Alternatively, it means that those who are free of passion should arouse passion again and again after donning the armor.

To show this, the passage beginning "by oneself" is stated. "By oneself" means by the wisdom consort. "Passionate attachment" is embracing and so forth. "Then" means after that. "The vajra" means by Vajrasattva. "Then one should contemplate oneself as the vajra, together with the karaṇa" teaches the nondual meditation together with the karaṇa. For that very reason the passage beginning "the vajra" is stated; it is easy to understand.

Alternatively, these teach the reversed activity. "Then" means at the end of the stage of the karaṇa. "The vajra" is Vajrasattva. "By oneself" means by himself: Vajrasattva himself performs the embracing and so forth. If one asks when, "the mind of passionate attachment" is stated. On citta na: citta is mind, and na is negation, that is, without a mind of attachment. This teaches the following: when, at the end of the karaṇa, the wisdom consort is without a mind of passionate attachment, then he himself performs the embracing. "Then" means afterward. "Oneself as the vajra" is Vajrasattva. On "One should contemplate with the kara": the kara is easy to understand; contemplating is thinking; and the action is the action in accord with that thinking. At that time, reversing, he remains beneath and takes up acting as the woman. To show this, the passage beginning "of the vajra" is stated; the meaning is that he abides merely in the pride of being Vajrasattva, not in his activity.

Having thus taught the four karaṇas, now, to teach the nature of the channel of Khāgamukha, the passage beginning "born from water" is stated. "The flower born from water" is the lotus, the source of phenomena (dharmodaya). If one asks what it does, "from the characteristic within the secret" is stated: the secret characteristic within the sign of that source of phenomena is the very channel of Khāgamukha. If one asks what it is like, "root, shoot and flower, like a bandūka in full bloom" is stated: just as a root is the source of shoot and flower, so, since it has become the place of the shoots of all the channels and of the flower together with the fruit of bliss, it is the root, namely Khāgamukha. Alternatively, it is the root because it has become the place of the shoot of name and form, the flower of the six sense sources, and so forth.

Now, in order to clarify the places of the armor taught earlier by teaching them, the passage beginning "on the two thongka" is stated. On "the seeds of the two thongka": "the two thongka" are the two shoulders.

"The seeds" is a plural expression. Because the seeds to be set out are many, it teaches that the places where the stacked syllables are to be set should also be taken as the throat, the heart, and as far as the root of the navel; it does not use the plural merely for the stacked syllables. Here, on the two shoulders is hmryuṃ; at the throat, smryuṃ; at the heart, ymruṃ; at the navel, kṣmaṃ.

In the passage beginning "earth and": "earth" is the syllable hūṃ, and it is placed on the two eyes. "Wind" is the syllable yuṃ, which is placed on the ears and the nose. "Below" is the tongue, because it lies below; "having the nature of water" means one sets the syllable suṃ there. "The shoot" means that which is like a shoot, the nipple of the palate, which lies above. "Her seed" is the syllable kṣuṃ.

Now, to teach that the very seed by which the goddess is generated is to be set within the lotus, the passage beginning "the group" is stated. Its meaning is that the form of the goddess is generated from the syllable a; it recalls what was taught earlier.

To teach what is to be done after having recalled this and made the connection, "One should dissolve it into the earth" is stated. "From that" refers to the source of phenomena, because it has the nature of the twelve in the place of great bliss. "Dissolving" means setting it there.

If one asks what one should do after that, the passage beginning "the fire of time" is stated. Here "the fire of time" is the right, and "the fire of the pretas" is the left: first skillful means embraces the wisdom consort with the right arm, and after that embraces her with the left. To teach the wisdom consort embracing in the reverse order, the passage beginning "the pretas" is stated: the wisdom consort first embraces skillful means with her left arm, and after that with her right. Thus these teach that for skillful means the right is principal, and for the wisdom consort the left is principal.

"Joining with both hands" is churning; it means embracing with both arms in this way. If one asks in what manner, the passage beginning "the little finger" is stated. "Having pressed the little finger with the thumb" means that the two thumbs of the two hands press the third joints of the two little fingers. "The three are stretched out as the vajra tip" means the remaining three fingers are raised. "Vajra" means the shape of a three-pronged vajra: one clasps with that mudrā of the three-pronged vajra. If one asks where one clasps with it, "having set the two mudrās at the sides" is stated. "The two mudrās" are the two mudrās of the three-pronged vajra, of the right and the left hand. "Having set them at the sides" means that skillful means and wisdom clasp each other at both flanks of the waist.

On "Intent on eating the nectar, one should press the lower one with the sharp ones": "the one intent" is the yogin. "The lower one" is the lip, because it has become the place of a nectar like honey. "Eating" is drinking and tasting, as already explained.

"From the paths of ten and eight, one should stir the nectar of the bell" means causing the fluid of the wisdom consort to descend from the eighteen places; it recalls what was taught earlier.

On "On the left side, the bandūka": "the left side" is the left side of the source of phenomena. "Bandūka" means that which is like the bandūka flower, namely the channel of Khāgamukha. If one asks what it is like, the passage beginning "having the appearance of dripping water" is stated. "Dripping water" is the dripping of the fluid; "appearance" means making it evident. On "dripping water, like the shape of a bird's beak": "bird's beak" is the face of birds; it is like that shape. On "There one should stir with the mudrā": "there" means in that channel where the seed of the hook was set. "The mudrā" is the finger on which the syllable of nectar was set. Alternatively, where passion is very firm, one stirs with the mudrā of the hand shaped like an elephant's trunk.

On "The waters on the right of the vajra": "water" means the water-born, that is, the lotus. "Right" means the right side, and the left is also to be included. This teaches that one should rub with the right side of the vajra, and rub the lotus with the left. For that very reason "One should act in the manner of equal nature" is stated: rubbing each other equally.

On "Then, in all the places beginning with the thongka, one should apply the fingers": "the thongka" here are the two breasts. "All the places" are the eighteen places beginning with these. "Applying the fingers" is rubbing with the fingers; the intention is that by rubbing with the fingers in the places beginning with the highest limb, the fluid will come to drip.

On "Then, having joined the mudrā of the vajra tip at the sides": "then" means for that reason, that is, in order to stir the awakening mind. "The vajra tip" and the rest have already been explained in the section on embracing.

To teach clearly the mutual rubbing, "Through the complete union of both, one should stir vajra and lotus" is stated. The meaning is that the right and left sides of the vajra and lotus of both skillful means and wisdom are joined to each other and stirred, that is, one should rub.

To recall the recitation, the passage beginning "of hūṃ" is stated. The meaning is that by reciting hūṃ, the seed of wisdom, one should satisfy the deities of one's own body with bliss. To teach the recitation of the bell, the passage beginning "the bell" is stated; it is easy to understand.

On "The seed of wisdom at the vajra tip: one should contemplate oneself as the deity": the seed of wisdom is the syllable hūṃ. "The vajra tip" means at the tip of the vajra, and "one should contemplate" is to be supplied. This teaches that at the time of union one meditates on hūṃ at the tip of the vajra and so satisfies one's own body. By this indication it should be understood that the syllable a is set in the lotus.

On "The vajra and bell are the body; one should join the mudrā in union": "the vajra" is Vajrasattva. The mudrā is the union of the bodies of those two.

To teach that through realizing this suchness one should also take up the outer vajra and bell, the passage beginning "vajra and bell" is stated. The meaning is that the yogin takes up the mudrā of the outer vajra and bell with this very purity as well.

"Moving the vajra through suchness" means that, having recited the suchness of hūṃ, one should rub with the vajra. "One should ring the bell at will" means that likewise the wisdom consort too, having uttered the syllable a, should rub the vajra with the lotus and suck it.

The passage beginning "Vajra and bell are fully joined; that is the joining of the mudrā" teaches that by delighting oneself with the ultimate wisdom of the equal union of vajra and bell, all the deities dwelling in the body become satisfied.

"The mind that is the very self of both" is the awakening mind arising from the union of both and so forth, the fluid born of both. On "One should bless that water": the water is the fluid, and blessing it is eating it; both eat the fluid of both.

Having earlier taught the karaṇa in which the wisdom consort is principal, now, to teach the karaṇa in which skillful means is principal, the passage beginning "amorous" is stated. "Of moving" refers to the karaṇa. "Amorous" means with a proud bearing, because he himself is principal. "Seated on a comfortable seat" is to be taken literally; others say that one meditates on oneself as Vajrasattva.

If one asks what one should do then, "The vajra has the playful foot; the bell plays with the left thigh" is stated: Vajrasattva remains in the playful stance with his left foot stretched out and his right slightly bent, and the wisdom consort remains in the stance with her right stretched out and her left bent. "The left hand embraces and holds the breast" means Vajrasattva embraces the wisdom consort with his left hand and holds her left breast. "With the right one should insert the vajra" means that with the right hand one places the vajra of skillful means into the lotus. "One should join again and again" means kissing moment after moment. The passage beginning "laughing" is easy to understand.

To teach the second, other karaṇa, the passage beginning "the bell" is stated. "In the manner of moving the bell" means that skillful means and wisdom reverse the order of the earlier playful stance. "Seated on the seat of the thigh" means the wisdom consort is placed upon the left thigh of skillful means. "Having wrapped with the right foot, one should place it at the place of the waist" means the right foot of skillful means is wrapped around the wisdom consort's waist. "Having embraced the neck with one" means the right hand of skillful means embraces the wisdom consort's neck. "With the left one should ring the bell" means that with the left hand one opens the wisdom consort's lotus; or else, stirs and rubs it. The rest is easy to understand. By these two karaṇas stated explicitly, the remaining ones should also be understood by implication.

On "Then one should sing the vajra song; it is held to be 'various hūṃ'": "then" means at the end of the karaṇa. In "the vajra song", the vajra is the nondual secret, and a song endowed with it is a vajra song, namely the one beginning "Saṃsāra is empty". "It is held to be 'various hūṃ'" means that at the end of each line of the song one should chant te na hūṃ and the like.

On "Having taken the water of nectar, one should sing the song again": "again" means once more, after that. "The water of nectar" is liquor. "Taking" is by drinking: in order to rest again and to please the wisdom consort, both drink liquor and sing the song.

The vajra song is taught, in the passage beginning "Saṃsāra is empty". "Saṃsāra" is the five aggregates. "Empty" means free of the apprehended and the apprehender. For that very reason it is without essence, like a plantain tree. To teach the essence of what is without essence, like the fruit from a plantain tree, "the essences are thoroughly accumulated" is stated. "The essence" is nonabiding nirvāṇa. "Thoroughly accumulated" means that by it all phenomena are accomplished: that is, through becoming accustomed to the five aggregates as having the nature of deities without intrinsic nature, nonabiding nirvāṇa is accomplished.

If one asks how, if phenomena are free of the apprehended and the rest, there could come to be nonabiding nirvāṇa, "Liberation from entities is fully accomplished" is stated. "Entity" is the characteristic of the apprehended and the rest; the meaning is that liberation from it is itself what is called nirvāṇa. Alternatively, this mind that appears dualistically, meditating in terms of existence and nonexistence, permanence and impermanence and the like, is the "entity", and it is liberation from that. "Fully accomplished" means that from that, nirvāṇa is accomplished; the meaning is that like produces like.

To teach synonymous names, the passage beginning "liberation" is stated. "Liberation" is a synonym of nonabiding nirvāṇa. "Fully delivered" means that by it all sentient beings are delivered, or are delivered into it. To show this, "Having made the union of embracing each other completely, by that one accomplishes the true aim of all" is stated. "The union of embracing each other completely" is the deity yoga in which emptiness and compassion are inseparable. "Of all" means of all sentient beings. "The true aim" is the purposes culminating in nirvāṇa.

"In saṃsāra, which has no essence, accomplish the essence in the midst of the yoginīs" means that, having trained in that union of complete embrace just described, from saṃsāra, which lacks essence, the essence of the yoginīs sunk in the midst of saṃsāra, namely nirvāṇa, will be accomplished; the intention is that this is because they are pure by nature.

"Then one should sing the song thus, with the sound of vajra and bell" means singing the song again and again together with the sound of the bell.

To show that a yogin who is thus endowed with meditation and recitation comes to accomplishment, the passage beginning "Meditative equipoise" is stated. To show that very accomplishment, the passage beginning "The rites" is stated; it is easy to understand.

Now, to show the application for bringing under control, the passage beginning "Medicine" is stated. "Agitating" means that by this one brings under one's own control; because it draws in, it is "agitating," namely the medicines and so on. "Application" is its thorough application. "Endowed" means together with the wisdom partner. The rest is easy to understand.

"It should be taken in the place of application" means all of them in equal parts. Some say it is to be done with the awakening mind. "Having consecrated those substances" means consecrating those substances with the awakening mind. The rest is easy to understand.

In the passage beginning "Indra's hand and," "Indra's hand" is the name given to the particular root that resembles the hand of Indra. "The deluding one" is paṭaparnaka. "The paralyzing one" is salaparṇika.

In "The five of oneself and the five limbs," "the five of oneself" are the five nectars. "The five limbs" are the impurities of the eye, the ear, the nose, the tongue and the vajra.

"Having made all equal in proportion" means that Indra's hand and the rest are in equal parts, while the others are taken in whatever amount there is. "Drink" means liquor. "Tooth-powder" means that, when chewing tooth-powder, it is mixed with conch-shell ash. The rest is easy to understand.

In "Through blood, by the application of the torma, the rites of control and so on are accomplished," "blood" is menstrual blood. "The application of the torma" is making the application of the deity red in color.

To show the recitation for that very application, the passage beginning "The mantra" is stated. If one asks how it is done, "What is requested to depart from one's own body" is stated: what is requested to depart from one's own body is the semen, and it is mixed with that. "How one should delight oneself" teaches this: those who wish to apply it to someone mix menstrual blood and semen, abide in the yoga of the deity red in color, and recite the mantra over it a hundred thousand times; whoever it is given to then comes under control.

Now, to show the divisions of the mudrās according to the divisions of the rites, such as pacifying, the passage beginning "Vow" is stated. "The woman of the vow" is a woman who keeps the skull observance. "Kinswoman" is a female arrow-maker, because of destroying. "King" is a kṣatriya woman. "Guru" is a brahmin woman. "Secret" is a female dancer. "Deer" is one who sells meat, that is, a butcher. "Fisher" is one who catches fish. The rest likewise is simply to be understood.

In the passage beginning "Therefore," "yoga" means the rites of pacifying and so on. "Treatise" means the mantra. "Taken" means the assembly of mudrās just described. "Effort" means practicing diligently.

Now, to show the particulars of binding the stances, the passage beginning "Furthermore" is stated. "The binding of the stances" is to be construed with "shall be explained." "It is the place for liberating sentient beings" means that which liberates sentient beings. Alternatively, "place" is the stance: the stance that liberates sentient beings. That very thing is stated:

The two, fully united, set in motion;
that itself, called the mudrā, is to be bound.

Here "the two" are skillful means and wisdom. "Fully united" is union in equipoise. That itself is "setting in motion," because it completely destroys the afflictions. "That itself" is the unerring, nondual primordial wisdom. That itself is the mudrā, because it delights the mind. "To be bound" means to be formed.

Some say this is the characteristic of the equal stance: "the two, fully united" means the two feet joined to each other. That itself is "setting in motion," because it drives away the obstructors. "Mudrā" is the mudrā of the equal stance, they say.

In the passage beginning "The equal stance and," "the equal stance" is when skillful means and wisdom, the two, have their feet touching each other, toe to toe. Some say it is the tip of the left foot touching the tips of the right toes. "The divided stance" is, from that same stance, the wisdom partner's left foot slightly bent. "The round stance" is, from the equal stance, the knees spread wide. "The stance of pressing the three" is, from that same equal stance, the wisdom partner's left raised upward. "The tortoise stance" is the right foot pressing down on the left. "Right extended" is the right extended and the left slightly bent. "Left extended" is the reverse of that. "One place" means standing on one: raising one foot, entering union with various karaṇas, and turning around like a wheel on a single foot. "Sitting" is the stance of play itself, because by it the bliss of wisdom and skillful means sits. "Throwing of the feet" means sitting however is comfortable. "The manner of the sacrificial post" is, in order to relieve fatigue, both skillful means and wisdom straightening the upper and lower legs and entering union. "Squeezing the knees" is squatting, planting the elbows on the knees and embracing at the neck. "The manner of gathering" is crossing the two thighs and entering between the wisdom partner's two legs. Others say that placing the wisdom partner's two knees in the two armpits of skillful means and embracing at the neck with both hands is the true manner of gathering. "Abiding together" is both partners sitting with their two legs extended not too far apart. "The fire of time" is the wisdom partner's right foot pressed against one's own heart. "The essence" is placing the wisdom partner's two feet between one's own two feet and pressing the heart with each of the two feet. "Abiding in joy" is resting with the two thighs of skillful means laid crosswise upon the wisdom partner's two upper thighs. "Gathering into one" is bending one leg and entering union.

"The eighteen stances of motion are to be applied at the time of worship" means entering union and sporting with those eighteen stances.

"The source from which all good qualities arise
should be worshipped by the yogin"
means that the yogin should take whichever wisdom partner possesses those qualities, endowed with all those capacities, and unite with her.

Now, to show the particular manners, "Pressing at the heart and" is stated. Embracing the wisdom partner from behind with both hands and pressing heart against heart: this play is "pressing the heart." Placing the wisdom partner's two feet upon one's own heart: this play is "joining the feet." Alternatively, it is playing with the wisdom partner's feet joined to one's own feet. Embracing all around, preceded by the manner of a flower garland: this is "the garland manner." Touching the breasts is "the breast manner." When one licks the wisdom partner's lotus and the wisdom partner in turn sucks the vajra, the two being joined, that is "eating." This too should be understood as joining while placed one above and one below. Embracing each other with one side of the body is "the half embrace." Touching each other's secret place with the two lower hands while embracing with the two upper hands is "touching the secret place in a half embrace." Touching body against body is "massaging the body"; alternatively, it is massaging each other's hands and feet. Raising the index finger and the middle finger in the koko gesture is "the koko praise."

"With a face laughing and coquettish" means the manner accompanied by laughter and accompanied by coquetry.

"Endowed with various characteristics,
by that the rites should be accomplished"
means that it is not only these ten manners. What then? It should be accomplished with various characteristics, such as applying the teeth and the nails.

Now, to show the conclusion that the vajra and bell must certainly be held, the passage beginning "Vajra" is spoken. "By suchness" means with reverence for the yoga as taught. "Individually" means definitely.

Now, to show that when skillful means and wisdom are separated from each other there is no accomplishment in any way at all, the passage beginning "Secret" is spoken. The secret vajra is skillful means; the bell is wisdom. Likewise, a vajra separated from the bell is one isolated from wisdom. "Accomplishment becomes distant" means being far from accomplishment, that is, separated from it.

Having thus shown the negative concomitance, to show the positive concomitance it is stated:

"If the mudrās of vajra and bell
are focused upon, one will liberate others."

The meaning is that if skillful means and wisdom come together, there will be accomplishment.

To give an example, the passage beginning "Night" is stated. "A night without a moon" is a moonless night, and it is to be construed with "what can it do?" This shows that because a moonless night is without light, it can do nothing at all for the aims of oneself and others. "What can the moon by day do?" means that likewise the daytime moon, separated from the night, is without its beautiful light, and so it cannot do even the slightest thing for the aims of oneself and others.

To apply this to the meaning, the passage beginning "Therefore" is stated. Just as the night and the lord of the night, occurring together at the same time, are able to serve the aims of oneself and others, so too the vajra and bell are equal; that is, through union they are able to illuminate suchness. Here "suchness" is the intrinsic nature, and that is the accomplishment of the Great Seal (mahāmudrā). "Illuminates" means endowed with the power to illuminate, that is, the sādhana. In some versions of the text it reads, "Through equality all is illuminated"; there too the word "all" indicates what is to be accomplished.

In the passage beginning "Āli kāli," āli kāli is the union of skillful means and wisdom. "Meditating in front" is meditating first. If one asks how, "by the great yogin, the practitioner" is stated. "Great yoga" is generating oneself into the deity's body, as taught before, through the sequence of seed syllable and sign, preceded by emptiness. "Practitioner" means the yogin, and it is connected with "should accomplish all purposes." The meaning of āli and kāli is given in the passage beginning "Āli"; it is easy to understand.
